## 📚 Paper List

- **[Better Calibrated, Better Planned: Uncertainty-Aware Multi-ASV Informative Path Planning for Oil Spill Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21783)**  `arXiv:2611.21783`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Alejandro Casado-Pérez, Marija Popović, Daniel Gutiérrez-Reina, Sergio L. Toral., Samuel Yanes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monitoring oil spills with fleets of Autonomous Surface Vehicles (ASVs) requires mapping a heterogeneous pollutant field from sparse, noisy measurements under tight time and energy budgets. Informative Path Planning (IPP) addresses this problem by coupling a reconstruction model, which infers the field and its uncertainty, with a planner that decides where to sample next. Prior work has focused on planning strategies and has largely relied on Gaussian Processes (GPs), leaving the calibration of the model's uncertainty underexplored. We study how uncertainty calibration conditions multi-vehicle IPP with a fleet of ASVs that coordinates online through a shared uncertainty map. We introduce Evidential Deep Learning (EDL) as an uncertainty-calibrated information model. We benchmark EDL, Monte Carlo Dropout and Deep Ensembles against a GP in closed loop with five planners on physics-based oil spill simulations. Across 60 maps, the choice of model dominates the outcome, explaining 80\% of the variance of the final reconstruction error, against 1\% for the planner among the deep models. EDL achieves the lowest error and the highest IoU under every planner, is the best calibrated model and runs at 3.9 ms per step. With 50 cells per vehicle it reconstructs the spill better than MC Dropout and Deep Ensembles with 150. Deep Ensembles become more confident as their error grows, whereas EDL is unaffected. Robustness of the information model to the data its own planner collects is therefore a requirement of uncertainty-aware IPP.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Diversity Windows: Diagnosing Noise Influence in Robotic Diffusion Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22743)**  `arXiv:2611.22743`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Tina Lakhani, Jing Wu, Yu-Kun Lai, Ze Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models are increasingly adopted in robotics and control due to their capability to express multiple feasible solutions to a given task. However, the role of stochasticity in the diffusion model-sampler across denoising remains poorly understood. We introduce a per-timestep counterfactual diagnostic that tests how changing the sampled noise affects the final output. Across five model-sampler configurations spanning three robotics benchmarks, diverse architectures, domains, and conditioning mechanisms, we reveal highly varied temporal sensitivity profiles, where noise-induced deviations can amplify, partially contract, or undergo near-total attenuation downstream. From these profiles, we define stochastic diversity windows (SDWs): compact intervals that identify where sampler stochasticity has the strongest measured influence on the final output. Guided by these windows, we apply two heuristic interventions: targeted stochastic amplification, which increases sample diversity exploration of the solution space, and an SDW-hybrid sampler, which replaces DDPM steps with DDIM skips in low-impact regions, resulting in network evaluation reductions of up to 40-60% and wall-clock time by up to 85%, with no consistent changes detected in feasibility, cost, or diversity across held-out tasks.
  </details>

- **[Heave-Ho: Decentralized Long-Horizon Multi-Humanoid Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22275)**  `arXiv:2611.22275`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ting-Hsuan Chen, Siheng Zhao, Han Jiang, Qi Pan, Louison Lu, Jiaheng Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaboration promises to extend humanoid capabilities beyond what individual robots can achieve. However, realizing this potential requires coordinated whole-body interactions, coherent long-horizon planning, and precise synchronization across robots. We introduce Heave-Ho, a decentralized framework for long-horizon collaborative multi-humanoid loco-manipulation. At the low level, we develop a multi-agent post-training framework that adapts a humanoid foundation tracking model to physically coupled interactions between humanoids, enabling coordinated decentralized control from local observations alone. At the high level, we train a vision-language planner and design a speech-based leader-follower coordination mechanism to synchronize decentralized execution without network communication. Trained with two identical humanoids, our system scales to teams of up to ten robots and generalizes to heterogeneous teams without retraining. To benchmark these capabilities, we introduce HH-Bench, comprising 12 tasks involving teams of 2 to 10 humanoids, including heterogeneous teams. Systematic evaluations show that Heave-Ho consistently outperforms baseline methods, while real-world experiments demonstrate robust decentralized collaboration across challenging tasks.
  </details>

- **[HubEgo-100K: In-the-Wild Egocentric Video of Skilled Work for Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21938)**  `arXiv:2611.21938`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Javad Amirian, Armin Kiani, Loïc Schaeffer, Tim Sprecher, Mohamed Taha, Karthik Yadati_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot policies trained by imitation generalize poorly outside the environments and objects seen in training. Published data-scaling studies find that generalization grows with the diversity of training environments and objects rather than with more demonstrations per environment. Robot data is costly to collect across many environments, whereas egocentric human video can be recorded in the wild at scale; existing egocentric video, however, mostly shows daily-life activity and contains little skilled work in real workplaces. We present HubEgo-100K, a dataset built for diversity. This version of the paper covers the first release, v1.0: 10,124 hours of 1080p egocentric video with synchronized 100 Hz inertial (IMU) data from one head-mounted phone camera. In v1.0, 12,227 contributors record their own unscripted work in 15,548 environments (each one contributor's own setting of one type, such as their kitchen or workshop) across 11 setting types, 30 work categories, 56 task types and 35 action families, with 83% of hours in workplace settings. The release comprises 72,754 episodes with machine-generated action and language annotations, in LeRobot and MCAP formats under CC BY 4.0. We measure diversity along each axis, show that inside v1.0, with episodes in random order, new contributors and words keep appearing, and relate the measurements to published evidence on six data properties tied to policy performance, including hand visibility and inertial sensing. We also document collection, curation, privacy measures and data format, and provide a datasheet. Later versions of this paper will add benchmarks and cover further releases toward 100,000 hours.
  </details>

- **[FIRE-Drive: Feasibility-Informed Risk Evaluation for End-to-End Diffusion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22297)**  `arXiv:2611.22297`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Qifeng Xie, Kai-Yuan Feng, Wenkang Wan, Yue Wu, Maoguo Gong, Kai Sheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based end-to-end autonomous driving models have shown strong potential in generating multimodal planning trajectories. However, their performance still depends heavily on trajectory initialization, mode evaluation, and supervision quality. Weakly constrained noisy initialization may introduce infeasible proposals and increase the burden of denoising. Moreover, selecting the optimal mode remains challenging when candidate trajectories are geometrically similar but differ in safety, interaction risk, and progress. To address these issues, we propose FIRE-Drive, a feasibility-informed risk evaluation framework for end-to-end autonomous driving. FIRE-Drive improves diffusion-based planning through dynamic feasible anchor initialization, candidate-specific bird's-eye-view risk evidence, and compact high-quality candidate supervision with lightweight refinement. This design aligns the initial support, risk-aware scoring, and multimodal supervision of trajectory modes under a unified planning objective. It enables the diffusion decoder to focus on refining plausible trajectories while the scorer distinguishes modes using local risk evidence. Experiments on NAVSIM v1 show that FIRE-Drive achieves a Predictive Driver Model Score of 91.5 with a ResNet34 backbone. Additional closed-loop evaluation on 220 Bench2Drive routes yields a Driving Score of 71.43 and a Success Rate of 42.73%. Component ablations and a scorer comparison under matched candidate inputs support the contributions of feasible initialization, auxiliary supervision, and risk-informed selection. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RoboTeam: Benchmarking Vision-Based Multi-Agent Control of Many Robot Arms in Real Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22138)**  `arXiv:2611.22138`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Changxuan Fan, Zhongwei Xie, Boyi Zhang, Shaoli Hu, Chang Xu, Xiyu Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents can now control robot arms by calling tools and writing code. Existing benchmarks fall short in three ways: most test one or two arms on short tasks, many pause the world while the agent thinks, and many give it ground-truth object positions. We present RoboTeam, a simulated benchmark where agents control 4 to 16 robot arms in real time from camera images alone. Its 36 tasks are long jobs that the arms must do together, such as passing timbers or building a picture from hundreds of cubes. We start from the most flexible agent system, the adaptive system, where one agent decides when to fork delegates. With it, gpt-6.1-sol and opus-5.5 complete 28 and 27 tasks, and four other models at most 3. But at a high price: a typical task takes gpt-6.1-sol 3.5 hours and 218 million input tokens, and a third of its steps fail at least once and must be retried. We then compare it with three fixed systems: single-agent, hierarchical, and decentralized. All four complete a similar number of tasks but differ in time and tokens. The hierarchical and decentralized systems are faster but use up to 1.9 times the tokens, and the single agent uses fewer tokens but takes 1.6 times as long, so the adaptive system lies between them. Even so, no model or system is yet fast, cheap, and reliable at once. RoboTeam measures these limits where earlier benchmarks cannot: long multi-arm jobs, camera images only, and a world that never pauses.
  </details>

- **[Let the Mind Conceive, Let the Hand Achieve: Coupling Grounded Action and Progress Evaluation for Long-Horizon Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18352)**  `arXiv:2610.18352`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Youpeng Wen, Gang Wang, Tianyu Huang, Weiwei Zhang, Zhijian Huang, Wei Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot following a multi-step instruction needs to act on the right objects in the right order, even as the scene changes. It must also maintain awareness of task progress and determine when to advance. To address these challenges, we introduce Orchestra, an agent harness that separates task planning and execution management from action generation. Orchestra decomposes a complete multimodal instruction into ordered subtasks, tracks completed steps, and updates the locations of relevant objects and destinations during execution. It maintains a shared context containing the active subtask, spatial grounding, and recent observations. An action policy uses this context to generate actions, while a separately trained progress evaluator estimates when to advance to the next subtask. To evaluate whether robots trained on atomic instructions and short trajectories can execute longer subtask sequences, we introduce a benchmark named RMBench-Compose. Orchestra achieves a mean success rate of $68.22\%$ across nine RMBench tasks, outperforming Mem-0 by $26.22$ percentage points. On RMBench-Compose, it achieves an Average Success Length (ASL) of $7.35$ subtasks, $2.46$ times that of HarnessVLA. Across four real-world multi-step tasks, it improves mean ASL by $25.0\%$ over Orchestra using the base $\pi_{0.5}$ policy. These results show that Orchestra helps robots carry out longer sequences of subtasks while preserving the requested subtask order and acting on the specified objects.Our code and project page are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[PAIR: Bringing Household Robots into the Smart Home](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23070)**  `arXiv:2611.23070`  `cs.RO`  
  _Junseong Pyo, Hyeongjin Kim, Jongwook Han, Seungwon Kook, Sungjib Lim, Yohan Jo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Household robots often need to operate appliances, and smart appliances offer both control panels and device APIs. Existing embodied AI simulation environments, however, model appliances solely as physical objects. We introduce PAIR, a smart-home simulation environment grounded in the Matter protocol~\footnote{this https URL}, the industry standard for smart-home devices. PAIR provides 84 smart-device assets whose functional control panels and APIs act on a shared device state, with ambient conditions that evolve in response to both. From a survey of 1,443 requests, we construct a benchmark of 40 tasks that all require object manipulation yet remain solvable without API access, with 340 human demonstrations for policy training. Whether run alone, with a high-level planner, or with an API-enabled planner, two fine-tuned VLA policies complete none of the 120 evaluation episodes. Robots rarely get far enough to use the API: even with oracle plans and API access, over 90\% of failed rollouts stall at navigating, grasping, or operating a door. With a perfect oracle in place of the robot's body, the best planner still gets through only 57.3\% of its plan. When choosing between pressing a button and calling an API, several planners go by whether they can read the panel, not by API latency.\footnote{We will release our code and data upon publication.}
  </details>

- **[Arboreus: A Realistic Boreal Forest Simulator with Topographic Terrain and Satellite-Informed Parametric Tree Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22908)**  `arXiv:2611.22908`  `cs.RO`  
  _F. Labra Caso, A. Saradagi, A. Koval, G. Nikolakopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work presents Arboreus, a realistic Boreal forest simulator, developed to support statistical validation of robotic autonomy algorithms in Boreal forest environments. Arboreus presents an automated methodology for synthesizing large-scale, data-informed, plausible forest worlds from parametric trees, height-variant terrain, and satellite-informed tree density distributions. The parametric generation of dominant Boreal tree species such as Birch, Spruce, and Pine is guided by trunk, canopy, and foliage parameters, extracted from growth studies in the Nordic areas. Tree distribution in an area of interest is guided by the Copernicus satellite dataset, which provides tree cover density and dominant species type at up to 10m raster resolution. The forest floor generation is guided by topographic data from the Swedish cadastral agency to produce realistic data-informed terrains. Given coordinates and extents of forested areas of interest, Arboreus synthesizes data-informed plausible forests, while offering flexibility to inject user observations of real forests (detail, age, dimensionality and tree count), which is critical for realistic synthetic data generation and for the testing of feature-sensitive autonomy algorithms. The applicability of Arboreus for benchmarking and validation of robot autonomy for different robot mobilities is demonstrated in Gazebosim, Unreal Engine, and Isaac Sim physics engines. The applicability of Arboreus as a source of synthetic forest data and its potential to support downstream forest resource mapping research (volume estimation, tree instance segmentation and stem attribute estimation) is also demonstrated. Arboreus has been open-sourced along with a dataset comprising 25 Boreal forest worlds of size 1000m2 corresponding to three real forest sites.
  </details>

- **[ReLAP: Latent Action Pretraining through Recurrent Video Prediction in JEPA Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22077)**  `arXiv:2611.22077`  `cs.RO`  
  _R. Gnana Praveen, Ali Rouzbayani, Zhiying Jiang, Bidhan Roy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Videos provide a rich source of physical interaction for learning robot behavior without action annotations, yet effective latent actions must capture not only local transitions but also how those transitions evolve over time. We introduce ReLAP, a latent-action pretraining framework that couples local transition learning with recurrent multi-step prediction in a frozen JEPA representation space. A shared inverse dynamics model infers compact continuous latent actions from adjacent observations, while a latent world model recurrently predicts the future JEPA features. Because each predicted feature becomes the context for the next transition, errors at later steps backpropagate through the rollout to earlier latent actions, encouraging temporally coherent dynamics beyond pairwise reconstruction. We transfer this representation to a vision-language model using two complementary objectives: latent distillation from the frozen inverse teacher and recurrent future-feature prediction through the frozen latent world model. The resulting policy is then adapted to robot demonstrations with action flow matching. At deployment, the explicit latent-action and world-model branches are removed, and the fine-tuned VLM predicts physical actions directly, reducing inference complexity. With only 11M pretraining samples and 1.06B trainable parameters, ReLAP achieves 97.9% on standard LIBERO and 70.0% over zero-shot LIBERO-Plus perturbations. On the LARY latent-action benchmark, ReLAP ranks second in both mean regression error and mean classification accuracy among the compared methods. These results demonstrate that recurrent action-free latent action pretraining can learn compact, temporally structured, and transferable representations for downstream robot control.
  </details>

- **[Agile Monoped Hopping on Complex and Discrete Terrain via Kinodynamic Planning with Distilled MPPI Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21905)**  `arXiv:2611.21905`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yue Qin, Denglin Cheng, Jingyu Liu, Yanran Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents FlowRRT, a kinodynamic planner for agile monoped hopping on discrete terrain. Steering for kinodynamic RRT on complex dynamical systems currently relies on motion primitives or closed-loop policies, limiting the diversity of motions or the planning speed. Distilling a trajectory optimizer avoids both, but existing methods regress a single solution per query, which fails on monoped hopping, where the feasible action set is narrow and disconnected. Our key insight is to treat steering as set-valued and predict the distribution of feasible actions instead. A multi-start MPPI expert collects the entire feasible set of each query offline, and conditional flow matching distills it into a generative sampler. With this sampler, FlowRRT coordinates successive jumps over long horizons and finds non-greedy maneuvers that are hard for reactive policies to discover. Benchmarks across different terrains show higher success rates and substantially shorter planning times than sampling-based baselines. On terrains with inclined platforms, FlowRRT reaches $80\%$ success within $1$~s, versus $24$~s for RL-RRT and $77$~s for SST. Sim-to-sim tracking in MuJoCo confirms accurate execution under full-order dynamics.
  </details>

- **[ASTRA: ASV Simulator for Transferable Realistic Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21789)**  `arXiv:2611.21789`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tsimafei Lazouski, Cody Sheltraw, Alan Papalia, Maani Ghaffari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomy development for marine autonomous surface vehicles (ASVs) remains challenging because field testing is costly and difficult to reproduce. Existing marine simulation tools provide mature capabilities in vessel dynamics, robotics integration, and visual rendering, but these strengths are emphasized differently across platforms. This paper presents ASTRA, an ASV simulation framework built in NVIDIA Isaac Sim that combines marine vehicle dynamics, photorealistic perception, and Robot Operating System (ROS 2) autonomy interfaces within a common environment. ASTRA incorporates a Fossen-style marine dynamics model together with geometry-based buoyancy and programmable spectral wave disturbances, enabling non-planar hydrostatic and wave-induced motion. The simulator reproduces the sensor configuration of a physical BlueBoat testbed, including GPS, IMU, LiDAR, and RGB-D sensing exposed through ROS 2. This allows localization, perception, planning, and control software to be developed against interfaces consistent with the physical platform. The BlueBoat's existing ROS 2 localization and perception modules were ported directly into ASTRA, while targeted zero-propulsion experiments were used to verify the implemented wave-body coupling. The simulated roll response was consistent with the expected non-oscillatory hydrostatic behavior, and heave remained highly coherent with the generated wave field, with approximately unity gain at the dominant wave frequency in the tested sea states. ASTRA is intended as an open-source testbed for marine autonomy development, simulation-based benchmarking, and sim-to-real experimentation.
  </details>

- **[An Open-Source da Vinci X/Xi Instrument Module for Laparoscopic Surgery Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21771)**  `arXiv:2611.21771`  `cs.RO`  
  _Douglas McDonough, Justin Liao, Jiawei Ge, Antony Goldenberg, Yvonne Zhang, Mackenzie A Eagleson, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Studying electrosurgical tasks on a research robot requires control of both instrument motion and energy activation, along with a record of when each action occurs. This paper presents an open-source module for da Vinci X/Xi instruments on a UR5/UR5e robotic arm. Five motors transmit torque through timing belts to spring-loaded drive discs. An electrosurgical interposer accepts software commands, gives the surgeon's foot pedals priority, and records the source of each activation request. In bench testing, the interposer recorded all 12 activation and deactivation events from six pedal presses. Ex vivo demonstrations included monopolar and bipolar coagulation of splenic tissue and monopolar cutting during gallbladder dissection. Optical tracking of the Fenestrated Bipolar Forceps showed that calibration brought pitch and yaw gains from 0.904 and 1.14 to 1.00 and 1.02, respectively. The module enables researchers to record instrument motion, video, and the timing and source of electrosurgical commands during laparoscopic tasks.
  </details>

- **[Video-Action Models for Data-Efficient Industrial Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15974)**  `arXiv:2611.15974`  `cs.RO`  
  _Eugenio Chisari, Stefanos Charalambous, Oliver Sanchez, Thomas Umbach, Michael Gentner, Fabio Quattrini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial manipulation requires precise alignment and controlled handling of rigid, deformable, and flexible components. Yet, the benefits of pretrained visuomotor policies remain underexplored in settings where limited access to specialized hardware, components, and fixtures constrains demonstration collection and evaluation. Understanding task-specific data requirements is important for assessing their scalability across diverse processes on the factory floor. We investigate whether video pretraining can support reliable industrial manipulation with limited action data. We propose FLUX-mimic, a video-action model, motivated by the potential of video pretraining to capture motion and interaction patterns that enable sample-efficient training. FLUX-mimic is built on a backbone pretrained on large-scale web and industrial task data, and an action expert to generate robot trajectories. We train our policy by joint video-action training. Across four automotive manufacturing benchmark tasks and two bimanual robot platforms, FLUX-mimic outperforms all considered baselines on the dexterous robot platform and matches pi_{0.5}'s average success rate on the gripper-based robot platform. On a deformable part kitting task, FLUX-mimic reaches 77.5% success with as little as two hours of task-specific action-labeled demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[Future Prediction as Training-Only Video Supervision for Pretrained VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34593)**  `arXiv:2610.34593`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hanwen Cui, Yuanyuan Li, Qing Guo, Zhida Zhang, Chao Chen, Xi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) introduce predictive supervision into robot policy learning by jointly modeling future visual observations and actions. Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policies acquire rich visual and semantic representations as well as action-generation capabilities. Their action objectives typically lack direct supervision for predicting future visual observations. Future videos in robot demonstrations record object motion and task progress. They can provide temporal visual supervision that complements action labels.We propose Training-Only Video Supervision, which introduces future-video prediction as an auxiliary objective for VLA fine-tuning. We condition a randomly initialized video-prediction expert on the VLA's shared vision-language features. The expert learns to predict future video latents from robot demonstrations. The video prediction loss and the original action loss jointly update the shared vision-language backbone, providing additional temporal visual supervision. After training, we remove the video expert and its adapter. The fine-tuned VLA retains its original action-generation pathway at inference. On the 50-task RoboTwin 2.0 benchmark, our method achieves 71.18% average success, compared with $\pi_{0.5}$ of 58.33%. In a matched evaluation on four real-world manipulation tasks, success increases from 42.50% to 61.25%. These results show that future visual modeling can serve as auxiliary training supervision for pretrained VLAs. The fine-tuned action policy benefits from this supervision without retaining a video-prediction branch at deployment.
  </details>

- **[Stereo-Planner: Learning Stereo-to-Trajectory Planning for Zero-Shot Sim-to-Real Quadrotor Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15560)**  `arXiv:2610.15560`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hongming Shen, Junjie Lu, Xun Chen, Wei Wang, Xinhang Xu, Bailing Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing navigation systems commonly rely on dedicated depth sensors or explicit depth reconstruction, while learning to generate trajectories directly from RGB images remains challenging due to the sim-to-real domain gap. In this work, we propose Stereo-Planner, an end-to-end vision-to-trajectory framework that enables zero-shot sim-to-real planning from stereo RGB observations. The key idea is to formulate trajectory generation as state- and scale-conditioned querying of latent stereo geometry. A Scene Representor incorporates a pretrained vision grounding foundation model to extract geometric cues shared across simulated and real-world environments, supporting generalization across visual domains. A lightweight Trajectory Decoder then translates these representations into trajectories through interleaved attention, separating trajectory-state interaction from scene-feature retrieval and incorporating the stereo baseline as a metric-scale cue. With these designs, Stereo-Planner is trained entirely on simulated data by minimizing differentiable trajectory costs and deployed directly in real-world environments without fine-tuning. Benchmark results demonstrate strong cross-scene generalization, with a 95% success rate in simulation environments at a commanded speed of 8 m/s, and competitive performance against depth-based planners using only stereo RGB observations for scene perception. Furthermore, deployment on a compact quadrotor equipped with an NVIDIA Orin NX enables autonomous flight at speeds of approaching 8 m/s, with a mean onboard vision-to-action inference time of 14.44 ms. The software, simulation environments, and hardware designs will be open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AVL-JEPA: Preventing Causal Dynamics Information Collapse In Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03587)**  `arXiv:2610.03587`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yikang Qiao, Ling Zhang, Ziying Song, Duan Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Joint embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) predict future latent representations without reconstructing observations, enabling world models to focus on high-level semantic dynamics. However, a JEPA can preserve high dimensional visual information while discarding information about the physical consequences of actions. We call this failure mode causal dynamics information collapse and propose action-grounded vision-invariance latent (AVL) to prevent this collapse. We first use the executed action as an auxiliary dynamics anchor that encourages the model to preserve dynamics information, and then use a vision-invariance pathway which aligns perturbed and clean latent predictions without discarding dynamics information, forcing the model to fully understand and utilize causal dynamics information. We validate AVL on four robotic control tasks (TwoRoom, PushT, OGBench Cube, and Reacher), showing that it substantially improves success rates under visual perturbations while preserving clean-environment performance. We further evaluate physical consequence alignment, clean-noisy dynamics consistency, and the causal effect of targeted transition subspace erasure. Collectively, these results indicate that dynamic information causally relevant to planning is preserved from collapse under AVL.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Centric Observation Adaptation for Robust Visual Control under Dynamic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24661)**  `arXiv:2604.24661`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhengru Fang, Yu Guo, Huanhuan Lou, Fei Liu, Yuang Zhang, Yihang Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual control maps visual streams to actions in reinforcement learning and vision-language-action (VLA) policies. Changing weather, noise, blur, and compression can obscure different task cues within one episode. Existing control benchmarks mainly test scene distractions, while generic restorers optimize whole-image fidelity. We introduce the Visual Degraded Control Suite (VDCS) to evaluate seven degradations under Markov switching. We propose Agent-Centric Observations with Mixture-of-Experts (ACO-MoE), which routes each frame to a repair expert, restores RGB content, and composes task-relevant foregrounds. On eight VDCS tasks, corruption lowers DreamerV3's mean return from 769 to 562. ACO-MoE raises it to 730, recovering 81.2% of the lost return. It also improves returns on DMC-GB and RoboSuite and retains 70% to 97% of clean-input mean return under six unseen perturbation families. We train ACO-MoE on robot demonstrations and deploy it with a 3B-parameter VLA policy on a Piper arm for cube placement in blue and white boxes and cup placement on a coaster. On the blue-box and cup tasks, motion blur and physical low light with added noise reduce success from 100% to at most 10%. ACO-MoE raises success to 75% to 95% over 20 trials per task and condition.
  </details>

- **[Addressing Exogenous Variability in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22067)**  `arXiv:2611.22067`  `cs.MA` `cs.LG`  
  _Seongmin Kim, Woohyeon Byeon, Jiwon Jeon, Seungyul Han, Youngchul Sung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) often fails under exogenous variability such as shifts in opponents or environment regimes that cannot be controlled by the team but alter the transition dynamics. We formalize this challenge as Exogenous Dec-POMDP (ED-POMDP), in which an episode-level exogenous regime, selected independently of the team, indexes the dynamics, and the global state is partitioned into components that are directly responsive to the current team action and components that are not directly action-responsive at the current step. This formulation exposes mediated influence: while team actions do not directly update the latter at the current step, they can shape their later evolution through induced changes in the former. Based on this, we propose LEICA, a CTDE-compatible algorithm that learns history-conditioned context representations for both components and shapes policy updates using agent-wise, influence-weighted counterfactual intrinsic rewards. Across SMAX benchmarks with opponent strategy shifts, LEICA consistently improves both training performance and generalization to unseen opponents' strategies over existing baselines, supporting the usefulness of influence-weighted shaping under exogenous train-test regime shifts.
  </details>

- **[VTT: Reading Vehicle Size and Powertrain Type from Motion Trajectory Across Unseen Car Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22109)**  `arXiv:2611.22109`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Yuhang Wang, Yuechen Guo, Jingxin Yang, Chuheng Wei, Suyang Chen, Abdulaziz Alhuraish, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vehicle size and powertrain information can enrich trajectory data for traffic analysis and simulation. We present VTT, a dataset and benchmark for identifying these attributes from motion and controller area network (CAN) signals across unseen car models. The dataset contains 9,062 drives, about 3,950 h, from 1,431 vehicles and 174 car models. It provides three footprint-based size classes, powertrain labels, and evaluation on unseen car models and unseen drivers. Using 60-s windows, we compare four sequence architectures, statistical features and physical features proposed by large language models. Training with auxiliary physical targets, data augmentation and task-specific weighting raises drive-level macro-F1 to 92.6-93.0% for battery-electric versus combustion vehicles and from 62.9% to 67.1% for size classification. On public logs from 4,061 additional drivers, the full-input model identifies powertrain correctly for 86 of 88 car models; size accuracy is 63.2%, close to the internal vehicle-level result of 63.5%. Physical-feature analysis links powertrain recognition to deceleration behaviour and size recognition to steering response. Errors near size-class boundaries and limited gains from longer observations highlight the importance of label resolution and training-fleet coverage for cross-model generalisation.
  </details>

- **[VRLLM: Turning LLMs into Powerful Tabular Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22308)**  `arXiv:2611.22308`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yingtong Dou, Shubham Jain, Junpeng Wang, Hadi Abdullah, Vineeth Rakesh Mohan, Yuzhong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on text, but whether pretrained LLMs are effective for large-scale tabular classification remains unclear, as many previous works demonstrate that LLMs' tabular data classification performance falls behind tree-based models or neural architectures designed for tabular data. At Visa Research, we managed to fine-tune a pretrained Llama 3.2 1B LLM on billions of rows of labeled tabular data and make it outperform a wide array of tabular classifiers and even our competitive production deep learning model. For ease of reference, we refer to the model as the Visa Research LLM (VRLLM). In this paper, we share how we achieved this exciting result, covering data preparation, training recipe, ablation studies of design choices and deployment validation. We also benchmark frontier LLMs on the same task and analyze their prediction behavior across model families and generations. Our interpretability experiments suggest that VRLLM learns feature-specific predictive associations, while component ablations indicate that the earliest two FFN layers contribute to the emergence of linearly decodable task information. Our paper provides an engineering recipe and a focused empirical case study on adapting compact LLMs to industrial tabular prediction tasks.
  </details>

- **[PromptPack: Training-Free Augmentation of Diffusion Editors for Intent-Aligned Spatial and Semantic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21865)**  `arXiv:2611.21865`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Sophia Zalewski, Mustafa Munir, Enting Liu, Anjul Patney, David Dynerman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many image-editing tasks require confining changes to intended spatial regions while exactly preserving user-designated source content. Instruction-driven editing pipelines often leave such user-intent underspecified, while inpainting editors condition generation on explicitly defined edit support but not on the spatial assignment of individual edit concepts. PromptPack addresses both limitations by equipping diffusion editors with two explicit 'Edit Guides': an edit-role partition that labels each pixel as 'Keep,' 'Permit,' or 'Modify' to specify where edits may occur, and a regional-prompt assignment that maps spatial regions to text prompts to specify which concepts apply where. These guides may be manually specified or, for source-object edits, automatically derived from a text-grounded SAM 3 mask. Without retraining, PromptPack injects independently embedded regional prompts into the editor's fixed-length text sequence and uses the Edit Guides to spatially mask attention, gate flow updates, and restore protected source pixels. We integrate PromptPack into FlowEdit FLUX, FlowEdit SD3, and FLUX Kontext and demonstrate improved requested-concept placement and protected-content preservation across all three integrations on PIE-Bench++. In a pairwise user study, PromptPack achieves an 87% preference rate over FlowEdit FLUX, establishing PromptPack as a practical, training-free interface for intent-aligned diffusion editing.
  </details>

- **[Weight-Space Programming Doesn't Need Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22055)**  `arXiv:2611.22055`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hyunjong Ok, Jaeho Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard neural architectures such as the Transformer were chosen because they train well with gradient descent and run fast on GPUs. In weight-space programming, a large language model (LLM) writes target weights by hand without gradient-based training, so those reasons no longer apply. Should we keep the Transformer? Given minimal guidance, ten labeled examples, and a sandbox, mainly on multiple-choice questions, we task three frontier LLMs with either generating the weights of a fixed architecture or designing the architecture themselves. When free to design, all three compile plain-text facts into a sparse table queried by a fixed procedure, reaching up to 54.4% test accuracy. An LLM-designed model achieves higher test accuracy than the most accurate released Transformer that the same LLM wrote while using about 1,800x fewer parameters. Without gradients, they choose a table, in which the LLM can state what each weight means and can find and rewrite a wrong one. Each wrong answer points to one row, and removing a row changes about one other answer. From this, we propose an associative-memory-based model up to 17x faster on GPUs than a same-sized Transformer. We also test pretrained weights, other benchmarks, and tasks where models generate answers. Pretrained LLMs remain more accurate; the claim concerns which structure to build when writing weights.
  </details>

- **[The Hidden Cost of MoE Diffusion Decoding: Profiling and Fixing the Sampler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21852)**  `arXiv:2611.21852`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Harshit Samrat, Ramakrishna Paramahamsa Kompella_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) decode by iteratively denoising a full canvas of tokens, so every forward pass runs a per-token sampling and stopping pipeline over the entire canvas-by-vocabulary logits tensor. We profile single-user decoding of a 26B-parameter diffusion Mixture-of-Experts model on a bandwidth-bound edge accelerator (NVIDIA GB10) and report a result that is easy to miss: after weight quantisation, this logits pipeline, not the expert GEMMs, is the single largest consumer of decode time. Unlike autoregressive decoding, where sampling touches one position's logits per committed token, diffusion decoding processes S times as many logit entries per output token, where S is the number of denoising steps. We show this pipeline is largely redundant or fusable and remove it in two lossless tiers: a bit-exact tier whose output is byte-identical to the reference decoder, and an accuracy-parity tier using a fused single-pass entropy kernel and Gumbel-max sampling. Combined with a W4A16 expert kernel, the full stack reaches 2.4-2.7 times end-to-end speedup over bf16 at unchanged benchmark accuracy on GSM8K, HumanEval, and MATH, on a single 273 GB/s device. We confirm the finding on a second, independently built diffusion MoE (LLaDA2.0-mini: pipeline 28.8% of decode, fused W4A16 3.3 times at accuracy parity) and show it disappears on a dense diffusion model (3.6%). The cost is specific to MoE diffusion, where quantisation makes the backbone cheap. Code and quantised weights are released.
  </details>

- **[Localized LoRA-MoE: Mixture of Low-Rank Experts With Adaptive Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05114)**  `arXiv:2607.05114`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Babak Barazandeh, Subhabrata Majumdar, Vinay Prithyani, George Michailidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA adapt large models through a single low-rank update. Such updates are susceptible to gradient interference: when a training stream interleaves multiple operational contexts, conflicting updates to shared adapter weights collapse the adapter into an unspecialized average. While recent methods introduce block-wise isolation to remedy this issue, their topologies are static and cannot adapt to context switching or localized sensor degradation. In this work, we introduce Localized LoRA-MoE, a framework that combines localized spatial blocking with context-conditioned expert routing. We propose and evaluate two architectural variants: Block-Wise LoRA-MoE (centralized routing), which modulates the entire structural grid through a single context-conditioned gate, and Cell-Wise LoRA-MoE (decentralized routing), which assigns an independent gate to every coordinate cell of the matrix grid. Across three structured transformation benchmarks--a high-dimensional SVD matrix simulation, context-switched transformations over real tabular data, and spatial reconstruction under simulated sensor occlusion--both variants outperform static baselines under comparable adapter budgets. Both variants perform similarly when context shifts the grid uniformly, while cell-wise routing is strictly better when contexts are spatially heterogeneous. When fine-tuning a language model on six interleaved GLUE tasks, task-conditioned routing at a fixed adapter budget outperforms LoRA and static localized adapters, matching per-task adapters with 6x budgets. At decoder scale on Qwen2.5-1.5B, both routed variants outperform static baselines across eight commonsense benchmarks, with cell-wise routing achieving +3.9 points over LoRA at matched active capacity.
  </details>

- **[Do LLM Agents Still Need to Be Told How to Self-Improve?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23219)**  `arXiv:2611.23219`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shobhita Sundaram, Cansu Sancaktar, Phillip Isola_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly drive their own learning. Self-improvement methods delegate various components of model optimization to the models themselves. As models become more capable, this motivates a basic question: do these increasingly capable models still need us to specify how they should self-improve? In this paper we study an autonomous paradigm in which, given basic primitives, resources, and a general goal for self-improvement, agents construct their own self-learning algorithm for context-based optimization. Using the setting of gameplay as a testbed, a frozen LLM agent is given a compute budget and tools for collecting experience and updating text documents, and allowed to freely experiment without an externally imposed loop or access to real-world data. We find that sufficiently capable models do self-improve under these conditions, demonstrating strategies that align with classic cognitive frameworks for self-regulated learning and constructs from machine learning literature on self-generated curricula. Furthermore, we study the extent to which the learned text artifacts transfer to external benchmarks, observing improvement across different game horizons, mechanics, and 3D navigation.
  </details>

- **[Projected Penalty-Gradient Tracking for Communication-Efficient Byzantine-Resilient Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22929)**  `arXiv:2611.22929`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanjie Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate communication-efficient federated learning over heterogeneous datasets in the presence of Byzantine adversaries capable of uploading arbitrary information. Penalty-based methods bound the influence of each worker through the bounded gradient of a penalty function; however, the bound is lost once the uploads are compressed, since a Byzantine worker can accumulate an unbounded state at the server. To retain the Byzantine resilience under compressed uploads, we propose a projected penalty-gradient tracking framework, where each honest worker uploads a compressed residual of its penalty gradient and the server projects each reconstructed state onto the known penalty-gradient ball. The framework accommodates any contractive compressor and yields two algorithms: fault-resilient proximal gradient with consistent sparsification (FRPG-CS), whose shared mask is protected by freshly drawn reserve rows, and lazily aggregated FRPG (LAG-FRPG), whose uploads are event-triggered. For smooth and strongly convex losses, we establish a finite-time bound on the expected objective gap, where the Byzantine error floor is independent of the compressor and the compression error decays faster than the optimization error. We also bound the expected number of lazy uploads and derive certificates that separate the finite-penalty model bias from the optimization error. Experiments on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST show that the proposed algorithms attain the highest accuracy among twelve methods under the Min-Max attack, whereas a compressed-momentum benchmark and a local-update benchmark remain preferable in attack-free accuracy and total traffic, respectively.
  </details>

- **[WATERFALL: Workflow for Adaptive Training with Evolutionary Reward Formulation and Automated Learning Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22854)**  `arXiv:2611.22854`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Eleftherios Triantafyllidis, Filippos Christianos, Zhibin Li, Bernd Bickel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward design remains a central bottleneck in reinforcement learning (RL), particularly in sparse-reward, long-horizon and partially observable settings requiring dependency chaining, memory, and deceptive affordance disambiguation. While recent foundation-model approaches reduce manual reward engineering, most assume goal-conditioning, task descriptions, expert demonstrations, or ground-truth metrics. These assumptions are problematic in partially observable environments, where task-relevant objectives, affordances, and dynamics must be discovered through interaction rather than disclosed a priori. We formalise this problem setting by introducing WATERFALL, an iterative population-based workflow for automated reward discovery under the strict observability constraints inherent in POMDP environments. WATERFALL achieves this by (i) synthesising diverse programmatic reward candidates via persona-conditioned generation, (ii) evaluating candidate behaviour from raw visual rollouts utilising a Swiss-system evolutionary tournament judged by vision-language models to identify elites, and (iii) leveraging longitudinal assessment histories to iteratively mutate, escalate, or prune candidates. We evaluate WATERFALL across fully observable and reveal-gated partially observable environments ranging from discrete to continuous, comparing against sparse-reward RL, intrinsic-motivation and foundation-model reward-synthesis baselines. Our results indicate that WATERFALL discovers and refines reward programs without access to privileged information, outperforming baselines that retain their native information advantages, most clearly in the POMDP regime. Extensive ablations substantiate that visual evidence, tournament-based selection, and iterative refinement are jointly important. Enforcing this contract is what separates autonomous reward discovery from benchmark-privileged reward shaping.
  </details>

- **[PANO: Parallel Latent Space Neural Operator for Solving PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22782)**  `arXiv:2611.22782`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zijiang Yang, Xiaomeng Wu, Chen Kuang, Dongmei Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aggregating observations into a limited number of latent tokens and learning physical interactions in the latent space has emerged as an effective approach for Transformer-based neural operators to solve Partial Differential Equation (PDE) problems involving large-scale observations. However, existing methods typically compress observations using a single aggregation pattern, making physical information not retained by that pattern invisible to the latent space and thereby constraining physical interactions within it. To address this limitation, we propose PArallel Latent Space Neural Operator (PANO), a novel neural operator for solving PDEs that formulates physical interactions among observations as an additive combination of interaction results from parallel latent spaces with shared parameters. Specifically, PANO transforms observations into multiple distinct compressed representations through independent mappings, learns physical interactions in parallel based on these representations, and finally fuses the decoded results to predict the target physical field. Within PANO, we further propose Coupled Assignment Projection (CAP) to construct bidirectionally consistent encoding and decoding mappings, and Pairwise Latent Space Attention Layer (PaLA) to jointly update latent representations of each latent space through adaptive cross-space interactions. Extensive experiments on nine benchmarks spanning diverse physical systems, geometries, and observation scales demonstrate that PANO achieves state-of-the-art performance. Notably, PANO reduces equivalent plastic strain prediction errors on Unilateral Stamping by 28.3% (or more) and lift-coefficient prediction errors on AirCraft by 21.4% (or more) compared with existing methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Right Labels, Wrong Reasons: Signed Feature Attribution for Multi-Label Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22515)**  `arXiv:2611.22515`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kenton White_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a multi-label classifier assigns the right labels, it may do so for the wrong reasons. Diffuse feature combinations may vary across classifier families and lack a dominant feature. This paper proposes FLEX (Fisher LDA Explainer), a global surrogate that fits a single direct multi-label Fisher LDA over the full binary label matrix, producing signed feature attribution lists for each label in a single coherent coordinate system. Across the evaluated benchmarks and classifier families, FLEX reproduces the black box's predictions with consistently high fidelity. Pooling covariance across labels outperforms a per-label alternative on most datasets, with the advantage growing largest where labels are numerous and positive examples are scarce. FLEX's fidelity-complexity Pareto curve is monotonically increasing, giving practitioners a predictable path to higher-fidelity explanations as more features are used, and its in-sample fidelity matches or leads Binary Relevance alternatives once a modest feature budget is allowed. The magnitude of each discriminant weight vector bounds its label's separability, flagging labels that may be predicted correctly for the wrong reasons. The evaluated classifiers largely agree on which predictions to make, but reach those decisions through different feature subsets when no single feature dominates.
  </details>

- **[Eyes in the Update? Learning Where Rewards Land in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22438)**  `arXiv:2611.22438`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lixuan He, Tianzhe Chu, Shulin Tian, Shikang Zheng, Feng Chen, Yi Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal post-training has moved from broad instruction tuning toward reinforcement learning for image and video reasoning, where outcome rewards can improve final-answer accuracy. Yet these rewards are assigned to complete generated trajectories, while visual evidence is used only at sparse and uneven positions inside the response. Recent work has begun to make multimodal RL more token-sensitive through grounding signals or external token-level weighting. We ask a more specific question: can the allocation of the same trajectory-level reward be inferred from the model's own multimodal computation, rather than from an auxiliary annotator, perception module, or process label? We propose Gaze-RL, a representation-driven policy optimization method that redistributes completion-level advantage through a latent reward landing field. The field is computed from internal cross-modal geometry and local predictive uncertainty, assigning larger update weight to positions that are both visually anchored and decision-bearing while remaining mean-one, so the total credit of each rollout is preserved and only its positionwise allocation changes. Gaze-RL requires no process supervision, auxiliary perception module, or architectural modification, and improves both Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3.5 across a broad range of image and video benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Encoding, Decodability, and Use: A Five-Layer Framework for Benchmark Measurement Audits with Evidence from UAV Intent Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22434)**  `arXiv:2611.22434`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhirong Su, Tian Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark accuracy is routinely read as evidence that a model understands the construct the benchmark measures. We propose and instantiate on AeroIntent a five-layer framework that decomposes that reading into observation, representation, decoding, use, and behaviour. The framework separates encoding, decodability, and use as distinct levels. In the instantiation, the observation layer is unreliable on 7 of the 20 Drone programs and floors the direct visual correlate of radial velocity at a 0.26\% scale change. The representation layer exposes two task-designed RGB features with nominal signals. RAFT flow radial bias reaches $r=+0.257$ ($p=0.030$), and bounding-box optical-flow divergence reaches $r=+0.203$ ($p=0.029$). No multi-feature readout gives a fold-mean test correlation above the cluster minimum detectable effect (MDE), and the largest positive global out-of-sample correlation is $r=+0.080$. The decoding layer sets the MDE at $|r|=0.599$ at the multi-feature Bonferroni threshold ($\alpha=0.00714$, power 0.80), and the observed $r=+0.080$ has power 0.00. Two injection experiments establish the MDE's condition from both sides: aligned injections are detected at or above the simulated power, and orthogonal injections are not detected. The use layer finds accuracy consistent with program memory (program-identity 0.937/0.597/0.210) and multi-region static cues. The behaviour layer is consistent with accuracy not requiring a radial-velocity readout (appearance 0.6552 vs bounding-box trajectory 0.4080, cluster-level paired $\Delta=+0.2471$, $p=0.0132$).
  </details>

- **[Inventa: Decentralized Self-Improving Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21847)**  `arXiv:2611.21847`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Prithwish Jana, Nikos Kanakaris, Purak Jain, Anoop Deoras, Sahika Genc_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can generate research ideas and improve scientific discovery through iterative experimentation. However, increasing the number of agents or iterations does not automatically translate to producing more diverse or feasible ideas. Agents may converge on similar candidates, propagate unsupported claims through shared memory components and repeatedly apply search strategies that no longer yield any improvements.We introduce Inventa, a framework for automated scientific discovery that employs autonomous research units and verified shared knowledge. Each unit creates a lineage of candidate ideas using a mutator agent. A local orchestrator agent periodically adapts the mutator's harness. When progress stalls, the orchestrator agent replaces its model or restarts the idea evolution process. Verifiers evaluate each candidate idea and record verified improvements, useful components and constraint violations in a shared knowledge, the only channel between units. We evaluate Inventa on five benchmarks spanning the research cycle, covering retrieval (ScholarCatalyst), ideation (IdeaBench), proposal writing (IdeaScientist), and scientific law discovery (EurekaBench, SciLaws-Bench). It leads all five, achieving 73.6% on EurekaBench versus 56.2% for CORAL. On IdeaBench, it discovers 26.0 novel ideas per paper, compared with 18.6 for ResearchAgent, with 82% fewer model calls.
  </details>

- **[Margin Dynamics for Large Language Model Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21766)**  `arXiv:2611.21766`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingzi Xu, Mehmet Saygin Seyfioglu, Karim Bouyarmane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Practitioners choose among LLM alignment methods largely by trial and error. We provide a theoretical foundation: each pairwise method defines a vector field on the probability simplex that governs how the model redistributes mass between preferred and dispreferred completions. Projecting onto a single preferred/dispreferred pair reduces this to a one-dimensional margin ODE with a closed-form solution. These solutions reveal that DPO margins grow without bound (logarithmically as $O(\log t)$ in the gradient-flow limit) while IPO margins converge to a finite $1/(2\tau)$; that label noise induces a qualitative phase transition in DPO (unbounded $\to$ bounded) but affects IPO only quantitatively; and that gradient concentration under asymmetric weighting governs trainability at scale. We confirm all three predictions across five model families spanning four benchmarks. The closed-form solutions also enable principled method design: we prove that adding any label smoothing $\varepsilon > 0$ to SimPO creates a finite margin attractor at $\Delta^* = \beta^{-1}\log((1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)$, while $\varepsilon = 0$ (standard SimPO) has no attractor. We test this across three models (8B-9B) and confirm the prediction: the theory-derived configuration (TPO-S, $\varepsilon = 0.05$) is competitive with the strongest baselines, attaining the highest mean IFEval score among the compared methods on all three, validating that the predicted finite attractor is a usable operating point rather than proposing a new state-of-the-art loss.
  </details>

- **[Auditing World-Model Explanations in Retail Forecasting: Faithfulness, Accuracy and Price Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20811)**  `arXiv:2611.20811`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kathik Iyer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting models that read the price and promotion plan explain each forecast as a baseline, what would sell with nothing planned, plus the lift the plan adds, and merchants order stock and plan promotions on that lift. We show that an explanation can pass the checks used to trust it and still be wrong enough to cost money. We define eight checks for any forecaster that states a plan lift, six with a pass rule fixed before any result, and run them on a world model and on LightGBM. In generated store data with known effects, the world model passes the checks of construction, stability, price direction and agreement with sales and with LightGBM, yet when merchants plan into high-demand weeks it states 1.33 to 1.40 times the true lift, and 1.23 to 1.42 times in eight variants of that world; LightGBM overstates it too (1.26, and 1.16 to 1.35). Both still pick the right weeks to promote, but stock ordered to the world model's lift is off by 0.44 to 0.48 of the true lift. The world model's stated response to a lasting regular-price change is 4.7 to 6.5 times the true -0.5, an error in kind that only a measured cause finds. Sanity checks for explanations test whether an explanation depends on the model; a decision needs to know whether it is right about the world, and these results show the first does not give the second. For the final tuned world models of our companion paper on nine data sets, baseline and lift add up under each model's own plan on every window, but a deeper price cut lowers the forecast in 29% to 83% of item-week steps on Dominick's. Across 703 configurations from the settings searches, checks passed were not related to accuracy (Spearman 0.02, 95% interval -0.02 to 0.05). A noisy benchmark of price episodes in Dominick's data is reported as a check of direction only.
  </details>

- **[Activation-Space Exploration for Visual and Textual Reasoning with RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27012)**  `arXiv:2610.27012`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunan Zeng, Jinhao Jing, Xi Cheng, Yuwang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves visual and textual reasoning by sampling responses, scoring them with an automatic verifier, and updating the policy from the resulting rewards. The policy therefore receives direct learning signals only from trajectories that appear in its rollouts. Yet repeated sampling from the same policy can concentrate on similar reasoning paths, leaving useful alternatives unexplored under a fixed rollout budget. We introduce Activation-Space Exploration (ASE), an online method for diversifying training rollouts. By clustering activation differences between correct and incorrect responses to the same problem, ASE builds a structured bank of latent exploration vectors. Different vectors are then applied to subsequent rollouts to explore alternative reasoning paths. Under the same rollout budget, ASE improves average accuracy over GRPO on Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct by 5.41 and 3.15 percentage points across six visual mathematical and six general visual reasoning benchmarks, respectively. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base, it improves average Mean@8 over GRPO across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks by 4.87 percentage points, with gains also observed on Qwen3-8B-Base.
  </details>

- **[Pseudo-Label-Triggered Retraining from Forecast Errors for Online Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39789)**  `arXiv:2609.39789`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yeryeong Kwak, Yoo-Min Jung, Jonghun Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world time series forecasting systems operate under non-stationary data streams, where forecasting performance may degrade over time. Although retraining can recover the performance, it incurs non-trivial computational and operational costs. Under limited deployment resources, the key challenge is therefore not only how to retrain but also when to retrain. While existing retraining policies often rely on indirect indicators such as drift alarms or model staleness, we instead use realized forecast errors as direct deployment feedback. In this paper, we propose PILOT (Pseudo-label-Informed Learned Online Trigger), an online retraining framework that learns when to retrain from forecast-error dynamics. Since ground-truth retraining labels are unavailable, PILOT constructs a pseudo-label from future increases in forecast error and trains a lightweight scorer to predict it from observed error states. At deployment, PILOT uses only completed forecast errors and serves as a plug-in module for arbitrary forecasting backbones without architectural modification. We evaluate PILOT under standard multivariate forecasting settings across eight benchmarks with three representative backbones---DLinear, iTransformer, and TimesNet. Across all three backbones, PILOT achieves state-of-the-art average-rank performance among retraining policies while maintaining a favorable performance--efficiency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[OpenMHC: Accelerating the Science of Wearable Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16235)**  `arXiv:2607.16235`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Narayan Schuetz, Yuze Bai, Lianggang Pan, Edgar Eggert, Favour Nerrise, Juan Delgado-SanMartin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile and wearable devices offer an unprecedented opportunity for continuous, passive health monitoring and active health coaching. However, the largest wearable datasets are proprietary, and leading wearable foundation models trained on them are rarely open weight or include reproducible training code. To accelerate open science in wearable health, we introduce OpenMyHeartCounts (OpenMHC), the largest and most comprehensive wearable health dataset broadly accessible to qualified researchers, alongside open-source implementations and weights for recent wearable foundation models. Derived from over a decade of data collected through the My Heart Counts study app, OpenMHC comprises over 67M participant-hours of wearable recordings across up to 19 sensor channels (e.g., step count, heart rate, sleep, workouts) and up to 170 health, lifestyle, mood, and behavioral variables from 11,894 participants. Furthermore, we establish a benchmark for standardized evaluation across three tasks: (1) health and behavioral outcome prediction, (2) multivariate data imputation, and (3) time-series forecasting. Benchmarking classical methods alongside recent wearable and time-series foundation models, we find that while foundation models perform strongly, carefully tuned classical baselines like XGBoost remain highly competitive. By uniting broad-access wearable data with open code and model weights, OpenMHC provides a framework needed to drive reproducible advances in wearable health AI.
  </details>

- **[Offline Preference-Based Trajectory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17541)**  `arXiv:2606.17541`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Fernando Diaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline evaluation of agentic systems often collapses trajectories into a binary terminal success value, discarding information about partial progress. This, in turn, results in widespread ties that create statistical inefficiency by reducing effective sample size and weakening the ability to distinguish systems. We propose preference-based trajectory evaluation, an evaluation measure that compares pairs of trajectories directly by using temporal preferences between time-to-return profiles. We find that, across diverse agentic and interactive benchmarks, success-based metrics result in tied comparisons on roughly 75% of instances, while trajectory-aware preferences reduce ties to roughly 35%, improving discriminative power, ranking stability, and data efficiency. Our results suggest that benchmark saturation, often portrayed as the result of poor data collection or problem difficulty, may also be explained by the design of the evaluation measure.
  </details>

- **[Latent Q-Barrier Shielding for Safe In-Context Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25267)**  `arXiv:2605.25267`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Minjae Kwon, Amir Moeini, Shangtong Zhang, Lu Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe in-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) adapts online from interaction history without test-time parameter updates while controlling episode cost under a safety budget. Under out-of-distribution (OOD) deployment shifts, pretraining-only safe ICRL can give poor reward-safety tradeoffs because the remaining budget affects behavior only through frozen policy conditioning, not an explicit action-level check against predicted future cost. We propose a latent Q-Barrier shield that learns a context representation, latent dynamics, and an ensemble cost critic before deployment. Without parameter updates, the shield infers context from history and filters or softly reweights candidate actions using the remaining budget and predicted future cost. We prove a conditional one-step barrier-margin bound over realized candidate sets, with explicit Bellman and latent-prediction error terms. Across five safe ICRL benchmarks, the shield improves deployment-time reward-safety tradeoffs over a strong safe-ICRL baseline: after a short context window, it achieves higher return in four of five benchmarks while matching or lowering average episode cost in all five. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where Should Physics Enter? Information, Identifiability and Calibration in Probabilistic Sea Surface Temperature Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23129)**  `arXiv:2611.23129`  `cs.LG`  
  _Parmitha Vangapandu, Jonnakuti Pavan Kumar, Sathwik Narkedimilli, MSVPJ Sathvik, T. M. Balakrishnan Nair, Santanu Bhattacharya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advection-diffusion priors are a common way to make neural sea surface temperature (SST) forecasts physics-informed. We test where such a prior must enter a heteroscedastic ConvLSTM to help, using daily NOAA OISST v2.1 for the Bay of Bengal (1982-2024) and a synthetic benchmark on the same grid with known currents of controlled strength. (i) On real data, a soft advection-diffusion residual with a learned velocity gives no reliable benefit (lead-15 CRPS -0.007 degrees C, 95% interval [-0.022, +0.008]); a plain temporal-smoothness penalty matches it, and equivalence within 3% is not established either. (ii) Learned velocities do not recover the currents, whether placed in the loss, in an explicit transport step, or in a transport step without neural correction: they correlate with the reference no better than a shuffled-date null, even on synthetic data where knowing the currents would improve day-15 forecasts by 16-23%. (iii) Supplied currents help on synthetic data, but mostly as information: giving them to the network as input channels, without transport, recovers 92% of the transport design's lead-5 gain, and a frozen-current numerical forecast does as well as either. (iv) With GLORYS12 reanalysis currents on real data, the transport design is 6% and 4% worse at leads 1 and 5; reanalysis advection explains essentially none of the daily OISST change (correlation 0.02), and frozen-current transport is worse than persistence. (v) The defect that matters is lead-dependent miscalibration shared by every model. One variance multiplier per lead, fitted on validation data, brings 90% coverage to 0.85-0.91 at all leads and lowers day-1 CRPS by 13-21%; fine-tuning on 15-day rollouts fixes long-lead coverage but costs short-lead skill. This version withdraws an earlier claim of an 81% CRPS reduction that came from an uncontrolled comparison.
  </details>

- **[Get Hint-Augmented GRPO Done Right Through Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23124)**  `arXiv:2611.23124`  `cs.LG`  
  _Khiem Le, Ting Hua, Nitesh V. Chawla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GRPO has become the dominant reinforcement learning method for eliciting reasoning capability in LLMs using verifiable rewards. However, it suffers from a fundamental failure mode: when a training question exceeds the base model's current capability, all generated responses fail and receive zero reward, yielding no gradient signal and stalling further capability expansion. An emerging class of GRPO extensions, termed Hint-Augmented GRPO (HA-GRPO), adds a "hint" to the context to reduce the difficulty of the question, thereby producing a usable gradient signal. Nevertheless, existing HA-GRPO methods share a key issue: no hint is available at inference time to append to the question, creating a training-inference context mismatch that leads to inconsistent performance gains. In this work, we reinterpret the function of hinting -- not as reducing question difficulty, but as steering model behavior -- and propose ASH (Activation Steering for Hinting), a novel HA-GRPO method built on this view. During training, the model is presented with both hinted and plain questions, while a trainable steering vector is optimized to widen the margins between the model's generation probabilities for responses to the hinted version relative to the plain version, thereby capturing the question-agnostic component of the behavioral shift induced by the hint. During inference, the trained steering vector is directly injected into the model's activation, reproducing the hint-conditioned behavior without requiring any hint in the context. Experiments with four LLMs (Qwen3-1.7B/4B-Base, Llama-3.2-1B/3B-Instruct) show that ASH consistently improves pass@$k$ on competition-level mathematics benchmarks (AMC, OlympiadBench, AIME24, AIME25) and out-of-distribution benchmarks (Minerva, GPQA-Diamond). Notably, it improves average pass@8 of Qwen3-1.7B-Base and Qwen3-4B-Base by \textbf{2.60}\% and \textbf{2.21}\%.
  </details>

- **[Variance Restoration over Covariance Rank: Memory-Budgeted Analytic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23040)**  `arXiv:2611.23040`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elena Voss, Pixel Nomand, Marcus Hale, Sofia Reyes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analytic continual learning replaces iterative gradient updates with closed-form ridge regression over frozen pre-trained representations, summarizing all past sessions in two running sufficient statistics: the feature autocorrelation matrix $\Sigma_t$ and the class cross-correlation matrix $\Gamma_t$. Lifting backbone features into a wide random code of dimension $M \gg D$ substantially improves linear separability across tasks, but forces $\Sigma_t$ to grow quadratically as $\mathcal{O}(M^2)$. Under a fixed persistent memory envelope $\Bcal$, a learner faces a fundamental structural trade-off: store exact pairwise co-activations inside a narrower feature code, or expand to a wide code while compressing cross-coordinate interactions. We observe that the coordinate self-variances $v_t = \diag(\Sigma_t)$ and class statistics $\Gamma_t$ grow only linearly with $M$, whereas off-diagonal interactions dominate memory. We introduce \method (\textbf{Co}ordinate-\textbf{Re}stored \textbf{A}nalytic \textbf{C}ontinual \textbf{L}earning), which retains $(v_t, \Gamma_t)$ exactly, maintains off-diagonal co-activations through a streaming rank-$q$ factor $F_t$, restores exact diagonal variances via $\Sighat_t = F_t^\top F_t + \Diag(v_t - \diag(F_t^\top F_t))$, and solves the full $M\times C$ normal equations via the Woodbury identity. Across five class-incremental benchmarks on frozen ViT-B/16, \method matches dense full-covariance readouts within $0.09$ points while cutting persistent state by $90.7\%$ ($35.9$ vs.\ $385.3$ MiB). Under equal memory envelopes of $16$--$80$ MiB, prioritizing expansion width $M$ over covariance rank $q$ outperforms exact narrow readouts in $17$ of $18$ configurations by up to $+2.19$ points.
  </details>

- **[NVIDIA Kumo Tabular: Pushing the Accuracy$\unicode{8211}$Efficiency Frontier for Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23001)**  `arXiv:2611.23001`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jingang Qu, Valter Hudovernik, Martin Jurkovič, Cedric Lorenz, David Holzmüller, Federico Lopez, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Kumo Tabular, a family of open tabular foundation models for classification and regression, in three sizes: Kumo Tabular-S (27M parameters), Kumo Tabular-M (61M), and Kumo Tabular-L (214M). Pretrained only on artificial tables from the Kumo prior, they can make predictions on new tables without any training. In our approach, a table encoder compresses each row into an embedding, and an in-context learning transformer predicts the unlabeled rows from the labeled ones. Kumo Tabular introduces architectural innovations that improve both training stability and predictive performance, enabling the model to scale up to 150M cells on modern GPUs. Kumo Tabular-L ranks first on eight benchmarks: TabArena, TALENT, BeyondArena, ScoringBench, STRABLE, MulTaBench, RamanBench, and TabBench-Bio. These cover probabilistic regression, tables with over 1M rows or 100K columns, and tables with strings, text, or images. Accuracy improves with model size in all tested comparisons. All three sizes lie on the accuracy-speed frontiers of TabArena and BeyondArena. Kumo Tabular-L predicts faster than the runners-up, LimiX-2 on TabArena (36 times) and TabPFN-3.5 on BeyondArena (1.7 times). Weights and pretraining code are released under OpenMDW-1.1 and Apache 2.0, respectively.
  </details>

- **[The Kernel Reality Check: Benchmarking and Distilling Efficient Attention at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22947)**  `arXiv:2611.22947`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fırat Öncel, Cem Subakan, Mirco Ravanelli, Çağatay Yıldız_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Kernelized attention methods, which aim to replace the $O(N^2)$ complexity of softmax attention with $O(N)$ alternatives, have been the focus of intense research in the last five years. Despite growing interest, systematic evaluations and fair comparisons across approaches and scales are still missing. We analyze kernelized attention in two halves and highlight prominent limitations on each. On efficiency, FlashAttention achieves lower memory consumption and faster wall-clock time than SOTA kernelized methods during training and in vision transformers across most sequence lengths (with inference-time memory in causal language models the only clear exception), contradicting the core assumption for pure kernelized attention used as a drop-in replacement for softmax. We also discover that reported efficiency gains often fail to reproduce against modern baselines. On quality, current kernelized methods leave gaps of 2-10 points relative to softmax under a unified distillation protocol, and model scala alone does not close these gaps. We show this gap is not intrinsic to kernelization: LARA++, a principled extension of LARA that approximates softmax via importance sampling with adaptive proposal distributions, recovers up to 99% of softmax accuracy. We establish that the gap is closable; however, the computational overhead remains substantial, leaving the efficiency wall intact. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>
