- **[LoopPrune: Recurrent Pruning for Looped Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22757)**  `arXiv:2611.22757`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Huaijia Kou, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped diffusion Transformers reuse shared blocks to deepen computation and match larger models with fewer parameters, reducing storage and deployment burdens. Yet repeated attention and MLP computations remain costly. Pruning reduces storage and computation by removing redundant parameters or units. Training-free methods struggle to resolve recurrent contributions, while training-based methods lack explicit constraints on position-specific errors affecting later inputs and final outputs. We propose LoopPrune, a pruning and recovery framework addressing these requirements. Recurrent importance estimation combines shared-removal effects and position variation to select a shared structure; shared reconstruction pools recurrent input statistics to compensate weights. Loop-position recovery compares complete denoising predictions at identical student states and matches text guidance, updating all student parameters against a fixed dense teacher. Extensive experiments across four open-source models, six benchmarks, and three pruning paradigms demonstrate effectiveness. At 30% structured parameter pruning, Looped-DiT B/16 retains 93.14% of dense six-benchmark average accuracy, exceeding the best baseline by 9.49%. Across PyTorch, vLLM-Omni, and SGLang Diffusion, pruned SDXSv3 reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 34.36-44.64% and accelerates denoising 1.46-1.74x over dense models.
  </details>

- **[Escaping Latent Traps by Remembering: Training-Free Episodic Kernel Repulsion for World-Model Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22470)**  `arXiv:2611.22470`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yican Zhao, Bolin Yang, Ruizhe Wang, Haiyang Guo, Fuji Yang, Bingo Wing-Kuen Ling, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models plan towards a goal image by minimising the distance between predicted and goal latents. This distance is not geodesic: it has local minima, latent traps, behind walls, at corners and in dead ends, and memoryless receding-horizon replanning keeps the agent in them, revisiting states far from the goal. We propose Episodic Kernel Repulsion (EKR), a training-free cost term that penalises candidate plans by a top-$k$ Gaussian kernel to a novelty-gated memory of the latents the agent has actually visited, rescaled by the current base cost so that its strength is comparable across models. Across two JEPA world models and four benchmarks, EKR raises average success from 31.8% to 46.7% (LeWM) and from 54.7% to 64.0% (H-JEPA) with the same model, planner and budget. Same-memory counterfactuals show that, in most settings, EKR pushes plans away from visited states, in navigation, often at a higher base cost; stuck episodes and revisits drop accordingly. In seven of eight settings it matches or exceeds $4\times$ samples at $0.8$--$1.5\times$ compute. Without tuning, it transfers to DINO-WM with CEM: $+6.7$ and $+19.3$ points on official and longer-goal Wall tasks, and from 44.7% to 100.0% and from 21.0% to 32.0% on long-horizon PointMaze and Push-T.
  </details>

- **[BQ-LoRA: Binary-Quantized Low-Rank Adapters as Implicit Regularizers for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22334)**  `arXiv:2611.22334`  `cs.LG`  
  _Juyoung Park, Heejun Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning of quantized large language models is dominated by QLoRA, which combines 4-bit weight quantization with FP16 low-rank adapters. We ask whether the FP16 adapter precision is necessary. Replacing it with a single binary bit per adapter parameter, combined with a learned per-layer scale $s$ and a Scaled Straight-Through Estimator, reduces adapter storage by 13-16x. We introduce two variants -- NormScale, which binarizes under a norm-preserving scale with a clipped STE, and LearnThresh, which makes the binarization threshold learnable -- unified under a framework we call BQ-LoRA. Our main theoretical contribution is a tight characterization of the BQ-LoRA hypothesis class: it has bounded entrywise norm, a typical-set spectral norm bound that is $\Theta(r)$ times sharper than the worst case, a full-rank, near-isotropic update, and consequently a Rademacher complexity that yields an explicit $\tilde{O}(s_{\max}\alpha\sqrt{d_{out}/(rn)})$ generalization bound. FP16 LoRA has no comparable a priori norm or isotropy control. On Qwen2.5 (1.5B-72B), BQ-LoRA exceeds QLoRA's benchmark averages at every scale by 0.8-3.5 points; on LLaMA-2 (7B/70B) it remains competitive at the same storage reduction but does not uniformly lead the strongest FP16-adapter baselines (LoftQ at 7B, QLoRA at 70B). On Qwen2.5-7B the binary adapter preserves the 4-bit base WikiText-2 PPL within 0.06 (9.86 vs. 9.80) while QLoRA worsens it by 0.59. The reduction applies to inference-time adapter storage and serving; training memory matches QLoRA and step time rises by under 4%. We translate the storage saving into a deployment study, BQ-SGMV, by extending Punica's SGMV kernel with a binary inner-product primitive: the same A100-80GB holds 15.6x more adapters in HBM, and our research prototype eliminates cache misses under a skewed synthetic workload.
  </details>

- **[Regularized Score Centering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21791)**  `arXiv:2611.21791`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hao Bai, Chenlu Ye, Xuanchang Zhang, Aviral Kumar, Tong Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asynchronous reinforcement learning increases training throughput, but data from stale policies and numerical mismatch between training and inference can destabilize learning. To stabilize off-policy learning, score centering (SC) removes the effect of reward baselines on the expected update. Our gradient diagnostics show less extreme gradients with SC than with importance sampling (IS), but SC can still fail when the learner moves far from the behavior policy that generated the data. To limit this policy mismatch, we introduce Regularized Score Centering (RSC), which adds a reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) penalty to encourage the learner's next-token distribution to stay close to the behavior policy's next-token distribution. RSC works with or without truncated importance sampling (TIS), which caps token importance weights. Our theoretical analysis supports this approach, showing that KL regularization improves convergence guarantees. We evaluate dense and mixture-of-experts models on mathematical reasoning and agentic tasks, including online shopping, tool use, games, and fully asynchronous software engineering. RSC with TIS substantially improves sample efficiency across all agentic benchmarks, attaining higher training rewards with the same number of sampled responses. In final evaluations, it achieves top performance across all six agentic tasks, improving by 14.3% on AppWorld and reaching 9.9 times the strongest baseline's score on BabyAI.
  </details>

- **[Hybrid Quantum Selection of Learning Rates in Language-Model Training: Termination Criteria and an Interaction-Guided Schedule Oracle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21701)**  `arXiv:2611.21701`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wei-Che Hung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning-rate choices strongly affect language-model fine-tuning, but the best joint profile across layer groups is generally unknown. This paper studies quantum minimum finding for layer-group learning-rate selection within a hybrid classical-quantum training framework. It combines Durr-Hoyer minimum finding with BBHT search and compares fixed-budget and confidence-based stopping policies using a lookup oracle built from measured profile scores at fixed training checkpoints. The paper then introduces an Interaction-Guided Schedule Oracle that scores and marks joint schedules in superposition without a precomputed score table. Classical one-step loss measurements supply the main, pairwise and three-way interaction effects between layer groups. The circuit adds the effects for each schedule, compares the resulting score with the current baseline score, and marks improving schedules for amplitude amplification. Measured candidates are checked classically before acceptance. Uniform-rate training supplies the initial baseline; using schedules from classical adaptive methods as baselines for joint refinement is left for future work. In closed-loop fine-tuning of a 1.5-billion-parameter language model, each selected schedule drives the next 200 classical training steps. Benchmark accuracies lie within the range observed across five uniform-rate seeds. The results give a working example of how quantum superposition can support adaptive learning-rate selection during training; the remaining quantum limitations are discussed in the paper.
  </details>

- **[FACE: Flow-Guided Angular Channel Estimation Framework for mmWave MIMO Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21698)**  `arXiv:2611.21698`  `cs.LG`  
  _Samitha Gunarathne, Eslam Eldeeb, Nurul Huda Mahmood, Matti Latva-aho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Acquiring accurate channel state information (CSI) is essential for millimeter-wave (mmWave) multi-input multi-output (MIMO) systems. However, it remains challenging due to high-dimensional channels, limited pilot observations, and measurement noise. To address these challenges, we propose flow-guided angular channel estimation (FACE), a novel channel estimation framework based on flow matching, an emerging generative modeling paradigm that learns continuous transport dynamics between probability distributions. FACE exploits the inherent angular-domain sparsity of mmWave channels and incorporates the learned flow matching model as a generative channel prior. Numerical results show that FACE consistently achieves higher channel estimation accuracy than both conventional estimators and diffusion-based generative estimator across diverse channel and system configurations. Moreover, FACE maintains high estimation accuracy with fewer pilot observations and achieves an average achievable rate close to the perfect-CSI benchmark. Compared with diffusion-based estimation, FACE requires substantially fewer sampling steps while achieving superior or comparable estimation accuracy, reducing inference time and computational cost. Overall, the proposed framework demonstrates the potential of flow matching for accurate, pilot-efficient, and computationally efficient mmWave MIMO channel estimation.
  </details>

- **[SLICES: A Benchmark and Evaluation Framework for Self-Supervised Learning on Sparse ICU Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18868)**  `arXiv:2611.18868`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hannes Ill, Dimitrios Proios, Leo Anthony Celi, Peter J. Schüffler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning (SSL) results for intensive care unit (ICU) time series are hard to compare when data processing, encoders, and adaptation vary together. We present SLICES, a benchmark and evaluation framework for comparing and extending SSL methods under a shared clinical setup. It fixes 24-hour value and observation-mask inputs, a Transformer encoder, patient-level splits, four endpoints, two adaptation protocols, and five matched seeds across MIMIC-IV, eICU-CRD, and their pooled union. Masked autoencoding (MAE), joint-embedding prediction (JEPA), and contrastive learning provide reference implementations. Among these objectives, MAE leads mean AUPRC in all nine frozen classification comparisons, also under converged probes, and in seven of nine fine-tuning comparisons. Fine-tuned MAE leads hospital mortality at full labels and scores higher than a supervised Transformer of the same architecture at every label fraction, and SSL fine-tuning gives the lowest length-of-stay error. Supervised baselines remain strong elsewhere: GRU-D leads 24-hour mortality and XGBoost leads acute kidney injury in all three regimes. Rankings depend on the adaptation protocol, the comparator set, and whether the probe loss matches the metric. A two-seed MIMIC-IV rerun with the maintained code stays within two across-seed SDs of the reference in all 48 downstream runs. The code repository supports new objectives, shared downstream evaluation, and regeneration of the reference results, so new SSL methods can be compared with these references under the same conditions. Generalization to unseen hospitals is not tested here and is the natural next extension.
  </details>

- **[Estimating aerosol particle number size distributions from PM2.5 with physics-regularized flow matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33557)**  `arXiv:2610.33557`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhi-Song Liu, Jenni Köykkä, Wenqing Peng, Ammar Kheder, Petri Clusius, Michael Boy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The mass concentration of particles smaller than 2.5um (PM2.5) is cheap to measure and widely monitored, whereas the particle number size distribution (PNSD) requires costly instruments and is measured at only a few research stations. Yet many aerosol effects, such as vapor uptake, cloud droplet formation and deposition in the human respiratory tract, depend on the PNSD rather than on mass. We explore whether generative deep learning models can infer the current PNSD from routine data: from the PM2.5 record and eight meteorological variables over a 24h window, a model generates an ensemble of plausible hourly PNSDs for the same window, reflecting that one mass can arise from many size distributions. Rather than proposing a single best model, we use two contrasting Finnish stations, the boreal forest site Hyytiala and the urban site Kumpula, as a controlled testbed. On it, we benchmark six generative models against deterministic regression and historical-mean baselines, locally and after transfer between sites, and test physical constraints for neural network training. Flow matching performs best overall; its guided variant has the lowest continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) at both sites. A constraint based on a reduced aerosol general dynamic equation (GDE) lowers the CRPS by 5.5-11.5% at the training site and reduces errors after transfer between sites. Transfer remains harder than local estimation, identifying multi-station training as the next step towards PNSD estimates at unmonitored locations. This benchmark establishes physics-informed generative models as a promising route from routine air-quality monitoring to size-resolved aerosol information.
  </details>

- **[OVAL: Output-Aware Local Page Bases for KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06686)**  `arXiv:2610.06686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ashkan Shahbazi, Chayne Thrash, Soheil Kolouri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context decoding, whether over long reasoning traces, long videos, or robot episodes, is increasingly limited by the memory of the key-value (KV) cache. Page-retrieval sparse attention makes each decoding step cheaper by attending only to the top-scoring KV pages. However, it still stores every page exactly, and adds a small digest per page for scoring, so the cache ends up slightly \emph{larger} than the full cache. We propose OVAL, which stores each page as a pair of output-aware low-rank bases that act as both the retrieval index and the storage. A key basis scores the page and rebuilds its keys when the page is selected, and a PCA basis rebuilds its values. The exact pages can then be dropped, and nothing extra is added to the cache. The key idea is that the key basis should be output-aware. A low-rank error in the keys perturbs the attention output at first order, but perturbs the page mass that retrieval ranks only at second order in expectation. OVAL therefore builds the key basis from a mix of key geometry (key PCA) and the attention-output error, with a per-head mixing weight set in closed form. At the same attended budget, OVAL is best or tied-best in eight of nine long-reasoning settings. It matches FullKV on MATH500 with 66\% of its memory, is 1.25 points above the best baseline on AIME24 with 56\%, and is on par with FullKV on LongBench~v2. On \mbox{Video-MME} and LVBench, it is within 0.32 points of exact-page retrieval at the same read, while storing 53\% of the full cache instead of 103\%. Its savings also stack with quantization and token pruning: with 4-bit bases, it is within 0.25 points of FullKV at 41\% of its memory.
  </details>

- **[Where Root Cause Analysis Fails: A Retrieval-Reranking Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36686)**  `arXiv:2609.36686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hada Melino Muhammad, Luan Pham, Laure Barrière, Sachin Shetty, Leonardo Pulga, Flora D. Salim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying the root cause of an anomaly among hundreds of sensors is critical for preventing safety incidents and costly downtime in complex monitored systems. Existing studies evaluate root cause analysis (RCA) methods using top@k accuracy. We show that this metric has a fundamental blind spot: it conflates two failure modes, retrieval failure, where the true cause is never considered, and reranking failure, where it is considered but ranked too low. In this work, we introduce a retrieval-reranking decomposition and audit four well-known benchmarks to expose this blind spot. Our experiments show that, on industrial CPS benchmarks, the statistical baseline scoring every sensor mis-ranks the true cause 79-100% of the time, and graph-based methods never clearly beat the best statistical baseline, whether their causal graphs are learned on short fault windows, on retrieved candidate pools guaranteed to contain the cause, or on multi-day normal-operation data. Meanwhile, on microservice benchmarks, where each injected fault drives the faulty service's own metric far above its baseline, retrieval is nearly solved (98-100%). Guided by the decomposition, we build a two-stage pipeline combining a multi-signal retriever with an LLM reranker that, as one fixed configuration, matches or exceeds the best baseline's top@1 accuracy on all six benchmark suites (by up to +12 points), with no causal graph or labeled data required. When all methods rank the same retrieved candidates with the true cause guaranteed present, adding a short system-description document lets the reranker lead the best baseline by +7 to +18 points on every benchmark. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ScAn-Bench: Evaluating Scaling Analysis Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35707)**  `arXiv:2609.35707`  `cs.LG`  
  _Artin Sermaxhaj, Nastaran Alipour, Donat Sinani, Johannes Hog, Neeratyoy Mallik, Steven Adriaensen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in machine learning is driven by large-scale foundation models, where scaling laws and finding optimal scaling prescriptions for architecture, data, and hyperparameters are key in advancing the state-of-the-art. Therefore, it is surprising that no systematic study evaluates the methodology to obtain scaling laws and prescriptions across different model types. To shed light on this crucial blind spot and facilitate future research, we introduce the surrogate benchmarks ScAn-Bench-LLM and ScAn-Bench-VLM based on 4524 and 8024 checkpoints of language and vision-language model pipelines. On our benchmarks, we perform the first systematic evaluation of both data acquisition and extrapolation methodology for scaling analysis across different data modalities.
  </details>

- **[Dandelion: A Spherical Flower for Neural Simulation of Planetary Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27521)**  `arXiv:2608.27521`  `cs.LG`  
  _Till Muser, Giovanni Abati, Ivan Dokmanić_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many dynamical processes unfold on the sphere but the default scientific machine learning architectures are Euclidean. Applying these architectures on a regular lat--lon grid causes problems: Cartesian convolutions become distorted at high latitude; 2D FFTs in Fourier neural operators incorrectly assume double periodicity; Cartesian positional encodings in ViTs distort spherical geodesic distances. Recent work moves towards natively spherical primitives, including spherical convolutions (e.g., DeepSphere or DISCO), Spherical Fourier Neural Operators (SFNOs), and geodesic attention. Here we propose Dandelion, a spherical version of Flower---a warp-based neural PDE solver. A Dandelion layer computes a tangent-plane displacement and then transports features along great circles. We obtain a U-Net-like structure by implementing hierarchical pooling entirely in the spherical-harmonic domain. There are thus no convolutions: spatial mixing is achieved only through spherical coordinate changes---or warps. To compare Dandelion with existing spherical architectures, we release an evolving benchmark suite of challenging, natively spherical PDE datasets including a modified Galewsky jet, anomalous chained turbulence, Cahn--Hilliard decomposition, spherical Riemann shocks, Held--Suarez dry atmospheric transport and global ocean dynamics. This new benchmark fills the gap between existing spherical datasets which are either too small and stylized, or much too large (ERA5) for model iteration. Dandelion achieves the best performance on all but one benchmark setting, where the best model is Flower2D, also based on warps. The spherical primitives result in the most physically consistent forecasts.
  </details>

- **[An Open-Source Training Dataset for Foundation Models for Hyperparameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23417)**  `arXiv:2605.23417`  `cs.LG`  
  _Aaron Klein, Herilalaina Rakotoarison, Luca Thale-Bombien, David Salinas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most hyperparameter optimization methods require extensive tuning of their own hyperparameters, often limiting their ability to generalize across different benchmark families. Foundation models for hyperparameter optimization that learn optimization principles from a large collection of optimization trajectories offer a promising alternative, with the potential to outperform manually designed methods across diverse problem classes. However, prior work as either relied on non-public datasets or on purely synthetic data, limiting reproducibility and generalization to real-world problems. As a result, progress in this area has been constrained by the lack of large-scale, real-world, publicly available pre-training data. We introduce BBO-Pile, the first open-source dataset comprising over 500K optimization trajectories evaluated across 3,095 different hyperparameter optimization tasks and global optimization problems for different optimizers, which represents by far the largest public dataset for this task. Using this dataset, we train a family of foundation models at multiple scales, ranging from 2M to 80M parameters and from 200M to 2B training tokens, and study their scaling behavior with respect to compute. Our results demonstrate that large-scale pre-training is a viable and effective approach to imitate hyperparameter optimization methods, paving the way for future research in this direction.
  </details>

- **[GPTZero: Robust Detection of LLM-Generated Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13042)**  `arXiv:2602.13042`  `cs.LG`  
  _George Alexandru Adam, Alexander Cui, Edwin Thomas, Emily Napier, Nazar Shmatko, Yue Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While historical considerations surrounding text authenticity revolved primarily around plagiarism, the advent of large language models (LLMs) has introduced a new challenge: distinguishing human-authored from AI-generated text. This shift raises significant concerns, including the undermining of skill evaluations, the mass-production of low-quality content, and the proliferation of misinformation. Addressing these issues, we introduce GPTZero 5.0, a state-of-the-art industrial AI detection solution, offering reliable discernment between human and LLM-generated text. Our key contributions include: introducing a hierarchical, multi-task architecture enabling a flexible taxonomy of human and AI texts, setting a new state-of-the-art for AI detection accuracy on 11 independent third-party benchmarks and 18 writing domains, and achieving superior robustness to adversarial attacks and paraphrasing via multi-tiered automated red teaming. GPTZero offers accurate and explainable detection, and educates users on its responsible use, ensuring fair and transparent assessment of text.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Success Rates: Trainability and Extractability for Offline GCRL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05459)**  `arXiv:2602.05459`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jan Malte Töpperwien, Aditya Mohan, Marius Lindauer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (GCRL) methods are typically benchmarked by the success rates of optimized hyperparameter configurations. However, success rate by itself does not provide information about the sensitivity of algorithms to hyperparameters for value learning or policy extraction, since an algorithm could obtain the same success rate across a wide range of hyperparameters, or might succeed only on specific settings. We study this phenomenon in the offline GCRL algorithms GCIQL, GCIVL, QRL, and CRL under a shared policy extractor, by constructing landscapes of interaction between the learning rate and policy extraction hyperparameters such as the temperature of advantage-weighted regression (AWR). These landscapes reveal large, task-dependent differences in hyperparameter sensitivity. On AntMaze-M, 59% of QRL configurations and 66% of CRL configurations achieve at least 90% of the method's best sampled success rate, compared with 3% for both GCIQL and GCIVL. On Cube and Scene, GCIQL and GCIVL instead attain higher success over broader hyperparameter regions than QRL and CRL. To interpret these differences, we analyze how reliably learned advantages rank actual goals from the same trajectory above random goals, and how strongly AWR concentrates policy updates on a small subset of transitions. Our findings indicate strongly differing behavior of these algorithms at policy-extraction time, even under the same success rates. Given these results, we argue that offline GCRL methods should be evaluated by both attainable success and the needed hyperparameter range to sustain it, complemented by post-hoc diagnostics to understand the signal used to train the policy. The overview and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HOPSE: Scalable Higher-Order Positional and Structural Encoder for Combinatorial Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15405)**  `arXiv:2505.15405`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guillermo Bernárdez, Marco Montagna, Louis Van Langendonck, Martin Carrasco, Amirreza Akbari, Louisa Cornelis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have proven highly effective at modeling relational data, pairwise connections cannot fully capture multi-way relationships naturally present in complex real-world systems. In response to this, Topological Deep Learning (TDL) leverages more general combinatorial representations--such as simplicial or cellular complexes--to accommodate higher-order interactions. Existing TDL methods often extend GNNs through Higher-Order Message Passing (HOMP), but face critical scalability challenges due to the steep complexity overhead of propagating messages through combinatorial structures. To overcome this limitation, we propose HOPSE (Higher-Order Positional and Structural Encoder), a framework free of learnable message passing layers that uses Hasse graph decompositions to derive efficient and expressive encodings over arbitrary higher-order domains. Notably, HOPSE training and inference scale linearly with the size of combinatorial representations while preserving the expressive power and permutation equivariance of the HOMP approaches. Experiments on molecular and topological benchmarks show that it matches or surpasses state-of-the-art performance while consistently achieving speedups over HOMP-based models, opening a new path for scalable TDL. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SiGMA: Learning under Graph-Level Heterogeneity with Gated Message-Passing and Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.00547)**  `arXiv:2503.00547`  `cs.LG`  
  _Raphael Pellegrin, Lukas Fesser, Melanie Weber_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In graph-level learning, graph neural networks are typically applied with a fixed computational template: the same depth, local message-passing operator, and global attention mechanism are used for every input graph. However, different graphs within the same dataset can vary substantially in prediction difficulty and may require different mixtures of local and non-local computation. We study this phenomenon as graph-level heterogeneity in both classification and regression tasks. To address it, we propose SiGMA (Sigmoid Gated Message-Passing and Attention), a gated hybrid graph neural network architecture that combines message-passing and attention heads within each layer. Inspired by the growing use of gated attention and gated computation in large language models and vision transformers, each head in SiGMA is modulated by a learned node-wise sigmoid gate, allowing the model to adaptively regulate which local or global mechanisms are active for different nodes, layers, and input graphs. The resulting architecture can emulate expressive message-passing backbones, admits near-identity computation paths that are not forced to over-smooth, and enables direct non-local communication beyond message-passing neighborhoods to mitigate over-squashing. Empirically, SiGMA achieves strong performance across graph-level benchmarks, consistently matching or improving over competitive graph transformer and hybrid baselines. Our results suggest that adapting computation to graph-level heterogeneity is a promising direction for building more flexible and effective graph learning architectures.
  </details>

- **[VSpark: Speculative Decoding for Autoregressive Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22666)**  `arXiv:2611.22666`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Suho Yoo, Youngjoon Jang, Hyebin Cho, Taeyeop Lee, Jaewook Jeong, Joon Son Chung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Our aim is to accelerate autoregressive vision-language-action models (VLAs), which generate robot actions one token at a time. Speculative decoding makes this possible: a lightweight draft model proposes several tokens at once, and the VLA verifies them in a single pass, accepting only those that match its own predictions, so decoding remains lossless. The speedup, however, is limited in robot control, where many different tokens encode nearly the same motion and a draft close to the correct action is still rejected. To address this, our approach, VSpark, is built upon three design choices: (i) rollouts collected with calibrated action noise, which expose the draft to a wider range of robot states; (ii) feature alignment, which trains the draft to match the VLA's hidden features rather than its output tokens alone; and (iii) continuation reuse, which keeps the predictions the VLA has already computed along rejected drafts as candidates for the next verification step, instead of discarding them. On four VLAs with different action tokenizers and four manipulation benchmarks, VSpark achieves 1.28x to 2.10x speedup while keeping the VLA's decoding lossless.
  </details>

- **[CoBoVLA: Decoupling Language from Action with Discrete Codes for Robust and Interpretable Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22395)**  `arXiv:2611.22395`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Mehul Arora, Dhruv Agarwal, Ambuj Agrawal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models achieve high success rates on the LIBERO benchmark but remain sensitive to paraphrasing. Existing VLAs ranging from 0.6B to 7.5B parameters experience a 22-52 point performance drop on LIBERO-Para while SmolVLA exhibits an even larger decline, from 83\% to 8\%. Because most VLAs jointly learn language understanding and action generation, it is difficult to determine whether a failure originates from misunderstanding an instruction or executing it incorrectly. To address these challenges, we introduce CoBoVLA, or CodeBookVLA, a 250M-parameter VLA that decouples language understanding, planning, and control through learned discrete interpretable codes. A text encoder maps the instructions to shared step codes, which a visual planner then converts into action code sequences executed by a language-independent actor. This design improves robustness to instruction paraphrasing while allowing failures to be traced to individual stages. It also enables efficient real-time control on resource-constrained hardware by encoding instructions only once and using compact discrete representations to keep each component lightweight. CoBoVLA achieves 91.4\% success on LIBERO-Goal, compared with 82.8\% for SmolVLA, under a matched protocol, with 17 ms per control step latency. On LIBERO-Para, it achieves 81.1\% across 4,092 unseen paraphrases and 78.5\% on matched episodes, compared with 36.5\% for SmolVLA LoRA fine-tuned on paraphrases. Without two-step demonstrations, CoBoVLA completes 48.8\% of 160 free-form two-step tasks. Finally, success reaches 91\% when instructions are correctly encoded but falls to 27.2\% otherwise, identifying language understanding as the main source of remaining failures. Together, these results demonstrate that decoupling language understanding and control can improve instruction robustness while making VLA failures easier to diagnose.
  </details>

- **[ReactWorld: Structuring Interaction Responses for Embodied Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06120)**  `arXiv:2611.06120`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Hanwei Liu, Huijia Zhang, Hanzhang Wang, Guangyu Liu, Hanbei Zhan, Linjie Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied video world models predict scene changes caused by robot actions, yet generated object responses may remain inconsistent with task intent and contact dynamics. Critical responses in embodied scenes often concentrate in local regions and specific time intervals. Inspired by chain-of-thought reasoning, we introduce ReactWorld, an embodied video generation model guided by structured interaction anticipation. In the generation backbone's feature space, an interaction response field predictor anticipates how the interaction will unfold, explicitly describing participant roles, future motion response intensity, and local motion changes. A response feedback adapter forms increments to object features from the difference between the object branch's outputs with predicted and zeroed local dynamics inputs. It also converts predicted displacements into positional biases for temporal attention. These two mechanisms guide object feature updates and information retrieval across frames, respectively. Through interaction anticipation and generative feedback, ReactWorld achieves an average score of 77.51 on RBench, outperforming PhysisForcing by 3.29 points with improvements in all nine categories. On EZS-Bench, it achieves a Domain score of 86.98 and an average score of 81.87.
  </details>

- **[Spool: Low-Latency Streaming Inference for Hybrid Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23228)**  `arXiv:2611.23228`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Rui Ning, Om Chabra, Hari Balakrishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Applications such as visual accessibility agents and robotics require answering questions about recent video content promptly, over a bounded window of past frames (e.g., the last two minutes). Hybrid vision-language models (VLMs), which interleave attention with linear recurrent layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), are attractive for this setting, but maintaining a sliding window is expensive. The reason is that recurrent layers combine all past frames into a shared state, so expiring old frames normally requires re-running the entire window through the model. We observe that the recurrent update is affine in the state with input-dependent coefficients, so the effect of each frame can be captured as a transformation that composes associatively. We present Spool, a training-free framework that caches per-frame state transformations and composes them efficiently as the window advances, removing expired frames from each recurrent layer's state without replaying frames through the backbone. Removal is exact within each layer; as with key-value eviction in attention, expired frames can retain residual influence through the cached inputs computed by earlier layers. We design a dedicated data structure and GPU kernels to achieve low caching and composition overhead. Across five open-source hybrid VLMs and four video reasoning benchmarks, Spool achieves the lowest p95 time-to-first-token in all 20 model-benchmark tests, reducing it by a geometric mean of $6.6\times$ over the faster of two recent vLLM baselines, while improving accuracy by 2.3 percentage points on average.
  </details>

- **[LoopZip: Visual Token Compression for Efficient Looped Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22728)**  `arXiv:2611.22728`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Zhicheng Liu, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped vision-language models (VLMs) deepen computation by reusing shared layers, approaching larger models' capabilities with fewer parameters and easing the storage and deployment burden of stacking independent layers. However, repeatedly processing long visual sequences still incurs substantial compute and GPU memory costs. Visual token compression reduces these costs by shortening sequences. Existing methods mainly select original tokens directly or combine selection with aggregation. In looped models, the former may overlook changing token importance across calls and discard information needed later; the latter lack joint consideration of output changes across calls, limiting information preservation. We propose LoopZip, a training-free visual token compression algorithm addressing both issues. Visual aggregation builds a compact candidate pool before the looped backbone, and cross-invocation selection averages instruction attention from the first two existing calls of the same shared attention layer to retain tokens needed by both states. Weight-tied value correction then jointly fits shared visual-value adjustments to both pre-selection layer outputs, reducing output deviations across states. To validate LoopZip's effectiveness, we conduct extensive experiments covering two open-source looped VLMs, eleven benchmarks, and three pruning ratios. On LoopVL-1B, LoopZip retains 97.16% of the original model's performance at 50% pruning ratio and exceeds the strongest baseline by 5.36% at 87.5% pruning ratio. Across Hugging Face Transformers, vLLM, and SGLang, LoopVL-1B with LoopZip reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 58.36%-58.98% and achieves 2.01-2.47x end-to-end speedups over the original model.
  </details>

- **[Social Signal Bench: Evidence-Weighted Evaluation of Co-occurring Social Signals in Video and Audio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22704)**  `arXiv:2611.22704`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Siddharth Ravi, Anders R. Bargum, Hugo Anselme, Patrizio Cugia di Sant'Orsola, Alberto Cardaci, Flora Kincso Parragi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing social signals such as interest and hesitation requires interpreting context-dependent behaviors that may co-occur in conversation. Existing affective computing benchmarks rarely evaluate such co-occurring signals, and conventional multi-label F1 overlooks semantic relationships and annotator disagreement. We introduce Social Signal Bench, a 47-hour densely annotated benchmark for the evaluation of social signal recognition and engagement, comprising two datasets: Social Signal Bench Video (25 hours), covering ten social signals in audiovisual recordings, and Social Signal Bench Audio (22 hours), covering six signals in audio-only recordings. Each dataset pairs a public training set of online conversational video, of which 10 hours are expert-annotated and the remainder pseudo-labeled, with a private expert-labeled test set spanning four conversational domains. We also propose Evidence-Weighted F1 (EW-F1), which weights reference labels by annotator evidence and assigns graded credit to semantically related predictions through optimal per-clip matching. Controlled perturbations demonstrate that EW-F1 distinguishes semantically related errors from unrelated ones, unlike conventional F1. Under a shared evaluation protocol, 7B open models post-trained on Social Signal Bench outperform general-purpose frontier models on video and match them on audio.
  </details>

- **[Do Diffusion Models Learn to Generalize Basic Visual Skills?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22427)**  `arXiv:2611.22427`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Amish Sethi, Boya Zeng, Wenhao Chai, Zhuang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models generate high-quality images, but how well they generalize is hard to measure. Current evaluations rely on human preference and prompt-following benchmarks for text-to-image models and on distribution-level metrics such as Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and Inception Score (IS) for class-conditional models, but these cannot tell whether models learn basic visual skills or largely match training patterns. To address this gap, we introduce the Visual Generative Lab (VGL), a controlled experimental framework for studying how diffusion models learn and generalize basic visual skills, including size, position, rotation, count, color, shape, and their compositions. By generating synthetic training data for each skill from explicit rules, VGL allows precise measurement of generalization with task-specific, rule-based metrics. Training models from scratch on these data reveals three patterns in this controlled setting: (1) with our default design, extrapolation is limited: size and position degrade within a few pixels of the training range, count reaches only about 50% accuracy on out-of-range counts, and rotation extrapolation accuracy varies widely across seeds; (2) for compositional generalization, the number of distinct basic visual skill combinations matters more than dataset size; (3) skills mostly fail independently, except position. Code and data-generation scripts:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Deciding Without Pointing: A Case Study of Whether a Decision Model Localizes the Anomalies It Detects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21995)**  `arXiv:2611.21995`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Hsiang-Hung Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models can answer a typed question about an image with a probability per option, without generating text. In our case study of Clef-flash (post-trained Qwen3.5-9B backbone) on industrial inspection benchmarks, we ask whether it localizes the anomalies it detects. We obtained uneven results with zero-shot detection: per-category image AUROC 0.924 on MVTec AD and 0.668 on MVTec AD 2. On the former, the decision head's routing attention produces 0.391 mean per-image pixel AUROC, and hiding the image tokens from it leaves detection unchanged. The multimodal LLM's backbone attention does point at the defect, however it falls below a center prior when we change question. It falls below chance if we remove textual context in the question, partly due to backbone attention leaving the object, while the head's map hardly changes. The backbone's states carry the location when probed with normal images, on the test-split images, however, the decision depends on the defect region. A larger model, Clef, that comes with a post-trained Qwen3.8-27B backbone, gives no useful map in the decision head either. We found that it's possible to recover anomaly location by asking question specifically on an image region, retraining the head or reading the backbone, but none matches dedicated few-shot localizers.
  </details>

- **[PRUE: A Practical Recipe for Field Boundary Segmentation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27101)**  `arXiv:2603.27101`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Gedeon Muhawenayo, Caleb Robinson, Subash Khanal, Zhanpei Fang, Isaac Corley, Alexander Wollam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale maps of field boundaries are essential for agricultural monitoring tasks. Existing deep learning approaches for satellite-based field mapping are sensitive to illumination, spatial scale, and changes in geographic location. We conduct the first systematic evaluation of segmentation and geospatial foundation models (GFMs) for global field boundary delineation using the Fields of The World (FTW) benchmark. We evaluate 18 models under unified experimental settings, showing that a U-Net semantic segmentation model outperforms instance-based and GFM alternatives on a suite of performance and deployment metrics. We propose a new segmentation approach that combines a U-Net backbone, composite loss functions, and targeted data augmentations to enhance performance and robustness under real-world conditions. Our model achieves a 76% IoU and 47% object-F1 on FTW, an increase of 6% and 9% over the previous baseline. Our approach provides a practical framework for reliable, scalable, and reproducible field boundary delineation across model design, training, and inference. We release all models and model-derived field boundary datasets for five countries.
  </details>

- **[Nunchi: Ears Help Eyes in Multi-Party Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23115)**  `arXiv:2611.23115`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _JunYeong Park, Shijing Wang, Seonwook Park, Michael Stengel, Shalini De Mello, Sang-Il Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying whom a person looks at is central to understanding multi-party conversations, yet gaze geometry alone becomes ambiguous as more people appear. Conversations offer cues that geometry lacks, such as who is speaking and what is said, but no gaze dataset provides them for many multi-party scenes. We introduce SORBET, a benchmark of 3,340 samples from 1,578 clips with synchronized speech, transcripts, and attended person labels, covering more multi-party scenes than existing gaze datasets. Prior gaze models struggle to use these cues, which come in different forms, must be linked to the right person, and can conflict with gaze geometry. We propose Nunchi, a framework that constructs a text prompt from the transcript and the outputs of gaze and speaker models, links every cue to the candidates with shared labels, and lets a vision-language model (VLM) weigh gaze geometry against conversational cues to identify the attended person. Nunchi outperforms the strongest existing method by 4.8 percentage points. On SORBET, conversational cues help whereas additional visual cues do not, and the gains hold across three VLMs, with automatic transcripts, and on two existing datasets.
  </details>

- **[CueClash: Frontier Vision-Language Models Judge 3-D Relations Close to the Picture Plane](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22180)**  `arXiv:2611.22180`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Soham Dan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A vision-language model (VLM) can judge 3-D relations from the picture or from the ground layout. In photographs the two usually agree. We separate them with CueClash, rendered scenes in which the 2-D cue for each of five relations is right on exactly half the statements. The shortcut gap is accuracy when the cue is right minus accuracy when it is wrong. For Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-5.5 and Gemini 3.6 Flash at low reasoning effort it is +18 to +37 percentage points. It is +7 to +29 without two relations whose cue-wrong items are confounded with borderline cases or occlusion. Given ground coordinates and each relation's definition, they answer 99 to 100% correctly. Pixel measurements with camera depths remove most of the gap for objects fitting through openings. The failure lies in the step from the picture to the layout. The maps that the models draw of the scene from above mostly keep the depth order but, like the picture, shorten depth. GPT-5.5's maps stay close to a copy of the picture. Opus 5.5's and Gemini's maps undo about 0.6 of this shortening. Gemini's then shrink all depths by a further uniform factor. Opus 5.5 follows the picture mainly on fits, and its maps correct depth about as much as human observers of real scenes do. The three models also lose accuracy on CV-Bench photographs where a reliable 2-D cue misleads. In two open models the layout information reaches the language model, but their maps lose much of it.
  </details>

- **[WorldFlow: Joint Video and 3D Motion Prediction via Large-Scale Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39278)**  `arXiv:2610.39278`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Yuhao Lu, Jiongxu Chen, Hexiang Wei, Jiajin Wu, Juelin Li, Mingjian Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce \method{}, a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train \method{} on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Deep Learning Models for Wheelchair Configuration Classification in Accessible Automated Vehicle Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21945)**  `arXiv:2611.21945`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Samir Ahmed, Shaaz Saleem, Devin Dhami, Wesley Maia, Angel Martinez-Sanchez, Parthib Roy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Making automated vehicles accessible requires consideration of vulnerable road users, including wheelchair users. To offer a more tailored experience that supports independence for this group, vehicle design needs to accommodate the wide range of wheelchair configurations. However, wheelchairs are individualized, varying in frame geometry, propulsion, and visual appearance. Moreover, occupants or accessories may obscure visual cues. Existing mobility-aid datasets only label the presence of a wheelchair, rather than describing its specific configuration. To fill this gap, this paper introduces the Wheelchair Configuration Image Dataset (WCID), comprising 467 face-blurred images across 192 capture groups solely for evaluation. Each image is annotated with (i) a two-class label of manual or powered, (ii) a chair subtype, (iii) occupancy, viewpoint, setting, lighting, occlusion, propulsion, and control, with a 273-image validation split and 194-image test split. Three convolutional neural network backbones and a frozen vision-language probe are trained on an external image dataset and evaluated on WCID to measure cross-dataset generalization, while also testing robustness using Gaussian blur. Convolutional models do well on clean images, but performance drops under strong blur, leaving a gap before deployment. Code, split assignments, labels, trained weights, and the face-blurred images is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Diffusion Transformer for End-to-End Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22962)**  `arXiv:2611.22962`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Wu, Yifei Wang, Weihang Guo, Chen Wei, Lydia E Kavraki, Niloofar Mireshghallah, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pixel-space diffusion learns visual representations and image generation jointly in a single end-to-end training stage, without a separately pretrained visual encoder. However, plain pixel-space Transformers still trail leading two-stage latent diffusion models in generation quality and training efficiency. We hypothesize that part of this gap arises from retaining one token per patch throughout the backbone, which does not explicitly encourage a visual hierarchy that summarizes related content at progressively coarser levels. To address this, we introduce Hierarchical Diffusion Transformers (HiT), which progressively group related tokens and represent each group with a single token. The main Transformer bottleneck processes these compact group tokens, and ungrouping restores features to their original image positions. A compression budget regulates the average compression ratio across the batch, allowing group memberships and counts to vary across images and diffusion timesteps during both training and generation. The model exhibits emergent semantic structure, learning to group coherent objects and object parts without segmentation labels. In text-to-image generation, HiT further learns to group caption words with image patches depicting the same concepts, without region annotations. HiT achieves 1.48 FID on ImageNet $256\times256$ and reaches comparable generation quality to JiT using approximately 40\% of its cumulative training FLOPs. For text-to-image generation, HiT exceeds UniLiP's average score across five benchmarks with roughly $6\times$ fewer denoiser parameters and $3\times$ fewer forward FLOPs. These results suggest that learning a visual hierarchy within the generator breaks down the barrier to stronger image generation without external visual pretraining.
  </details>

- **[From Perception to Simulation: Unveiling and Empowering Mental Spatial Dynamics in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22541)**  `arXiv:2611.22541`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiachun Li, Zhuoran Jin, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning is a fundamental component of world understanding for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While existing research has advanced the perception of spatial relations in static scenes, the domain of dynamic spatial simulation remains relatively unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce MentalGrid, a benchmark focused on spatial reasoning tasks that require models to simulate evolving spatial states without intermediate visual feedback. Extensive evaluation on our benchmark reveals a significant deficiency in the spatial simulation capabilities of existing models. Specifically, we find that performance is constrained by perceptual limitations in simple simulation scenarios and hindered by inadequate multi-step reasoning as complexity increases. To address these challenges, we propose a three-stage curriculum learning framework utilizing 16k specialized spatial reasoning samples. This approach yields SpatialWalker, which achieves a 33.8% average improvement over its base model, surpassing proprietary models like Gemini-3-Flash in spatial simulation. Furthermore, SpatialWalker demonstrates robust generalization, maintaining a 4.9% average gain on out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrating the pivotal role of spatial simulation as a fundamental building block for broader spatial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[See, Think, Use: Diagnosing Perception and Reasoning Failures in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01467)**  `arXiv:2611.01467`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present See, Think, Use, a protocol that traces each answer of a vision-language model to perception, reasoning or the use of perception. The diagnosis shows a developer which stage to improve and directs test-time compute to the stage that fails. Benchmarks score the final answer, and an answer that rests on the image earns the same credit as a guess or a memorized prior. The protocol asks the model two extra questions per item and needs no access to its weights. A localization probe asks for a box around every instance and measures seeing, and a text-replacement probe answers from a description and measures thinking. Use is the rate at which the answer follows the model's own perception across counterfactual image pairs. The rate needs no answer labels, because the question and its prior are the same on both images. On two model families and six datasets the protocol shows that the knob that helps follows the task family. Answers follow the model's perception except on images that contradict a common prior, and asking the model to localize first repairs this failure when its boxes are right. Allocating compute by the measured stages comes close to the best uniform policy at lower cost and beats a random allocation of the same compute wherever a knob raises the short stage. We will release the code, the prompts and the per-question outputs.
  </details>

- **[Reading Scene States: Uncovering Safety Failures in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23206)**  `arXiv:2611.23206`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Tongxi Wu, Yuyang Cheng, Jiacheng Xian, Menglin Yang, Kai Chen, Jian Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can provide harmful assistance by interpreting visual events, even without an explicit request for the underlying harmful objective. We identify this \textbf{scene-state safety vulnerability} and introduce \textbf{SceneSafetyBench}, comprising 375 image--question pairs spanning Initial, Medium, and Ultimate scene states and five visual presentations. Across eight VLMs, scene-state inputs elicit harmful assistance with attack success rates (ASRs) of 45.6--72.3\%. Comparative analysis across three VLMs shows that scene-state inputs most closely resemble QR-related images in their layer-wise safety-related signal profiles, while exhibiting lower average late-layer readouts and distinct signal dynamics during generation. To turn this vulnerability into a systematic attack, we propose \textbf{S3TA}, which translates harmful objectives into scene states, queries image--question pairs independently, and assembles the responses. On seven MM-SafetyBench categories across eight open-weight and commercial VLMs, S3TA achieves a model-average ASR of \textbf{71.2\%, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.1 percentage points}. Text-side filters and visual moderation provide limited protection. These findings highlight the need to safeguard the assistance models infer through scene-grounded reasoning, beyond screening input content.
  </details>

- **[A tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude open-water search and rescue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22825)**  `arXiv:2611.22825`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hui Xu, Chenfei Hou, Henglai Wei, Hao Wang, Yidong Jin, Xiulin Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large aerial platforms often operate above 1.5 km to improve the efficiency of open water search and rescue (OW-SAR). However, long imaging distances, wide fields of view, and limited sensor resolution reduce objects to a few pixels, increasing their susceptibility to environmental interference and making detection harder than for conventional small objects. To our knowledge, existing datasets focus on low-altitude scenarios and do not support automatic detection at 1.5-2.0 km. We therefore present TinyWOD, a tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude OW-SAR using lifebuoys as detection targets. It comprises 11,359 manually annotated images and 19,505 annotated instances spanning diverse environments, lighting conditions, water colors, and surface ripples. Compared with existing datasets, TinyWOD contains much smaller objects and a substantially higher proportion of tiny instances; its average equivalent side length (ESL) is 12.7 pixels, with 76.96% of in stances having ESLs below 15 pixels, reflecting actual high-altitude water-rescue imaging conditions. We define APtiny, a tiny object detection metric, and benchmark mainstream and state-of-the-art networks using this and conventional metrics. All evaluated networks effectively detect tiny waterborne objects, demonstrating TinyWOD's reusability and utility. We expect this dataset to support research on tiny object detection in high-altitude OW-SAR.
  </details>

- **[NexSight: Verifiable Tasks for Fine-Grained Agentic Long-Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22790)**  `arXiv:2611.22790`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Tianci Xie, Li Chen, Pinxue Guo, Rui Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained questions about long videos ask how many times an action occurs, in what order events unfold, or exactly when a state changes. Answering them can require finding brief moments scattered across hours of footage. Agentic models that decide when and where to look promise to find these moments, yet this ability is hard both to measure and to learn. Sparse supporting evidence may establish that an action occurred without establishing its total count; verifying timeline-wide claims requires checking for missed occurrences and double counting. Answer-only supervision, in turn, does not explicitly supervise the search for evidence. We present NexSight, which builds verifiable tasks to both evaluate and train agentic long-video understanding. Its agentic pipeline, SightForge, accepts a task only if a verifier blind to the draft answer can reconstruct it from evidence densely reacquired from the raw video and counterexample checks fail to refute it. This yields SightLine, 310 evidence-traceable tasks from 108 long videos, evaluated through SightLoop, a shared multiscale harness supporting temporal exploration, local inspection, evidence integration, and revisiting while recording returned observations. On disjoint videos, solver trajectories on verified tasks become step-level supervision for observation decisions. The strongest model reaches only 54.52% on SightLine, yet 79.35% when given the annotated evidence. With under 6K examples, Sight-35B nearly doubles its base model's interactive accuracy (21.29% to 42.26%), outperforms the 397B model of its family, begins using explicit spatial inspection after post-training, which its base model never uses, and gains over 8 points on two external long-video benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[ForceBody: Force-Paired Parametric Body Motion with Torque Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22769)**  `arXiv:2611.22769`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Joonwoo Kwon, Yufei Zhang, Xiaoming Liu, Zijun Cui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forces and torques drive human motion, central to biomechanics, robotics, and physics-aware animation, yet they are often absent from learning-based motion pipelines for body reconstruction and generation. Some motion datasets provide force annotations, but the paired motion sequences are incompatible with the parametric body models used in modern learning pipelines. In addition, existing datasets typically obtain joint torques through inverse dynamics, which is inherently sensitive to the quality of the input kinematics; however, they do not quantify the reliability of these torque estimates. We close this gap with ForceBody, the first dataset to bring ground truth force supervision into a parametric body representation, shipped with per-sample torque uncertainty. ForceBody pairs the SKEL body model with ground reaction forces and inverse-dynamics joint torques across 10,386 motion trials (9.7M frames, 26.9 hours) from 140 subjects. Each torque label is paired with a per-frame, per-joint uncertainty annotation obtained by Monte Carlo sampling through the inverse dynamics pipeline. We benchmark six architectures (MLP, Conv1D, LSTM, GRU, Mamba2, Transformer) on ground reaction force and joint torque prediction from SKEL motion. As one example use of the released uncertainty, finetuning a Transformer with per-sample uncertainty weighting reduces the MAE of joint torque estimation by 5%. Code and data are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FVScore: Specification-Driven Neuro-Symbolic Video Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22673)**  `arXiv:2611.22673`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Do Xuan Long, Long T. Le, Yiwen Song, Min-Yen Kan, Tomas Pfister, Yale Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Faithfulness Video Score (FVSCORE), a neuro-symbolic framework for specification-driven video generation evaluation. Existing evaluators rely on holistic MLLM judgments or vision-language metrics, yet aligning their scores with human judgments and tracing them to specific temporal violations remain open challenges. FVSCORE addresses these limitations by compiling each video into explicit propositions capturing prompt requirements and implicit propositions capturing unstated physical, temporal, and cross-modal constraints. It grounds each proposition using specialized perceptual tools and evaluates the resulting confidence traces with soft temporal operators inspired by Linear Temporal Logic. A Significance-Aware Calibration step then aligns verification with human perception by retaining only perceptually significant violations before producing a timestamp- and evidence-grounded report. We conduct extensive experiments across public and complex multi-event benchmarks with the strongest video models to date, demonstrating that FVSCORE outperforms holistic and agent-based evaluators in human correlation while providing highly accurate and interpretable feedback.
  </details>

- **[Motion-Aware Reward Modeling for Instruction-Guided Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22495)**  `arXiv:2611.22495`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Li Li, Xiangbo Gao, Bo Ni, Samyadeep Basu, Viet Dac Lai, Jing Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models for instruction-guided video editing can favor temporal consistency at the expense of motion. We study this failure, termed \emph{motion collapse}, using controlled motion-suppression variants of real edits. On VE-Bench, the evaluated composite reward prefers a motion-suppressed candidate in $97\%$ of cases. Learned video rewards and a VLM judge also struggle with mild suppression, while latent optimization and reward-guided fine-tuning exploit this weakness to increase reward as motion declines. A linear probe of our base reward backbone performs near chance on mild suppression, motivating an explicit inter-frame dynamics input. We propose a motion-aware reward model that combines an RGB frame-difference stream with preference training against motion-suppressed and fabricated-motion negatives. A distillation anchor preserves alignment with human quality judgments. On the VE-Bench motion ladder, our model reduces the rate of selecting motion-suppressed candidates to $0\%$ while achieving a Spearman correlation of $0.57$ with human quality ratings. It also detects unseen suppression variants and preserves motion during reward-guided fine-tuning on two editing pipelines. These results support explicitly modeling motion in rewards for video editing.
  </details>

- **[MAVE: Reconstructing Editable 3D Blockouts from Film Shots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22464)**  `arXiv:2611.22464`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Chang, Jian Yang, Jiangnan Shao, Xiaofeng Wang, Zhaoxin Fan, Renjing Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Creators can change viewpoints and character paths, then render the edited scenes to condition video generation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EgoLink: Egocentric Language-Vision Interactive Network Knowledge Challenge Summary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21690)**  `arXiv:2611.21690`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Renhe Sun, Yueying Feng, Bohan Yu, Tong Niu, Yunqi Liu, Zitong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The EgoLink Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026 benchmarks egocentric multimodal intelligence linking first-person perception, social reasoning, and interactive action. Unlike benchmarks centered on visible objects and activities, EgoLink evaluates whether models can interpret emotional and social cues, resolve visually grounded references, and convert user requests into verified tool-mediated actions. Its two tracks cover social reasoning in egocentric video and interactive agents that complete service tasks through dialogue, tool use, and closed-loop execution. Together, they test whether perception, reasoning, and decision-making remain coupled under both offline question answering and state-changing interaction. We highlight a multi-stage Track 1 data pipeline for multiple-choice question (MCQ) generation, chain-of-thought generation, automatic quality grading, and video-contribution filtering. We summarize the task design, evaluation metrics, rules, leaderboards, and representative solutions. The benchmark combines objective answer scoring with process- and result-based evaluation, enabling analysis of both final correctness and execution quality. The best Track 1 system achieved 0.9263 accuracy, while the best Track 2 system achieved 0.844 joint success and 0.916 micro accuracy. Participant reports reveal a shared principle: strong systems make intermediate evidence explicit through candidate verification, option-robust reasoning, scenario-aware planning, tool canonicalization, and state-aware correction before answering or acting. These findings highlight the importance of process verification and evidence-grounded reasoning for reliable egocentric multimodal intelligence.
  </details>

- **[LynnReal-Hand: Unlocking World Interaction with Hand Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21479)**  `arXiv:2611.21479`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shaohao Rui, Chaoyang Zhang, Xiaofeng Mao, Weijie Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive video world models enable exploration through text and camera controls, yet direct object interaction requires finer control. Hands provide a natural interface, but translating continuous hand movements into coherent visual responses remains challenging because of complex hand-object dynamics and demanding real-time computation. To address these challenges, we introduce LynnReal-Hand, a hand-controlled video world model that unifies precise interaction modeling with efficient streaming generation. We curate 100K interaction clips spanning diverse real and synthetic environments and develop hand-aware supervision to improve fine-grained hand motions and object interactions. Building on this foundation, we learn a causal flow map for few-step generation and introduce region-aware score distillation that combines complementary video and image priors to refine interaction dynamics and visual appearance. To further reduce inference latency, we propose Ultra-Fast-VAE, a compact video decoder that preserves high reconstruction fidelity with substantially lower computational cost. Extensive evaluations on HandBench demonstrate superior hand-following accuracy and visual quality compared with existing approaches. Our full-sequence model achieves an FVD of 181.67 and a MANO Dice score of 93.02, while the three-step causal model maintains a MANO Dice score of 92.49. For real-time interaction, 540p streaming on a single H200 reaches 18.03 FPS with 741ms latency and an optimized peak GPU memory footprint of only 34.89GB. We will release the datasets, model weights, and the full training and inference code to support research on hand-controlled video world models.
  </details>

- **[SkillPE: Creativity-Oriented Cinematic Skill Evolution for Text-to-Video Prompt Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34335)**  `arXiv:2609.34335`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanwei Huang, Mingxuan Zhu, Shujie Li, Shiyuan Liu, Yuanxing Zhang, Arpit Narechania_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving high-quality cinematic text-to-video generation remains challenging for non-experts, whose prompts often lack professional narrative and creative design. Existing prompt enhancement (PE) methods and static cinematic skills offer limited capabilities for exploring diverse creative realizations. We propose SkillPE, a creativity-oriented PE framework that systematically evolves reusable cinematic skills through reference-guided exploration. Starting from expert-provided cinematic knowledge, SkillPE initializes and normalizes 20 structured seed skills. It then retrieves movie references categorized as resonators (good matches), dissonants (weak matches), and divergents (creatively inspiring near-misses). Resonators and dissonants refine skill applicability and execution, while divergents inspire alternative cinematic realizations through controlled mutations that preserve user intent. Candidate skills are assessed through generated videos along prompt fidelity, cinematic quality, narrative appeal, and creativity to construct the final skill libraries. Experiments on StoryEval and VBench demonstrate improvements of up to 1.10 points over seed skills and 1.02 points over the strongest external baseline on a 7-point four-dimensional evaluation scale. Overall, SkillPE offers a practical approach to balancing fidelity and creativity in cinematic text-to-video generation.
  </details>

- **[Video-HopChain: Multi-Hop Questions and Confidence-Gated Exploration for Video Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25773)**  `arXiv:2609.25773`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Trung Nguyen Quang, Yuhao Dong, Shuo Sun, Shuai Liu, Shulin Tian, Kim-Hui Yap, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  HopChain has shown on still images that multi-hop data synthesis improves vision-language reasoning, because long chain-of-thought reasoning exposes errors that compound across steps, while most data used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) rarely demands a chain of visual evidence, so these weaknesses are likely to stay unexposed. We observe the same problem in video, where this framework has not yet been explored. We therefore build Video-HopChain, a dataset of 22,550 multi-hop video questions over 13,378 videos, together with a held-out benchmark of 1,000 questions. Each question chains three to six yes/no questions about moments in one video, and each yields one of two integers depending on its answer. The final answer is the sum of these integers, so an exact match on that sum gives the verifiable reward that RLVR needs. We first train Qwen3-VL-8B with GRPO on a standard video dataset, and a second stage on Video-HopChain then raises the mean over eight video understanding and reasoning benchmarks from 55.4 to 57.9 and improves every one of them. Training on such a dataset, however, exposes a known limitation of GRPO: its learning signal comes from the reward variance within a group, so hard questions whose rollouts are all incorrect and easy questions whose rollouts are all correct both leave the group with no gradient. To recover these groups at the same compute budget, we introduce Confidence-Gated Exploration (CGE). With 8 rollouts per question, CGE samples the first 4 as usual. If these 4 are either all correct or all incorrect, it samples the last 4 with the policy's most confident token masked inside the reasoning span, and removes the masked positions from the loss while all 8 rollouts enter the advantage. With CGE, the mean rises further to 59.3. We release the dataset, the checkpoint, and the data generation and training code.
  </details>

- **[Uncovering Faithfulness Gaps in VLMs via Explanation-Driven Counterfactual Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17953)**  `arXiv:2609.17953`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Sihao Ding, Santosh Vasa, Aditi Ramadwar, Thomas Monninger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can produce Natural Language Explanations (NLEs) that sound plausible yet remain inconsistent with the visual evidence they cite. We present Explanation-Driven Counterfactual Testing (EDCT), an intervention-based protocol that extracts visual concepts cited in a model's explanation, applies verified minimal edits to them, and tests whether the resulting answer and explanation remain consistent with the edited image. Using this protocol, we create EDCT-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark spanning three complementary domains: knowledge-intensive visual question answering (OK-VQA), safety-critical driving (DriveLM), and 3D spatial reasoning (3DSRBench). Across the evaluated VLMs, EDCT reveals substantial faithfulness gaps, with models frequently producing responses inconsistent with verified visual changes. Finally, our fine-tuning study suggests that EDCT-generated counterfactuals provide high-impact training signals.
  </details>

- **[Any2Building: Unifying Point Clouds and Images for Building Reconstruction via Shared Spatial Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23187)**  `arXiv:2611.23187`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongye Hou, Zhuoxiao Li, Liu Xiangyu, Tengxi Wang, Jing Ou, Dongli Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Abstracting structured buildings from limited observations is essential for 3D urban modeling and digital twins. Partial point clouds and overhead images of the same building provide incomplete 3D surface measurements and visual cues to its footprint, roof geometry, and rooftop structures, respectively.
