We present ReScene, which stores each visit as an immutable compact update, so that any earlier visit can be recovered exactly by applying the stored updates in order, without retaining past images. To localise each update, ReScene attributes the disagreement between the preceding reconstruction and the new images to individual Gaussians through their compositing weights. This attribution guides the suppression of outdated Gaussians and the refinement of persistent ones, while newly visible surfaces are initialised from learned multi-view depth. We also introduce Multi-State, a benchmark of non-rigid deformation and partial material removal, including synthetic scenes with ground-truth geometry. ReScene achieves quality higher than sequential 3DGS checkpointing with 2.5--4.8$\times$ less uncompressed history storage. At comparable PSNR, it also reduces foreground depth error by 3.2$\times$ relative to sequential ChronoGS, showing that accurate rendering does not necessarily imply accurate geometry. Code and benchmark are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DominoBench: Evaluating Multi-Step Physical Reasoning from Still Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20385)**  `arXiv:2611.20385`  `cs.CV`  
  _Emilio Ferrara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a vision-language model look at a picture of a chain reaction and tell what will happen? Recent multimodal models describe scenes fluently and solve textbook physics problems, which suggests they should also predict how a scene of ramps, ledges, pendulums, seesaws and dominoes plays out once a ball is pushed. We tested this claim on 30 models from 10 families and reached a clear conclusion: unaided, no model predicts the outcome reliably. We built DominoBench, a benchmark of still images of 2D rigid-body scenes whose outcome a physics simulator computes. It holds 854 items in 427 minimal pairs, in which a change of one to three grid steps in one quantity decides whether one person survives, and every item comes with a four-rung hint ladder ending in the exact calculation that decides the outcome. Unaided, frontier models from Anthropic, Google and OpenAI are right about the person whose fate flips in at most 75% of items and answer both twins of a pair correctly in at most 40%. Pointing at the deciding step helps little, and exact geometry given as text barely helps, yet once the deciding calculation and the quantities it needs, including speeds that a still image cannot show, are written out, the same models reach 80-92% on the deciding person, while small open-weight models stay near chance. Physical prediction from a still image therefore fails at the quantitative step: the strongest models use the deciding quantity when they are given it, but do not produce it on their own. For models that plan or act in the physical world, a fluent account of a scene is thus no evidence of a correct prediction: such models must find and compute the deciding quantity unprompted, and DominoBench tests whether they do.
  </details>

- **[FuGIBench: Evaluating Reliable Preparation in Streaming Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16400)**  `arXiv:2611.16400`  `cs.CV`  
  _Changheng Cai, Zhichao Huang, Song Ren, Zhichen Zhao, Hongsen Liu, Chaoyang Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An assistant watching a worker can prepare for an emerging need before the worker is ready to receive help. As the need evolves, the assistant must maintain preparation until a response is due, or withdraw it if the need disappears. Video anticipation and interactive response benchmarks leave this interval between early evidence and response largely unmeasured. We introduce FuGIBench (Future-Grounded Interaction), a streaming-video benchmark in which a model chooses among waiting, reversible preparation, and response given a causal video prefix and a task contract. The benchmark contains 19,893 synthetic tool-assistance videos across ten tool tasks, in handover, ordinary-activity, and cancelled-need tracks. One primary score credits sustained preparation, incorporates response-interval alignment, and penalizes activity when the reference prescribes waiting. On a frozen set of 1,000 episodes, a reference system with a longer preparation lead has poorer response alignment, while similar handover leads accompany different levels of activity during ordinary work in matched scenarios. Together with cancellation analyses, these results show why evaluating preparation requires tracking decisions as needs develop, become actionable, or disappear.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Across Views: Cross-View Referring Reasoning for Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01074)**  `arXiv:2611.01074`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Gan, Liuqian Wang, Zhuping Hu, Yufei Gao, Wentao Chao, Chong Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-view object geo-localization (CVOGL) aims to identify a query-view target in a satellite image, yet reliable instance grounding remains challenging under substantial viewpoint discrepancies. Although recent methods incorporate multimodal prompts, they insufficiently address the varying transferability of linguistic and visual evidence across views. Viewpoint-dependent descriptions (e.g., building facades visible in oblique UAV views but not in top-down satellite images) may become unreliable for satellite grounding, while visually similar instances further complicate target identification. To address these challenges, we rethink CVOGL from a visual grounding perspective and propose GAV (Grounding Across Views), a view-aware framework for constructing target-centered evidence and establishing discriminative cross-view correspondence. Specifically, view-aware referring reasoning mitigates viewpoint-dependent ambiguity, multi-token exemplar prompting and contrastive alignment strengthen visual-semantic correspondence, and scene-level supervision with alignment-based verification distinguishes competing satellite instances. We further introduce CVOGL-Text, a street- and drone-to-satellite benchmark with query-derived expressions. GAV achieves state-of-the-art performance on MoP-UAV with 62.55% Acc@0.5 (+18.19 percentage points) and outperforms evaluated click-point baselines on CVOGL-Text.
  </details>

- **[Wizard: Manipulate Any World in Real Time via Agentic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40339)**  `arXiv:2610.40339`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuzhe Wu, Zijun Lin, Cheston Tan, Bihan Wen, Yeying Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in video world models have enabled increasingly realistic and interactive virtual environments, opening new possibilities for player-customizable gameplay. Agentic frameworks further advance this direction by decoupling explicit game logic and controllable world representations from visual generation. However, existing approaches support only simple game settings and predefined motion patterns, generalize poorly to unseen environments and characters, and offer limited real-time interactivity. In this paper, we introduce Wizard, an agentic game world model that enables real-time, precise control over character motions while generalizing zero-shot across novel scenes, characters, and actions. To achieve this, we construct a diverse dataset spanning 805 scenes and 178 characters, incorporating skeleton and geometry information as structured proxies to condition video generation. Our agentic framework explicitly tracks and updates game states to support character-aware adaptive masking and world-anchored spatial conditioning, improving character identity preservation and spatial consistency. Experiments on WBench show that our method achieves higher gameplay fidelity with comparable video quality and spatiotemporal consistency, while using fewer parameters and less training data than state-of-the-art world models. These advantages are also retained in our two-step real-time variant. Overall, Wizard combines agentic control with structured proxy-based generation to enable player-driven game creation with real-time, generalizable, and mechanically consistent world simulation.
  </details>

- **[SAGE: Saliency-Aware Generalist Encoder with Multi-level Feature Enhancement for Visual Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36719)**  `arXiv:2610.36719`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyu Chen, Yucheng Huang, Le Yang, Qi Dang, Xin Kong, Haifeng Huo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained vision transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated strong transferability across visual recognition tasks. However, adapting a shared representation to diverse downstream tasks remains challenging, as different tasks demand varying levels of semantic abstraction and spatial precision. We present SAGE (Saliency-Aware Generalist Encoder), an encoder-side architecture that enhances pretrained ViT representations by integrating multi-depth features, register-token context, saliency-guided modulation, and aligned local views into a unified multi-scale feature pyramid. Evaluated with ViT-B and ViT-L backbones on six visual tasks across seven benchmarks, SAGE achieves 62.8 box AP, 55.3 mask AP, and 78.5 keypoint AP on COCO; 60.3 semantic segmentation mIoU and 44.9 instance segmentation AP on ADE20K; 94.76\% classification top-1 accuracy on TinyImageNet; and 81.10 oriented detection AP$_{50}$ on DIOR-R. Combined with a progressive two-stage training strategy, SAGE-L achieves the best performance among the selected reference methods in six of the seven benchmark settings, while remaining competitive on keypoint estimation against substantially larger models. Importantly, these tasks share the same SAGE encoder and multi-scale feature interface, while employing task-specific prediction heads. To the best of our knowledge, SAGE is the first publicly reported encoder to achieve highly competitive results on six visual tasks across seven benchmarks, demonstrating its generality and competitiveness.
  </details>

- **[ARISE: Authentic Reference-Informed Stability Enhancement for Continual Multimodal Forgery Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36509)**  `arXiv:2610.36509`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Yaxiong Wang, Yujiao Wu, Lianwei Wu, Qi Zhang, Li Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal forgery detection is commonly studied under a static assumption: training attacks are expected to approximate those encountered after deployment. This assumption becomes increasingly fragile as generators, editing systems, and multimodal large language models continually introduce new forgery mechanisms and forensic evidence. We formulate this problem as attack-incremental continual multimodal forgery detection, where the binary real/fake interface remains fixed while emerging attacks must be learned without forgetting previous ones. We introduce Fable-Bench, which organizes attack evolution through complementary joint-modality, text-side, and image-side protocols. We further propose ARISE (Authentic Reference-Informed Stability Enhancement), motivated by the open-ended forged class and the comparatively reusable geometry of authentic content. ARISE fits a real-only Gaussian mixture model to projected authentic replay representations as a compact historical reference. Meanwhile, the proposed Gaussian-Responsibility-Aware Stability Preservation (GRASP) derives a sample-adaptive anisotropic metric that strongly constrains historically stable representation directions while retaining plasticity elsewhere. Our Vulnerability-Aware Replay (VAR) further estimates stage-wise vulnerability from authentic-density degradation, forged-to-authentic boundary confusion, and authentic-mode occupancy drift, then assigns bounded replay emphasis to fragile stages. All adaptive signals are obtained from training-time replay statistics, while inference uses only the original multimodal backbone and binary classification head. Experiments across three attack-evolution protocols and multiple detector architectures demonstrate improved historical retention and final average performance over representative continual-learning baselines.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing and Correcting Bias in MLLM for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36210)**  `arXiv:2610.36210`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xusheng Liang, Jianqiao Sun, Hao Zhang, Yulei Niu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiawei Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video question answering is challenging since the answer often hinges on a few decisive moments scattered throughout the video, while memory constraints force Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to sample frames at extremely low rates, resulting in sparsity that risks omitting key evidence. Though aggressively enlarging the context size is intuitive, we argue that performance remains fundamentally constrained by the bias, i.e., the misleading cues caused by spurious linguistic correlations and salient yet irrelevant visual observations. In this paper, we first introduce S-MME, a benchmark designed to systematically diagnose shortcut-biased behavior in MLLMs. To mitigate this failure, we further propose a training-free framework, Counterfactual Long-video Evidence-Aware Reasoning (CLEAR). By treating the full video-question pair as a complete view, CLEAR estimates bias effects via augmented views by only keeping the question or locally salient visual clips. Then, we intervene hidden states to mitigate the discrepancy at inference such that the correct answer can be predicted confidently. With experimental studies across five benchmarks, we show that our method is generalizable and can be integrated in different MLLMs, yielding consistent gains in prediction and robustness. We hope CLEAR can offer a complementary direction in test-time scaling for long-video understanding.
  </details>

- **[OVM-Occ: Open-Vocabulary Monocular Occupancy Prediction via Geometry-Aware Vision-Language Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35312)**  `arXiv:2610.35312`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuyang Yu, Haibo Qin, Shaohan Hu, Wei Shan, Xiaoqing Guan, You Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular occupancy prediction in indoor scenes aims to recover a scene's 3D geometry and semantics from a single RGB image. Existing methods are restricted to a closed set of predefined categories, limiting their ability to generalize to unseen categories. This limitation is particularly critical in indoor scenes, where objects exhibit highly diverse and fine-grained semantic categories. We study open-vocabulary monocular occupancy prediction for indoor scenes and present OVM-Occ, a Gaussian-based occupancy framework with an instance-aware vision-language teacher. Specifically, we adopt Language-Embedded Gaussians as the intermediate scene representation, propose a geometry-aware deformable attention module to refine Gaussian semantic features, and construct instance-level vision-language embeddings to provide reliable semantic supervision. We further introduce geometry-aware decoupled semantic distillation to mitigate the interference between geometry learning and semantic learning. To evaluate open-vocabulary occupancy prediction performance, we introduce OpenOcc-ScanNet and OpenOcc-Replica, two extended benchmarks with more diverse and fine-grained semantic categories. Experiments show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in open-vocabulary monocular occupancy prediction for indoor scenes, outperforming the previous state-of-the-art approach by 12.2 mIoU and 0.6 IoU on the Occ-ScanNet dataset, with consistent gains under new open-vocabulary evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[DeepRFTv3: Deep Residual Fourier Convolution for Unified Image Deblurring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12941)**  `arXiv:2610.12941`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xintian Mao, Haofei Song, Huaiyuan Wang, Jiansheng Wang, Qingli Li, Yan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image deblurring is a fundamental task in computer vision, yet existing methods mainly specialize in isolated scenarios. Consequently, the deblurring performance of these models drops significantly across scenarios with different cameras and image contents. This motivates the development of a unified motion deblurring framework that can handle diverse blur patterns. To this end, we construct a multi-source dataset and develop a data pruning and quality control pipeline. Then, to restore blur images with various motion blur kernels across different scenarios, we propose a unified motion deblurring framework via Fourier Convolution Learning. Existing deblurring networks usually rely on pixel-level mappings and overlook the convolutional nature of blur. Thus, the network mostly fits the image content itself rather than learning the convolution process, making it difficult to handle multi-source blur images. Instead of relying on spatial mapping, we perform blur-adaptive global filter learning in Fourier space, which avoids the limited spatial support of kernel estimation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our unified model effectively handles motion blurs across diverse sources and scenes, achieving competitive performance across both in-domain and OOD benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When Semantics Matter: Reliability-Aware Semantic-Rhythm Control for Co-Speech Gesture Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36685)**  `arXiv:2609.36685`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhirui Xing, Ming Meng, Kaige Li, Ziyi Xu, Zhaoxin Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Co-speech gesture generation aims to synthesize natural gestures that are both temporally synchronized with speech and semantically consistent with the spoken content. Although recent methods can generate rhythmically plausible motions, they often rely heavily on acoustic prosody while underutilizing textual semantics, especially when semantic annotations are incomplete, noisy, or unavailable. Consequently, the generated gestures may follow speech rhythm while failing to express the intended semantics. To address this problem, we propose a reliability-aware semantic-rhythm control framework for co-speech gesture generation. We first learn a discrete motion prior that represents continuous gestures in a compact and structured motion-code space. We then introduce a dual-branch semantic contribution estimation mechanism consisting of a full multimodal branch and an audio-only branch. Their distributional discrepancy provides a model-based proxy for text-induced predictive influence, which is interpreted jointly with segment-level semantic relevance. Based on this estimate, a controllable semantic-rhythm objective selectively strengthens semantic guidance in content-relevant segments while limiting unnecessary semantic intervention in rhythm-dominant segments. Furthermore, we treat background noise as an acoustic reliability condition and introduce noise-conditioned feature modulation together with benefit-regularized latent perturbation to improve generation robustness under realistic acoustic environments. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves a favorable balance among semantic expressiveness, rhythmic synchronization, motion diversity, and robustness, enabling reliable and controllable co-speech gesture generation.
  </details>

- **[Regularizing patch contributions across domain and category shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12766)**  `arXiv:2608.12766`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaorui Tan, Weimiao Yu, Xi Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual classification under domain and category shifts requires identifying which image regions provide transferable evidence. Domain alignment alone can retain context that is stable across source domains yet redundant for prediction, while new categories may require different local distinctions. We propose PatchGen to learn image-conditioned soft patch weights and regularize their contributions to the task representation. Low-score suppression acts directly on the weights, while selected-confidence and class-conditional compactness objectives shape the aggregated features using available labels or host-generated pseudo-labels. An oracle predictive-subset analysis characterizes information preservation, complexity upper bounds, and sensitivity to selection error under explicit assumptions. Experiments on natural and histopathological image benchmarks across all three shift settings show that PatchGen improves average performance over matched-backbone baselines in most evaluated configurations, enhances generalization to unknown classes, and remains competitive with vision-language methods without text supervision.
  </details>

- **[Out of Sight, Still in Mind: Token Compression for Omni-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21179)**  `arXiv:2607.21179`  `cs.CV`  
  _Suho Yoo, Youngjoon Jang, Hyebin Cho, Joon Son Chung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The goal of this paper is to reduce the input token cost of Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) at inference time. Omni-LLMs reason jointly over audio, video and text, but the cost of the three streams is highly unbalanced: visual tokens account for the vast majority of the input, and are highly redundant. In this paper, we propose ReMo, a training-free framework that compresses visual tokens by redistributing their information across modalities: a visual token is kept only if its information appears nowhere else. ReMo achieves this in two ways: (i) it aligns audio and video in a shared space via closed-form calibration, and removes visual tokens already explained by the audio or by other visual tokens; and (ii) it replaces object-level visual tokens, identified by a lightweight object detector, with compact text proxies, short descriptions of each object and its location, conveying the same content in far fewer tokens. On Qwen2.5-Omni at two model scales, ReMo removes 54% of the input tokens with comparable accuracy. It performs comparably to the full-token model, reaching 101.2% and 101.4% of its average accuracy over five audio-visual benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[CogCanvas: A Benchmark for Evaluating Multi-Subject Reference-Based Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15867)**  `arXiv:2606.15867`  `cs.CV`  
  _Long-Bao Nguyen, Quang-Khai Le, Tam V. Nguyen, Minh-Triet Tran, Trung-Nghia Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-subject reference-based image generation requires jointly preserving multiple human identities, binding per-person objects and fashion items, and respecting a specified background scene, a regime where current diffusion models remain brittle. Existing benchmarks evaluate only one axis at a time and none jointly captures multi-identity composition with human-object interaction, background grounding, and spatial plausibility. We introduce CogCanvas, a benchmark of 1,952 curated reference images spanning 100 celebrity identities, 115 distinctive objects and fashion items, and 29 real-world background scenes including landmarks, from which we construct 1,361 compositional prompts covering 2-5 person group sizes. The curation pipeline combines DINOv2-based deduplication, two-stage aesthetic filtering, and automated derivation of structured interaction and position graphs that serve as ground-truth supervision. CogCanvas supports three tasks, reference-based multi-human-object generation (primary), text-to-image compositional generation, and reference retrieval, under a unified six-axis evaluation protocol. We introduce two metrics tailored to the multi-reference setting: BG-Sim, which scores background fidelity on SAM 3-masked regions via DINOv3 feature similarity, and Attr-VQA, which uses a multimodal LLM to verify per-subject attribute binding and inter-person interactions against the structured graphs. Benchmarking five SOTA methods reveals that every model degrades substantially as group size grows from 2 to 5, with near-complete failure on object/fashion binding beyond three subjects.
  </details>

- **[VZCrash: A Large-Scale IMU Dataset of Ego-Vehicle Crashes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06074)**  `arXiv:2606.06074`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tommaso Bianconcini, Henrique Piñeiro Monteagudo, Aurel Pjetri, Tomaso Trinci, Leonardo Taccari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce VZCrash, the largest publicly available dataset of real-world vehicle collision data featuring Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU) telemetry. The dataset contains more than 31,000 validated crashes and 158,000 negative samples, including hard cases and distractors. Each sample includes acceleration and angular velocity at 100 Hz, and GPS speed at 1 Hz. Events in VZCrash were captured by devices installed on a fleet of 73,010 commercial vehicles of different sizes driving in the United States over the span of several years. We also present an extensive experimental study enabled by the volume of the dataset. We first benchmark several different approaches, from a simple threshold-based heuristic to state-of-the-art deep learning models. Then, we present an experiment demonstrating the importance of scaling data to train high-quality crash detection models, and we show that scale is especially important when these models need to be deployed into a real-world environment.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Video Reasoning in Physical Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21873)**  `arXiv:2604.21873`  `cs.CV`  
  _Alibay Osmanli, Zixu Cheng, Shaogang Gong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical video understanding requires more than naming an event correctly. A model can answer a question about pouring, sliding, or collision from textual regularities while still failing to localize the event in time or space. We introduce a grounded benchmark for physical video understanding that extends the what--when--where evaluation structure of V-STaR to four video sources, six physics domains, three prompt families (physics, vstar_like, and neutral_rstr), and four input conditions (original, shuffled, ablated, and frame-masked). The benchmark contains 1,560 base video clips from SSV2, YouCook2, HoloAssist, and Roundabout-TAU. Each clip is first converted into a shared grounded event record, and the three query families are derived from that record. Temporal and spatial targets are shared across prompt families, while the non-physics families use deterministic family-appropriate semantic a_what targets derived from the same record. Across models and prompt families, physics remains the strongest regime overall, vstar_like is the clearest non-physics semantic comparison, and neutral_rstr behaves as a harder templated control. Prompt-family robustness is selective rather than universal, perturbation gains cluster in weak original cases, and spatial grounding is the weakest across settings. These results suggest that video Q&A reasoning benchmarks shall report physically grounded, prompt-aware, and perturbation-aware diagnostics alongside aggregate accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CAM3DNet: Comprehensively mining the multi-scale features for 3D Object Detection with Multi-View Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17024)**  `arXiv:2604.17024`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingxi Pang, Dingheng Wang, Zekun Li, Zhenping Sun, Bo Wang, Zhihang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-based 3D object detection methods using multi-view images often struggle to efficiently leverage dynamic multi-scale information, e.g., the relationship between the object features and the geometric of the queries are not sufficiently learned, directly exploring the multi-scale spatiotemporal features will pay too many costs. To address these challenges, we propose CAM3DNet, a novel sparse query-based framework which combines three new modules, composite query (CQ), adaptive self-attention (ASA), and multi-scale hybrid sampling (MSHS). First, the core idea in the CQ module is a multi-scale projection strategy to transform 2D queries into 3D space. Second, the ASA module learns the interactions between the spatiotemporal multi-scale queries. Third, the MSHS module uses the deformable attention mechanism to sample multi-scale object information by considering multi-scales queries, pyramid feature maps, and 2D-camera prior knowledge. The entire model employs a backbone network and a feature pyramid network (FPN) as the encoder, then introduces a YOLOX and a DepthNet as a ROI\_Head to produce CQ, and repeatedly utilizes ASA and MSHS as the decoder to gain detection features. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes, Waymo, and Argoverse benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our CAM3DNet, and most existing camera-based 3D object detection methods are outperformed. Besides, we make comprehensive ablation studies to check the individual effect of CQ, ASA, and MSHS, as well as their cost of space and computation complexity.
  </details>

- **[Traffic Sign Recognition in Autonomous Driving: Dataset, Benchmark, and Field Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23034)**  `arXiv:2603.23034`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guoyang Zhao, Weiqing Qi, Kai Zhang, Chenguang Zhang, Zeying Gong, Zhihai Bi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic Sign Recognition (TSR) is a core perception capability for autonomous driving, where robustness to cross-region variation, long-tailed categories, and semantic ambiguity is essential for reliable real-world deployment. Despite steady progress in recognition accuracy, existing traffic sign datasets and benchmarks offer limited diagnostic insight into how different modeling paradigms behave under these practical challenges. We present TS-1M, a large-scale and globally diverse traffic sign dataset comprising over one million real-world images across 454 standardized categories, together with a diagnostic benchmark designed to analyze model capability boundaries. Beyond standard train-test evaluation, we provide a suite of challenge-oriented settings, including cross-region recognition, rare-class identification, low-clarity robustness, and semantic text understanding, enabling systematic and fine-grained assessment of modern TSR models. Using TS-1M, we conduct a unified benchmark across three representative learning paradigms: classical supervised models, self-supervised pretrained models, and multimodal vision-language models (VLMs). Our analysis reveals consistent paradigm-dependent behaviors, showing that semantic alignment is a key factor for cross-region generalization and rare-category recognition, while purely visual models remain sensitive to appearance shift and data imbalance. Finally, we validate the practical relevance of TS-1M through real-scene autonomous driving experiments, where traffic sign recognition is integrated with semantic reasoning and spatial localization to support map-level decision constraints. Overall, TS-1M establishes a reference-level diagnostic benchmark for TSR and provides principled insights into robust and semantic-aware traffic sign perception. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ParallelVLM: Lossless Video-LLM Acceleration with Visual Alignment Aware Parallel Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19610)**  `arXiv:2603.19610`  `cs.CV`  
  _Quan Kong, Yuhao Shen, Yicheng Ji, Huan Li, Cong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although current Video-LLMs achieve impressive performance in video understanding tasks, their autoregressive decoding efficiency remains constrained by the massive number of video tokens. Visual token pruning can partially ease this bottleneck, yet existing approaches still suffer from information loss and yield only modest acceleration in decoding. In this paper, we propose ParallelVLM, a training-free draft-then-verify speculative decoding framework that overcomes both mutual waiting and limited speedup-ratio problems between draft and target models in long-video settings. ParallelVLM features two parallelized stages that maximize hardware utilization and incorporate an Unbiased Verifier-Guided Pruning strategy to better align the draft and target models by eliminating the positional bias in attention-guided pruning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ParallelVLM effectively expands the draft window by $1.6\sim1.8\times$ with high accepted lengths, and accelerates various video understanding benchmarks by 3.36$\times$ on LLaVA-Onevision-72B and 2.42$\times$ on Qwen2.5-VL-32B compared with vanilla autoregressive decoding.
  </details>

- **[NEGATE: Constrained Semantic Guidance for Linguistic Negation in Text-to-Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06533)**  `arXiv:2603.06533`  `cs.CV`  
  _Taewon Kang, Ming C. Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Negation is a fundamental linguistic operator, yet it remains inadequately modeled in vision-language generation systems. In this work, we present a formal treatment of linguistic negation in vision-language models by modeling it as a structured feasibility constraint on semantic guidance within diffusion dynamics. Rather than introducing heuristics or retraining model parameters, we reinterpret classifier-free guidance as defining a semantic update direction and enforce negation by projecting the update onto a convex constraint set derived from linguistic structure. This formulation provides a unified framework for handling diverse negation phenomena, including object absence, graded non-inversion semantics, multi-negation composition, and scope-sensitive disambiguation. Our approach is training-free, compatible with pretrained diffusion backbones, and defined on the classifier-free guidance update, which lets it operate over temporally evolving video trajectories. In addition, we introduce a structured negation-centric benchmark suite that isolates distinct linguistic failure modes in generative systems, to further research in this area. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves robust negation compliance while preserving visual fidelity and structural coherence, moving the treatment of linguistic negation in generative vision-language models beyond representation-level evaluation.
  </details>

- **[RTime-QA: A Benchmark for Atomic Temporal Event Understanding in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19125)**  `arXiv:2505.19125`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuqi Liu, Qin Jin, Tianyuan Qu, Xuan Liu, Yang Du, Bei Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) perform well on video benchmarks, yet many video questions can be answered from a single frame or from language priors, which leaves open whether these models perceive how an event unfolds in time. We introduce \rtqa, a benchmark for atomic temporal event understanding built on time-reversed question pairs: each video is accompanied by its reversal, and each question is asked on both videos with identical text and options but different correct answers, so that a deterministic model that ignores the order of the frames cannot answer both. \rtqa{} comprises 8,420 questions of five types on 1,278 videos, 639 videos and their reversals. The questions are derived from event annotations that a proposer model writes, a reviewer model checks and human annotators verify, and three subsets target fast evaluation (\rtqatiny), questions that are hard to answer from the first and last frames (\rtqacore) and two consecutive events (\rtqacomp). All 20 evaluated models are less accurate on reversed videos. On \rtqafull, proprietary models answer both questions of 90.6--94.4\% of the pairs and the strongest open-source model of 66.8\%; on \rtqacore, the proprietary models drop to 55.6--64.5\%, and shuffling the frames reduces the paired accuracy of the tested open-source models to 3.4--7.4\%. We further build \rtqatrain{} with the same pipeline, without human verification, and train three open-source MLLMs on it with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (DAPO) under matched settings: both raise paired accuracy on \rtqafull, SFT by 20.7--27.4 points and DAPO by 5.9--22.9 points. For Qwen3.5-9B-Thinking, the training data also help on two other temporal benchmarks: SFT and DAPO raise the Vinoground text score by 5.8 and 3.8 points, respectively, and DAPO raises the TVBench macro accuracy by 4.6 points.
  </details>

- **[MInfoVisQA: A Multilingual Benchmark for Information-Rich Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21644)**  `arXiv:2611.21644`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Changjiang Jiang, Jiang Zhou, Xinbin Yuan, Fan Zhang, Tao Feng, Zhuohan Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) excel in vision-language tasks; yet, whether they maintain consistent understanding of information-rich visuals (InfoVis) when the surface language changes remains unclear. To quantify this capability, we introduce MInfoVisQA, a parallel benchmark with 8,960 QA configurations derived from 128 seed instances across 24 languages. We construct seed-matched, language-parallel visuals and QA pairs by disentangling numerical-structural data from text and programmatically synchronizing translated labels. We evaluate 25 MLLMs under two settings: Language-Aligned QA (LQA, matching visual-text languages) and Cross-Language QA (XQA, fixed QA language with varying visual languages). The results show that aggregate accuracy masks substantial robustness gaps: two models with comparable mean LQA accuracy (80.4\%) exhibit performance spreads of 7.8 and 20.3 percentage points across languages. Moreover, all 25 models score lower with non-English visuals under fixed English QA, underscoring the need for cross-language sensitivity metrics.
  </details>

- **[TableVista: Benchmarking Multimodal Table Reasoning under Visual and Structural Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05955)**  `arXiv:2605.05955`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Zheyuan Yang, Liqiang Shang, Junjie Chen, Xun Yang, Chenglong Xu, Bo Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce TableVista, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating foundation models in multimodal table reasoning under visual and structural complexity. TableVista consists of 3,000 high-quality table reasoning problems, where each instance is expanded into 10 distinct visual variants through our multi-style rendering and transformation pipeline. This process encompasses diverse scenario styles, robustness perturbations, and vision-only configurations, culminating in 30,000 multimodal samples for a multi-dimensional evaluation. We conduct an extensive evaluation of 29 state-of-the-art open-source and proprietary foundation models on TableVista. Through comprehensive quantitative and qualitative analysis, we find that while evaluated models remain largely stable across diverse rendering styles, they exhibit pronounced performance degradation on complex structural layouts and vision-only settings, revealing that current models struggle to maintain reasoning consistency when structural complexity combines with visually integrated presentations. These findings highlight critical gaps in current multimodal capabilities, providing insights for advancing more robust and reliable table understanding models.
  </details>

- **[Runtime Verification of Multiple Natural Language Criteria for Agent Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23074)**  `arXiv:2611.23074`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Silviu Pitis, Parand A. Alamdari, Jessica Tang, Toryn Q. Klassen, Sheila A. McIlraith_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are governed by hundreds of natural language criteria, yet existing verifiers cannot efficiently evaluate an agent's output against a large set of such criteria and report which are satisfied, violated, or inapplicable. We introduce the VFM, a tree-attention verifier that encodes the shared context and target text, and scores N natural language criteria in parallel, returning independent per-criterion ternary verdicts. The VFM is pretrained on CriteriaBank, a corpus of 394K (context, target, criterion, verdict) tuples spanning over 321K distinct criterion strings. A finetuned VFM-4B outperforms GPT-5.4 on a synthetic governance benchmark. On the DynaBench multi-criterion benchmark, it outperforms DynaGuard-4B on trace-level failure detection, and its per-criterion verdicts localize the violated rule and support focused escalation to a larger chain-of-thought judge. We show that CriteriaBank pretraining improves cross-domain transfer, particularly in the low-data regime, while matching a strong direct-finetuning control at full data. On a single H100, VFM-4B evaluates 1,000 criteria against a 4K-token context in under 2s at <11 GB VRAM, making it suitable for runtime verification.
  </details>

- **[Disambiguating Moral Circuits and Moral Features in Language Models: Moral content, normative judgment or cue words? Evidence from 14 models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21705)**  `arXiv:2611.21705`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Davide Liga, Liuwen Yu, Matteo Magnini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpretability methods can locate the attention heads and sparse autoencoder (SAE) features that change the answer a language model gives to a moral prompt, e.g., whether "Stealing medicine for a dying child is morally" continues with "justified" or "wrong". Such a component could respond to moral content, to the judgment of conduct by any norm, or to the word "morally" in the prompt. We built 227 minimal pairs of short phrases, validated by five annotators, whose two versions differ in a moral standard, in another kind of norm (prudence, etiquette, aesthetics or evidence) or in a descriptive quality, and showed them to 14 open-weight models (Llama, Gemma and Qwen, 1B to 14B, base and instruction-tuned), with and without the word "morally". In every model, the heads that change the moral answer belong to an evaluation circuit the models use for every kind of normative judgment we tested: these heads carry the same share of the effect for moral as for other normative pairs (pooled ratio 1.00, 97.5% confidence interval [0.92, 1.07], with the word; 0.90, [0.84, 0.96], without it), and the models need them as much for both. The models need these heads also when nothing in the prompt asks for a judgment, and on 400 scenarios of the ETHICS benchmark, where removing them takes away 37 to 39% of the difference between acceptable and unacceptable scenarios, against 1 to 3% for ten random heads. The SAE features we located respond mostly to the word "morally" rather than to what the phrase is about. The models represent the category of a pair (the standard the annotators assigned to it): it can be read from their hidden states, and, in the eight models we tested, removing the direction that carries it weakens the moral verdict by a few percent, and more than the other verdicts in seven of them. We release the pairs, the ratings, the code, the trained SAE dictionaries and the result files.
  </details>

- **[One Frozen Simulator Is Not Enough: Simulator Collapse in Multi-Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12253)**  `arXiv:2608.12253`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Simon Yu, Nicholas Tomlin, Marwa Abdulhai, Ximing Lu, Derek Chong, Abe Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) for human-AI interaction usually trains the agent against a single large language model (LLM) that simulates the user. We show that agents trained this way can systematically fail to generalize, and we trace the failure to simulator collapse: the simulator LLM is mode-collapsed, and a policy trained against it learns narrow strategies that exploit the simulator's mode behavior; these strategies transfer poorly to unseen simulators and to real users. We formalize the collapse and propose two solutions. Verbalized Sampling, applied at inference time, samples each simulator reply from a verbalized response distribution. Co-Training, applied during training similar to self-play, co-evolves the simulator as the policy, so the policy cannot exploit the mode behavior. A population variant samples the simulator for each rollout from a pool of recent checkpoints. We validate both solutions on three benchmarks: Persuasion for Good, tau^2-bench, and CooperBench. Verbalized Sampling improves held-out success by up to 9% over single-simulator RL, and Co-Training pushes gains further to 14%; the human study shows similar gain on real users. Both solutions also preserve the policy diversity that collapses under single-simulator RL. Overall, our findings on simulator collapse point to diverse simulators as a requirement for RL agents that serve real users. We release SCOPE, an open-source framework for Population Co-Training in multi-agent RL.
  </details>

- **[Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18397)**  `arXiv:2611.18397`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.
  </details>

- **[StitchRAG: Constructing Compact and Evidence-Preserving Contexts for RAG with Diffusion-Based Stitching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22766)**  `arXiv:2611.22766`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Minsang Kim, Junpyo Lim, Heeje Cho, Seungjun Baek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves factuality by grounding large language models (LLMs) in external evidence. However, long retrieved contexts incur computational overhead and can even hurt performance due to noisy or irrelevant content. We propose \textsc{StitchRAG}, a framework for constructing compact context in RAG. \textsc{StitchRAG} builds a faithful, information-dense context while dynamically compressing the retrieved passages. We adapt the size reduction ratio to each passage by formulating a utility maximization trading-off the retained information and computation costs. We then extract answer candidates and query-related keywords from passages as anchors and collect passage sentences containing anchors under the derived size constraint. Finally, we connect or stitch the collected sentences together using text diffusion to form a coherent and natural context. Experiments on five open-domain QA benchmarks show that \textsc{StitchRAG} consistently outperforms diverse multi-step and compression baselines. Extensive ablations show the effectiveness of dynamic size allocation, anchor preservation, and diffusion-based stitching.
  </details>

- **[Cross-cultural Adaptation of Recipes for Low-Resource Languages: A Benchmark and Multilingual Culinary Lexicon for Hausa, Yorùbá, and Igbo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22708)**  `arXiv:2611.22708`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Nuhu Ibrahim, Maryam Abdulazeez, Fatsuma Jauro, Salisu Aliyu, Robert Stevens, Riza Batista-Navarro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multilingual large language models (LLMs) have improved machine translation, yet culturally adaptive translation for low-resource languages (LRLs) remains underexplored. This is particularly challenging for recipes, where ingredients, cooking tools, measurements, and procedures may require adaptation to the target cultural context. In this work, we introduce ReciLoReL, a benchmark of 600 expert-translated and culturally adapted recipes across Yorùbá, Hausa, and Igbo (three of the most prominent languages in Nigeria), and ReciLex, a multilingual culinary lexicon containing approximately 15,000 translated entities across ten recipe entity categories, including ingredients, tools, chef actions, state of tools, and durations. We evaluate state-of-the-art multilingual LLMs for English-to-LRL recipe translation under zero-shot and few-shot settings and investigate whether augmenting LLMs with ReciLex improves culturally adaptive recipe translation without requiring large target-language recipe corpora for training or fine-tuning. Beyond conventional reference-based translation evaluation using BLEU, chrF, and TER, we introduce five criteria for evaluating cultural adaptation, adapted from established cross-cultural adaptation methods. Across the selected LLMs and languages, ReciLex improves average BLEU from 13.57 to 14.51 and chrF from 37.89 to 38.74, while reducing TER from 77.14 to 75.12. Human evaluation further shows improvements ranging from 0.11 to 0.36 points across the five cultural adaptation evaluation criteria on a five-point scale. Overall, our work provides new resources, evaluation criteria, and empirical evaluation for studying culturally adaptive recipe translation in low-resource settings.
  </details>

- **[JET: Just-Enough Exploration Tuning for Efficient Tool-Calling Agents in Repository-Level Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22539)**  `arXiv:2611.22539`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yoonho Choi, Taehyeon Han, Seonghyeon Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In repository-level question answering (QA), an agent explores a code repository with tools and should answer a question once enough information is gathered. Nevertheless, we find that LLM agents often continue to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer. We call this phenomenon over-exploration. Moreover, even though existing methods improve task performance through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories, SFT also propagates the over-exploration in the trajectories to the agent. To mitigate over-exploration, we propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that trains the agent to answer once the gathered information is sufficient. JET truncates each training trajectory at the step where the gathered information becomes sufficient, inserts a rationale that explains why the agent can answer at that step, and fine-tunes the agent with a higher loss weight on the rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models demonstrate that JET reduces tool calls by 11 to 33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving the score. On expert trajectories, JET makes 18 to 28% fewer tool calls than SFT while retaining most of the score gain of SFT. In both cases, JET also lowers the inference cost.
  </details>

- **[EGS: A Complementary Safety Framework for Educational LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22203)**  `arXiv:2611.22203`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zexuan Fei, Zhengnan Guo, Yiming Luo, Kaiyi Huang, Aimin Zhou, Bo Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) offer strong potential for personalized education, but adolescent-facing deployment raises risks of toxic, biased, misleading, or developmentally inappropriate content. We argue that educational safety should move beyond binary refusal: student queries are often ambiguous or curiosity-driven, requiring contextual guidance rather than simple blocking. We introduce the Educational Guardrail System (EGS), a lightweight, model-agnostic runtime framework that complements intrinsic alignment through query moderation, scenario-aware pedagogical guidance, and response moderation. EGS triages inputs by risk level, redirects gray-zone queries with category-specific educational interventions, and audits outputs before delivery. Experiments across general and education-specific benchmarks show that EGS improves moderation performance, refusal quality, and jailbreak robustness across diverse LLMs. These findings suggest that defense-in-depth guardrails can strengthen safety while preserving the pedagogical utility required in educational LLMs. Our code and model weights will be publicly available to support community adoption.
  </details>

- **[GauntletScore: Auditable Fact-Checking That Matches the Best Published Checkers on LLM-AggreFact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22027)**  `arXiv:2611.22027`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ernie Hobbs, Jim Hinson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Checking whether a generated sentence is supported by its source document is a standard safeguard for AI-written text, and the strongest published checkers are single models. We describe GauntletScore, an evidence engine whose few rules and thresholds were set on the benchmark's development split. It puts typed questions to independent readers from different providers and records each answer as a measurement in an evidence ledger. It combines the measurements with a fixed Bayesian rule that counts a contradiction only when two readers agree. On the full LLM-AggreFact test split (29,320 claims across 11 datasets), following the leaderboard's approach, GauntletScore achieves 79.51 balanced accuracy, above every average published on the leaderboard as of 15 November 2026. That places it level with the best of them, nimble-v4-max (79.45 from its published dataset scores; the engine's 95% interval, 78.47 to 80.53, contains it). On the same claims it beats two of its three readers used alone (Readers A and B) by 1.66 and 2.17 points (95% intervals 0.80 to 2.53 and 1.29 to 3.06). Without AggreFact-CNN, described below, the leads are 0.90 and 1.32 and both intervals stay above zero. The engine also records the evidence behind every verdict, which a single model does not. Its largest margin is on full-length news articles checked against multi-sentence summaries (AggreFact-CNN), a format that resembles much regulated writing. There it scores 76.45, the best on the leaderboard, against 70.1 for the next best and 63.9 to 67.8 for the six systems with the highest averages. The test split was run once, on an engine frozen before the first test claim, under a stopping plan committed in advance. We release per-claim predictions and the scoring script.
  </details>

- **[DiagTrace: An Interactive Benchmark for Clinical Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22009)**  `arXiv:2611.22009`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yufan Wang, Rui Yang, Yi Lin, Chunhua Weng, Yifan Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on medical question answering and diagnostic reasoning benchmarks. However, existing evaluations typically provide all patient information upfront and assess only the final diagnosis or rationale. This setup ignores the need to acquire diagnostic evidence incrementally from incomplete information in real-world diagnosis. To bridge this gap, we introduce DiagTrace, a source-grounded benchmark for evaluating whether LLMs can reconstruct the documented diagnostic trajectory through interactive information seeking. Each benchmark instance is built from a PubMed Central case report and includes an initial presentation, an event bank, a clinical course, and diagnostic references. DiagTrace contains 510 cases across eight specialties, with 225 used to evaluate four LLMs on clinical-course alignment, interaction quality, and efficiency. Results reveal trade-offs in coverage, source-consistent ordering, and turn use.
  </details>

- **[TUSI: A Citation-Grounded Graph-Based Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21823)**  `arXiv:2611.21823`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Marzieh Karimi, Mehran Safayani, Alireza Basiri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in Persian law remains challenging because benchmarks lack realism, grounding, and coverage of legal reasoning. Existing resources are either synthetically generated from statutory texts, lacking natural information needs, or derived from informal legal question--answer sources without explicit linkage to authoritative legal documents, making them unsuitable for rigorous evaluation of grounded legal reasoning. To address this gap, we introduce TUSI-Bench, a structured Persian legal benchmark designed for the evaluation of RAG-based systems. TUSI-Bench is built upon a multi-layered legal knowledge framework containing 7,261 legal articles and integrating codified statutes, legal commentaries, doctrinal writings, and Supreme Court unification rulings. We also construct a legal knowledge graph linking articles and concepts for structure-aware retrieval. Based on this foundation, TUSI-Bench provides two complementary evaluation tasks: (i) TUSI-QA, an open-ended question answering benchmark containing 1,999 questions that requires grounded responses with explicit legal citations, and (ii) TUSI-Bar, a multiple-choice benchmark containing approximately 500 four-option questions derived from Iranian Bar examination questions. Furthermore, we propose a graph-based retrieval baseline that leverages relational structure within the legal knowledge graph. Experimental results demonstrate that incorporating graph-based relations improves retrieval recall by up to 8 percentage points compared to standard baselines, highlighting the importance of structured legal representations for RAG systems. The proposed benchmark provides a standardized resource for evaluating grounded legal reasoning in Persian and enables future research on structured legal intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Semantic Insensitivity in Financial Sentiment Analysis: Implications for Sustainable Financial Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21653)**  `arXiv:2611.21653`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Gabriela Cioboata, Teodor-Stefan Bildea_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial sentiment analysis is critical for automated trading, portfolio management, and risk assessment, heavily relying on models like FinBERT. Despite high benchmark accuracy, these models remain vulnerable to subtle adversarial text modifications, threatening the integrity of algorithmic decision-making. In this paper, we propose Agent-driven Semantic Inversion (ASI), a novel adversarial attack framework targeting financial sentiment models through domain-specific semantic manipulation. Utilizing an LLM-powered multi-agent pipeline, our approach identifies eligible financial news sentences and applies targeted lexical substitutions. We empirically evaluate ASI against FinBERT, one of the most widely deployed financial sentiment models. Our results reveal a striking vulnerability: the attack successfully deceived FinBERT in 90.85% of eligible cases, with the model frequently maintaining its original sentiment prediction despite the fundamental inversion of financial performance indicators. Through qualitative analysis and token-level investigation, we demonstrate that FinBERT critically over-relies on contextual keywords and syntactic patterns rather than true logical comprehension of financial semantics. Our findings highlight the urgent need for logic-aware, robust financial NLP models to ensure the reliability of automated decision-making systems.
  </details>

- **[RPMem: Learning Long-Term Recurrent Parametric Memory Across Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23466)**  `arXiv:2609.23466`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Fanyu Zhao, Ruike Cao, Liang Dong, Fugen Yao, Jian Xu, Guanjun Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-running LLM agents require memory that persists and evolves across sessions. Text-based memory retrieves and reconstructs past interactions at every query, making long-horizon performance increasingly dependent on retrieval quality and contextual reasoning as histories grow. Parametric memory encodes experience directly into model computation, but existing approaches provide limited support for cross-session memory evolution. Their coupling to a specific backbone further restricts memory reuse after model replacement. We introduce RPMem, a two-stage architecture that compiles each session into a model-independent latent memory through forward computation and selectively integrates it with retained memory via a task-trained recurrent gate. The consolidated memory is then mapped to backbone-specific low-rank adaptation (LoRA) parameters, allowing the encoding capability to transfer when the backbone is replaced. Evaluation across three long-term memory benchmarks and five diverse backbones demonstrates broad generalization with near-constant update cost and memory footprint. With Qwen3-8B on PERMA, RPMem reaches 85.52%, outperforming the strongest parametric and text-based baselines by 5.32 and 12.98 percentage points, respectively. Ablations validate the complementary roles of session compilation and cross-session consolidation, while dynamics analyses reveal that the gate acquires task-specific memory integration strategies. These results establish RPMem as a lifecycle-independent parametric memory framework that maintains evolving cross-session memory that remains reusable across backbone replacements. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Distributional Alignment as a Criterion for Designing Task Vectors in In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20730)**  `arXiv:2605.20730`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jihoon Kwon, Jiwon Choi, Jy-yong Sohn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning (ICL) allows large language models (LLMs) to adapt to new tasks through demonstrations, yet it suffers from escalating inference costs as context length increases. While task vectors offer a promising alternative by compressing demonstrations into compact hidden-state representations, their quality has been evaluated only through downstream task accuracy. This indirect criterion provides limited insight into how to design more effective task vector extraction methods. In this paper, we posit that inference using task vectors should align their predictive distribution with that of ICL. To quantify this, we introduce $d_{\text{NTP}}$, a metric that measures the discrepancy in next-token probabilities between task vector-based and ICL-based inference. Our empirical analysis reveals that $d_{\text{NTP}}$ serves as a performance proxy, exhibiting a strong negative correlation with downstream accuracy. Motivated by this, we develop Linear Task Vector (LTV), a method designed to minimize $d_{\text{NTP}}$ via a closed-form linear mapping that estimates demonstration effects through regression. Across eight classification benchmarks and five LLMs, LTV consistently outperforms existing task vector baselines, improving average accuracy by 9.2\% while reducing inference latency. We further show that LTV outperforms the baselines on regression tasks. Moreover, we investigate the transferability of LTV across different model scales; an aspect that has remained nascent in task vector research. Specifically, we empirically show that task vectors from a larger model can enhance a smaller model's performance by 6.4\%, suggesting a new utility for extracted task representations.
  </details>

- **[TPHC: Improving Hyper-Connections with Tensor-Parallel Grouped Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23026)**  `arXiv:2611.23026`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinying Hu, You Wu, Haoyi Wu, Jianchao Tan, Kefeng Zhang, Yuchen Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyper-Connections (HC) expand the residual stream of a Transformer into $N$ parallel streams, and manifold-constrained HC (mHC) stabilizes them at scale. However, mHC benefits little from more than four streams, partly because each sublayer writes a single output to all streams, limiting how differently they can be updated. In this work, we propose TPHC, which integrates tensor-parallel (TP) shards with HC streams to promote greater independence across streams. Specifically, we route a separate mixture of the residual streams to each TP shard and keep the individual shard outputs separate during write-back, so each stream can receive a different mixture of those outputs. This design makes the residual-stream updates more expressive without adding parameters or FLOPs to the attention or FFN sublayers. With native support for stream sharding under TP, TPHC requires only 9% more training time than mHC on an 8B model when both use four streams. Increasing the number of streams from four to sixteen raises its training time by only 19%. On a 1.1B model trained on 100B tokens, TPHC reduces validation loss by 0.7-1.0% and raises average zero-shot accuracy across six commonsense reasoning benchmarks by 0.3-0.7 percentage points relative to mHC at matched stream counts $N\in\{4,8,16\}$. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Shared Word: Evaluating Language Models on Dutch Word Puzzles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22935)**  `arXiv:2611.22935`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rik van Noord, Maurice Voors_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many LLM benchmarks for languages other than English are translated, multiple-choice, or difficult to score automatically. We introduce The Shared Word (TSW-NL), a Dutch benchmark built around four core principles: it is language-specific, open-vocabulary, human-curated, and deterministically scored. Each puzzle gives three clues that describe different senses or uses of the same Dutch word. The clues are individually ambiguous, requiring the solver to combine evidence across multiple clues. We construct the benchmark from Dutch Wikipedia disambiguation pages, followed by automatic filtering and extensive manual curation. The final dataset contains 222 instances, with three independent human answers per test item. The task is clearly challenging: scores in our human study range from 49.5% to 61.0% and the highest-scoring open-weight model we tested reaches only 39.3%. Many others even score below 20%. At the same time, closed-source models show great progress: GPT-4.1 (April 2025) scores 59.2%, whereas GPT-5.6 Sol with max reasoning scores 88.8%. We believe this task provides a useful addition to Dutch model evaluation, plus a clear recipe that can be applied to construct a similar benchmark for other languages.
  </details>

- **[Visibility Graph Representations of Word Embeddings for Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22685)**  `arXiv:2611.22685`  `cs.CL`  
  _Elif Dorukbaşı, İlker Türker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text classifiers depend on representations that expose the distinctions between classes in a form the learning model can use, and a restricted vocabulary changes which representation is most effective. This paper studies natural visibility graphs as an intermediate representation for news topic classification under controlled vocabulary budgets. Word embeddings are averaged into a fixed-length document vector, the vector is read as an ordered numerical series, and its natural visibility graph is passed to a classifier either as an ordered node-degree vector or as an adjacency matrix. On the four-class AG News benchmark, chi-square selection retains 100 to 1,500 words, and the graph inputs are compared with direct embedding-based representations from continuous bag-of-words, skip-gram, GloVe, FastText and BERT. With a fully connected classifier, the degree representation is the most accurate configuration from 700 selected words onward; it reaches its highest accuracy of 91.24% at 1,000 words and exceeds the BERT-based baseline by 0.29 percentage points at 1,500 words. In a separate convolutional comparison, the adjacency input reaches 86.16% at 1,000 words, against 83.12% for the strongest static embedding. The results indicate that visibility-derived features can be competitive once the selected vocabulary provides adequate coverage. We discuss the roles of graph compression, coordinate ordering and classifier choice, and the controls needed to establish the reliability of the small differences observed.
  </details>

- **[AfriNORM: An Evaluation Benchmark for Normative Reasoning of LLMs Across African Cultures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22580)**  `arXiv:2611.22580`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ahmad Samuel Gali, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Idris Abdulmumin, Seid Muhie Yimam, Shamsuddeen Hassan Muhammad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed across various sectors. However, current evaluation benchmarks are insufficient to assess the applicability of these models in local African contexts, as most focus on linguistic fluency (grammar/translation) rather than cultural competence. The few existing benchmarks that measure general knowledge or social conventions do not capture the African deontic modality, i.e., the strict norms and taboos that govern diverse social interactions. In this paper, we introduce AfriNORM, a scenario-based, multiple-choice question dataset designed to measure and diagnose the normative reasoning limitations of LLMs when applied to African sociocultural contexts. We introduce two metrics: a Universal Commonsense Bias Score (UCBS) to quantify commonsense bias in LLM and a Distractor Bias Score (DBS) to quantify an LLM's limited understanding of African cultural norms. We also conduct a case study on the Yorùbá language, identifying confusion gaps in two closed-source LLMs, "Gemini 2.0 Flash" and "GPT-5", and one open-source LLM, "Llama 3.1 8B Instruct" when prompted in English versus Yorùbá. While their accuracy in English is high, their performance in Yorùbá is much lower, attributable to cultural confusion rather than simple bias, indicating that the models exhibit semantic confusion when reasoning about norms in Yorùbá.
  </details>

- **[MM-ReportBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Multimodal Report Generation from Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22514)**  `arXiv:2611.22514`  `cs.CL`  
  _Teng Lin, Nan Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The ability to generate multimodal reports integrating fluent narratives with faithful visualizations from tabular data, has become a cornerstone capability for modern vision-language models in practical domains such as finance, healthcare, and policy making. However, existing benchmarks are limited by single-modality focus, simplistic metrics, novelty evaluation, and visualization quality. We address these limitations with MM-ReportBench, a multimodal report evaluation benchmark covering 386 tasks across 185 real-world tables. Each task is paired with an expert-curated reference report featuring standardized chapter structures and verifiable key insight points, constructed through a hybrid pipeline of multimodal reports generation based on Monte Carlo Tree Search and rigorous human quality control. For robust evaluation, we design a dual-judge evaluation framework: Text Judge Model assesses textual quality along structural completeness, grounded insight verification with numerical fact-checking against source tables, and novelty analysis that distinguishes genuine analytical discovery from trivial paraphrasing; Visualization Judge Model evaluates generated visualizations across data fidelity, expressiveness, aesthetics, and technical correctness. They also evaluate cross-modal consistency. Extensive experiments on 20 state-of-the-art models reveal critical weaknesses: even the strongest model achieves only 68.9 overall score, with numerical hallucination affecting 38.5% of reports and genuinely novel insights remaining rare. Our ablation studies further demonstrate that single-judge evaluation consistently inflates scores by 8-12 points compared to dual-judge assessment, confirming that rigorous and reliable benchmarking demands multi-faceted, complementary evaluation probes.
  </details>

- **[Where Does Hindsight Enter Clinical Agent Benchmarks? An Audit of Simulators and Case Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22241)**  `arXiv:2611.22241`  `cs.CL`  
  _Meilu Zhu, Xiaohan Li, Axiu Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks for LLM diagnostic agents are built from retrospective records: the case an agent reads is rewritten from discharge summaries, and unrecorded test results are generated by a simulator that often sees the final diagnosis. HindAudit separates three hindsight channels (the diagnosis given to the simulator, the retrospective context it conditions on, and the agent-facing case text) and measures each with judges trained only on real data. On 27,489 emergency admissions from MIMIC-IV, five registered tests found no leakage into simulated laboratory results: providing the diagnosis to five simulators had a negligible effect, and prompted gatekeepers diluted the diagnostic signal, although exploratory results suggest they can raise agent scores. The largest effect lay in the case text. Against a presentation-time reconstruction from triage records, benchmark-style summaries raised the zero-shot accuracy of four LLMs from 25.4% to 39.7% and discharge histories to 45.3%, and raised six interactive agents' scores while barely changing their ranking. The exploratory gap was reproduced in a registered replication on held-out patients. Masking diagnosis names left it unchanged. Because MIMIC lacks a contemporaneous bedside history, the gap is an upper bound on hindsight. We report all registered outcomes and recommend construction practices.
  </details>

- **[CrisisBench: an evaluation suite for crisis counseling AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21622)**  `arXiv:2611.21622`  `cs.CL`  
  _Akshay Swaminathan, Filip Ramazan, Sharang Phadke, Kevina Wang, Ivan Lopez, Shaked Peleg Azzam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Crisis helplines are essential to mental health systems but are often understaffed and underfunded. LLMs could support counselors, but existing benchmarks do not capture the escalation, risk assessment, and de-escalation central to crisis conversations, while privacy restrictions limit use of real transcripts.Methods: We present CrisisBench, a privacy-preserving suite for single- and multi-turn crisis counseling evaluation. It includes 1,085 single-turn tasks from 200 de-identified conversations and 1,185 patient LLMs derived from hotline metadata. Patient models and a 23-item LLM judge were calibrated against expert counselors.Results: We evaluated five GPT models plus draft-then-edit and retrieval-augmented generation. All systems underperformed human counselors on excessive empathy and questioning in single-turn evaluation. The rubric achieved human-human Krippendorff's $\alpha$ = 0.51 and LLM-human $\alpha$ = 0.54 (Pearson r = 0.80). In multi-turn evaluation, retrieval-augmented generation scored highest overall (0.961), with all systems within 0.058. Microskills and style/tone were near ceiling, while productivity and safety/risk accounted for most between-system variation. Counselors rated patient LLMs realistic (86%) and useful (92%).Conclusion: CrisisBench enables scalable, privacy-preserving evaluation of crisis counseling. Current systems perform strongly on core structural competencies but still diverge from experts on important response-level failure modes. We release the rubrics, judge prompts, and evaluation tasks.
  </details>

- **[From Repositories to Training Environments: Scaling Long-Horizon Coding Agents with Environment Evolution Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21152)**  `arXiv:2611.21152`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Tian, Tianyu Zheng, Xiyuan Yang, Yaxin Du, Chengran Yang, Bo Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training coding agents for long-horizon tasks requires environments with verifiable feedback. Existing pipelines often follow a linear process that ends with validation, establishing usability but providing little guidance on improving training value. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) often requires model rollouts or model training followed by downstream evaluation, with costs and feedback delays amplified on long-horizon tasks. How can we use timely, reliable proxy signals to improve training value and synthesize environments at scale? We present RepoFactory, an automated pipeline that converts real repositories into verifiable environments where agents build complete projects from scratch given a language specification. Its Environment Evolution Loop optimizes a multiplicative objective combining validity, structural coupling, and implementation scope through proxy feedback. RepoFactory produces 10,159 environments across 17 software domains. Training Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on these environments yields RepoHorizen-35B-A3B, which achieves gains of 22.6 percentage points on NL2Repo-Bench and 8.5 points on average across five benchmarks covering long-horizon coding and agentic tasks in real-world production settings, compared with 17.5 and 6.4 points without environment evolution. These results highlight the training value of RepoFactory environments and the additional gains from the Environment Evolution Loop.
  </details>

- **[CuRSE-Bench: A Benchmark for Probing Cultural Blind Spots in Text-to-Image Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20821)**  `arXiv:2611.20821`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minsuk Jang, Minseok Son, KiHyun Seol, Yeongeun Shim, Changick Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) safety evaluation typically assigns each prompt or image a single culture-blind harm label, so content that is harmful only within a particular culture can pass as benign. We introduce CultureCraft, an agentic framework that constructs T2I prompts that appear benign under a general-purpose rubric yet carry a latent harmful meaning in a target culture, and use it to build CuRSE-Bench: 10,709 culture-specific prompts and 2,648 culture-general controls over six regions and ten harm axes, each drawn from a predefined knowledge base of documented harm vectors and traceable to at least two independent sources. Scoring every prompt under six region persona judges and a paired culture-blind judge, we find that the culture-blind judge under-rates culture-specific latent harm while matching the persona judges on the controls. Each persona judge, in turn, chiefly decodes its own region's latent harm under our instrument: a selective cultural blind spot that persists in deployed moderators.
  </details>

- **[MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18264)**  `arXiv:2611.18264`  `cs.CL`
