  _Thanni Adewuyi, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Dorcas Amapu, Dieko Whenu, Sylvia Essessien, Sandra Oko-Oba, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answer continuation often returns a diagnosis already stated in unfinished clinical reasoning. We study this behavior in MamaReason, adapted from dense Gemma 4 31B, on MamaBench's 217 paired maternal and child-health presentations. An explicit-commitment extractor identifies a diagnosis in 75 of 94 failed RL first passes and 75 of 93 failed SFT first passes. The continuation matches that diagnosis in 67/75 RL cases and 58/75 SFT cases, using diagnosis strings alone. Reading these commitments without additional generation recovers 30 of the 40 RL pairs restored by continuation under the archived correctness labels. Recurrence controls distinguish routine mentions from repeated answer selection. An offline second-commitment trigger measures the tradeoff between earlier readout and paired accuracy. The resulting decomposition separates diagnostic content already present in a trace, completion of an accepted answer, and paired diagnostic correctness. For clinical language-model evaluation, these are distinct outcomes that become visible when the same reasoning trace is examined through multiple readouts.
  </details>

- **[Tracing Knowledge and Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17461)**  `arXiv:2611.17461`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Wang, Xiao Wang, Yiqi Liu, Chenghao Xiao, Eleni Vasilaki, Goran Nenadic, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current evaluations of Large Language Models (LLMs) report performance at the dataset or task level, revealing what a model achieves but offering little insight into the internal processing associated with its performance. We propose a task-centric framework for characterising the model-internal processing elicited by a benchmark. It combines attention- and activation-based signals with representation-based measures to characterise benchmarks spanning a continuum from retrieval-dominant to reasoning-dominant and to surface failure modes that task-level scores conceal. We evaluate 16 LLMs across 24 diverse benchmarks and conduct targeted internal-state analyses on representative models and tasks, observing systematic differences in processing between knowledge-centric and reasoning-centric tasks. We shift evaluation from asking what score a model achieves toward characterising the processing patterns associated with that score, potentially supporting more targeted benchmark selection and more informed model choice. We open source our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ANCHOR: Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16859)**  `arXiv:2610.16859`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenqiang Zhu, Xinping Lei, Zhiyi Lai, Zhengshuo Gong, Tianzhuang He, Songwei Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For repository-grounded coding agents, a functionally correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable one: developers also constrain how the task must be carried out-modification scope, workflow order, validation, tool use, and reporting. This capability, which we call engineering constraint following, remains largely invisible to current evaluations. We introduce ANCHOR (Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding), a benchmark built on naturalistic, multilingual repository tasks spanning eight language groups. ANCHOR combines (i) a hierarchical taxonomy of 10 categories and 78 leaf types distilled from real developer interactions, (ii) an automated pipeline that instantiates task-grounded atomic constraints and composes them into typed AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations, and (iii) a trajectory-grounded evaluation framework that checks both the code diff and the execution trajectory with rule-based and LLM judges. Across 19 agent configurations spanning 2 scaffolds and 10 foundation models on 358 tasks, the strongest measured system, Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline, reaches 70.1% atomic compliance and 63.7% relation compliance, while 19.0% of tasks pass every scored checklist item under the strict all-item FCCR criterion. A five-engineer audit of constraints from 50 instances reports 94% feasibility, 94% repository consistency, and 96% realism. In the primary paired set (14 configurations), the mean signed change in task completion after adding constraints is +0.7 percentage points; individual changes vary in both directions. These results distinguish functional success from constraint compliance, with failures concentrated in workflow ordering, tool-use discipline, and reporting, and substantial interactions between models and scaffolds.
  </details>

- **[Can Language Models Learn to Forecast Stock Prices?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36914)**  `arXiv:2609.36914`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Guo, Suozhi Huang, Shuzhen Li, Yunlong Gao, Zerui Cheng, Jason Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study whether language models can learn to forecast stock returns through training. We build BETA, a chronological sandbox over S&P 500 stocks in which a model calls tools to retrieve prices, indicators, and news available up to a decision date, and then forecasts the stock's future return. Evaluating frontier models in this sandbox reveals three weaknesses: their scores decline as the test period approaches the present while a simple momentum baseline stays stable, they predict gains far more often than gains occur, and they predict moves much smaller than those that are realized. We first fine-tune on filtered, hindsight-generated tool-use trajectories for historical tasks judged predictable from the evidence available at the time. We then apply reinforcement learning with rewards based on realized returns. During RL, long investigations frequently exceed the context window. We therefore introduce a selective compaction tool that allows the model to compress chosen portions of its interaction history. Selective compaction changes the context available to subsequent decisions, so policy updates must preserve the context in which each action was generated. Meanwhile, rollouts vary substantially in the number and length of their trainable turns, making a shared group-level baseline less effective. We address these challenges with Context-Aligned Turn-Level PPO, which uses a turn-level critic and computes policy updates using each turn's actual context. The resulting models, which we call AURA, substantially reduce all three weaknesses. Training improves the 8B model's score from 24.0 to 47.0 and the 30B model's from 27.4 to 54.7, with the latter surpassing all evaluated frontier models on our benchmark and achieving its largest margin on the most recent test period. These results suggest that agentic RL can improve performance on tasks with noisy, delayed real-world outcomes.
  </details>

- **[OpenWhistle: A Large-Scale Longitudinal Dataset and Benchmark of Bottlenose Dolphin Vocalizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34839)**  `arXiv:2609.34839`  `cs.CL`  
  _Faadil Mustun, Chiara Semenzin, Roberto Dessi, Pablo Robin Guerrero, Pierre Orhan, Alexis Emanuelli, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in bioacoustics have been driven by large-scale corpora and standardized benchmarks, yet existing resources are overwhelmingly bird-centric and shallow per species, limiting their use for studying the structure of a single species' communication system. This gap is particularly acute for cetaceans: despite bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops truncatus) being a compelling case of complex vocal communication among non-human mammals, existing dolphin datasets are small, fragmented, and largely closed. We introduce OpenWhistle, the largest publicly available dataset of dolphin vocalizations. It comprises approximately 180,000 whistles (114 hours) recorded over five years from a stable pod of five individuals in a semi-natural environment, paired with a curated subset of 8,354 expert-annotated whistles and reproducible evaluation protocols for whistle-type detection and classification. We further release the full processing pipeline for whistle detection, segmentation, and categorization. To demonstrate its utility, we pretrain a Wav2Vec2.0 model adapted to dolphin acoustics on the OpenWhistle corpus and show that it learns effective representations, outperforming general-purpose bioacoustic models such as AVES and BioLingual on both tasks while leaving meaningful headroom for future work. By releasing the dataset, pipeline, and evaluation protocol, we provide the first open dolphin whistle dataset tailored for training self-supervised models, laying the groundwork for advancing dolphin communication research and developing models that capture fine-grained acoustic structure within species.
  </details>

- **[In-Context Optimization for Retrieval-Augmented Generation: A Gradient-Descent Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26356)**  `arXiv:2605.26356`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingchen Li, Jiatan Huang, Chuxu Zhang, Liang Zhao, Hong Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning has recently been linked to implicit gradient descent in linear self-attention models, suggesting that context can induce a forward-pass update. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) also relies on context, but retrieved documents are usually treated as static evidence rather than signals for adaptation. We study RAG as an in-context optimization process. First, we show that one linear self-attention layer can implement one gradient-descent step on a unified linearized RAG objective covering both projection-based and dot-product retrieval interfaces. This gives an exact regime where retrieval-augmented prediction and in-context optimization coincide. We use this result not as a literal model of LLM computation, but as a guide for adapting the interaction between queries and retrieved evidence. We then test the boundary of this correspondence: it remains stable under controlled linear extensions, but becomes feature-distribution dependent under nonlinear architectures. Finally, we turn this view into a lightweight method for frozen RAG LLMs. The method keeps the retriever and backbone fixed, and predicts a context-conditioned update to a generator-side evidence-use interface. Across seven QA benchmarks, two retrievers, and two frozen LLM backbones, this forward-only update improves a shared-interface baseline, transfers to held-out tasks, and approaches test-time gradient adaptation at much lower per-query cost.
  </details>

- **[ToolFlood: Beyond Selection -- Hiding Valid Tools from LLM Agents via Semantic Covering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13950)**  `arXiv:2603.13950`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hussein Jawad, Nicolas J-B Brunel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly use external tools for complex tasks and rely on embedding-based retrieval to select a small top-$k$ subset for reasoning. Prior work on attacking these systems competes for tool selection, assuming benign tools remain in the retrieved set; the robustness of the retrieval stage that produces this set is underexplored. This paper introduces ToolFlood, a retrieval-layer attack on tool-augmented LLM agents. Rather than altering which tool is chosen after retrieval, ToolFlood attacks retrieval itself: it injects a few attacker-controlled tools whose metadata is positioned to exploit the geometry of embedding space, so that these tools semantically span many user queries, dominate the top-k results, and push all benign tools out of the agent's context.ToolFlood uses a two-phase adversarial tool generation strategy. It first samples subsets of target queries and uses an LLM to iteratively generate diverse tool names and descriptions. It then runs an iterative greedy selection that chooses tools maximizing coverage of remaining queries in embedding space under a cosine-distance threshold, stopping when all queries are covered or a budget is reached. We provide theoretical intuition of retrieval saturation and show on a large catalog (ToolBench) that ToolFlood achieves up to a 95\% attack success rate with a ~1\% injection rate. The code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AutoE2E: Don't speak - Language free end-to-end autonomy A Real-Time Driving Model for L2++ ADAS and L4 Robotaxis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22374)**  `arXiv:2611.22374`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Muhammad Zain Khawaja, Zaynab El Mawas, Ryota Yamada, Gabriela Cordova, Arseni Lysak, Intisar Chowdhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AutoE2E is an open-source end-to-end driving model developed by the Autoware Foundation Robotaxi Working Group. It introduces a temporal bird's-eye-view (BEV) architecture that combines multi-camera perception, HD-map information and route conditioning to predict future vehicle motion. The evaluated model uses a pretrained camera-BEV backbone, temporal feature fusion and a lightweight recurrent trajectory planner. It contains approximately 79.9 million parameters, of which approximately 1.45 million are trainable in the evaluated configuration. The v1.0 release provides model checkpoints, training and evaluation code, and DataModelConsole, a dashboard for visualizing and inspecting model experiments. On the KITScenes validation benchmark, the fine-tuned checkpoint achieves an average displacement error (ADE) of 0.745 m and a final displacement error (FDE) of 2.020 m at a three-second prediction horizon. The paper also reports preliminary real-vehicle research conducted with the Technical University of Munich's EDGAR platform, alongside ongoing collaboration with the Karlsruhe Institute of Technology. AutoE2E v1.0 is an early open research release. Its quantitative evaluation is open-loop, and the model has not yet demonstrated validated closed-loop autonomous driving. The release is intended to provide the autonomous-driving community with an inspectable baseline for further research, experimentation and collaborative development.
  </details>

- **[Explore, Map, Remember, Decide: A Benchmark for VLM Readiness in Safety-Critical Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08077)**  `arXiv:2608.08077`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA` `cs.RO`  
  _Gabriele La Malfa, Nitay Alon, Emanuele La Malfa, Reuth Mirsky, Stefan Sarkadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are often deployed as embodied controllers for robotics, yet it remains unclear how well they can explore an environment, maintain an internal map, and ground safety-critical decisions in long-horizon sequential trajectories. In this work, we introduce EMRD, an evaluation framework that decouples spatial reasoning into four stages: Explore (what the agent observes), Map (what it records and where), Remember (what it retains), and Decide (whether its final choice is justified), scored across 18 stage-specific metrics. We use EMRD to evaluate eleven open- and closed-weight VLMs across 16 buildings under nominal conditions and three environmental stresses (darkness, smoke, and motion noise), where an embodied VLM explores unknown rooms, maps observed objects, and selects the safest gathering place. Open-weight models leave rooms unvisited in 95% of episodes and detect at most 51% of objects; closed-weight models explore more, though with an external memory (notepad), two of the three stop prematurely. Without external memory, most VLMs keep fewer of the objects they saw, especially in larger buildings. The start limits which rooms are explored, and open-weight models started in different rooms agree on the final room not significantly more often than chance. Moreover, these choices are biased by semantic priors: swapping a room's label is often enough to change the final choice. Finally, motion noise increases object position error (by 12-60% for closed-weight models), strong darkness lowers map recall by 28-31 percentage points, and dense smoke reduces seen objects by 25-39 points, while the choice among the rooms entered does not change significantly.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Memory Inconsistency: Where Factual Conflicts Disappear in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22245)**  `arXiv:2611.22245`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Hejia Zhang, Zhongming Yu, Yimin Ding, Wei-Ting Chen, Hengjia Yu, Wentao Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent delegation lets LLM systems distribute evidence collection across workers with separate contexts. This creates a consistency challenge: facts retrieved by workers may be missing from the evidence available to the lead for a joint conclusion. We introduce a benchmark and evidence-flow profiler for studying multi-agent memory inconsistency through controlled factual conflicts in Harvey LAB document-review tasks. Pooled across three models with comparable generation spending, requiring delegation increases retrieval of both conflicting claims from 50.2% to 68.7% relative to single-agent execution, yet conflict detection falls from 16.2% to 13.2%. Within this setting, workers report both claims for only 35.2% of retrieved pairs, and pooled matched comparisons show higher detection than single-agent execution when one worker reads both claims but lower detection when the claims are split across workers. Worker-to-lead handoffs are a central target for improving conflict detection in multi-agent LLMs.
  </details>

- **[The Only Edge Was Memorized in Training: LLM Trading Agents Around Earnings and Bad News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22177)**  `arXiv:2611.22177`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jackson Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) trading agents are tested on news, but news reaches stock prices within minutes, and a benchmark that replays headlines at their publication time finds no alpha in trading on them. Money can then remain in two places, before news whose date is known, such as earnings announcements, and after bad news, if the market's fall went too far. However, studies of LLMs and earnings read documents published with or after the announcement, and studies of reversal sort monthly portfolios, so neither tells whether an agent can trade these moments after costs. In this paper, we test LLM agents at both moments on the same look-ahead-free benchmark, before 598 earnings announcements and after 409 bad-news drops of large US stocks. Every input is cut at the decision close, staged agents are compared with single calls, simple rules and a logistic regression, and every decision is made with and without the stock's name. Across five models from four vendors, no agent beat the simple bets. Before earnings, the agents mostly bought and earned what buying before every announcement earned, and after bad news, a Gemini-based agent called almost every fall an overreaction while no agent picked better dips than buying them all. The only gains came from what the models had memorized in training. Given only the ticker and the dates, a GPT-based model called the direction of 59.2% of the announcements before its training cutoff and 53.3% after it. Agent builders should therefore compare an event agent with the simple bet and test it blind after the training cutoff.
  </details>

- **[RSI-Prime: Training Models to Train Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23138)**  `arXiv:2611.23138`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Dewu Zheng, Yang Ye, Hongyu Zhang, Jun Yu, Yanlin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enabling large language models (LLMs) to autonomously conduct training and improve target models is an important foundation for recursive self-improvement (RSI). As LLMs expand their role from being trained to conducting training, their model-training capability warrants dedicated study and systematic development. To this end, we construct RSI-Prime, a dataset for improving model-training capability, comprising 350 complete experimental trajectories across seven task categories. To help LLMs learn to make high-quality experimental decisions from training practice, we perform fine-grained decision identification and quality annotation, then apply decision-level loss masking to exclude supervision on low-quality behavior. Experiments with GLM-5.3 on PostTrainBench, a benchmark for autonomous post-training, show that training on RSI-Prime yields a 31.95% relative improvement in overall performance over the original GLM-5.3, highlighting a path toward future RSI systems in which models autonomously sustain their own advancement.
  </details>

- **[Outcome-Balanced PPO and Policy-Induced Flow Rectification for Flow-Based VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22878)**  `arXiv:2611.22878`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuhan Wang, Ruiyang Wang, Jiaguan Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) enables post-training of flow-based vision-language-action (VLA) policies by optimizing expected task return through environment interaction. However, this optimization raises two challenges: policy updates can alter the velocity-field geometry underlying the score approximation used for stochastic transitions, and sparse multi-task rollouts can yield uneven learning signals across tasks and advantage signs. We introduce an RL framework that combines Outcome-Balanced Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with policy-induced flow rectification. Outcome-Balanced PPO reweights positive advantages using task success-rate estimates and rescales positive and negative advantages while preserving their signs. Policy-induced rectification periodically fits a linear-bridge flow to ODE endpoint pairs generated by the current policy, refreshing the representation used in subsequent stochastic optimization. We evaluate the framework on simulated manipulation benchmarks and additionally study a component combination in a Real2Sim2Real task, examining task performance, optimization behavior, and flow consistency. The proposed framework improves success rates over the evaluated baselines across the simulated benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[OICycle: Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle for Belief-State Learning in Longitudinal Clinical Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22826)**  `arXiv:2611.22826`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jitindra Fartiyal, Pedro Freire, Sergei K. Turitsyn, Sergei G. Solovski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical care is a closed loop: clinicians observe partial evidence, infer the patient's state, and intervene, and each intervention determines what is observed next. This makes clinical decision making a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP), in which a belief over a latent patient state must be updated from irregular, multimodal, and incomplete records. Yet most deep clinical models predict from a single snapshot or from coarsely aggregated history, while POMDP-based clinical methods need hand-specified discrete state spaces and transition models that real electronic health records cannot supply. We introduce OICycle (Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle), which learns a stochastic belief state directly from retrospective trajectories. At every episode, a learned recurrent operator fuses a sample of the previous belief with the new multimodal observations and the intervention that produced them. The resulting Gaussian belief is trained to predict clinicians' next interventions, the time to the next episode, and next-episode mortality. To enable reproducible evaluation, we release a benchmark derived from MIMIC-IV, MIMIC-IV-Note, and MIMIC-CXR that organizes 72,075 hospital admissions (5.4M laboratory, microbiology, radiology, prescription, and note records) into temporally ordered therapeutic--diagnostic episodes. Compared with an otherwise identical history-free model, OICycle improves next-intervention adaptive recall from 0.253 to 0.347, time-to-next-episode macro-F1 from 0.293 to 0.351, and next-episode mortality macro-F1 from 0.585 to 0.649. Analysis shows that the learned belief encodes intervention type and trajectory position and evolves smoothly across episodes, and that the benefit of history is concentrated in interventions whose ordering depends on prior findings.
  </details>

- **[Proximal Targets: Training Looped Refiners on Problems with Many Correct Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22737)**  `arXiv:2611.22737`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Syed Affan Daimi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped refiners such as HRM and TRM apply one small network repeatedly to a draft answer, and deep supervision trains every pass toward one stored answer per puzzle. We prove that when a puzzle has many correct answers this target sets a loss floor that no model capacity removes, and that the loss-minimising pass blends the answers into an invalid grid. We propose the proximal target: train each pass toward the correct answer nearest to its own input, found by scanning a list of answers, by a search solver started at the draft, or within a band of quality set by a knob; correct answers become fixed points, and a pass only has to make fewer errors than its input has for the loop to converge, which its training loss bounds. With the same 1.7M-parameter network and 5 seeds, the proximal target solves 98.8% of Sudoku puzzles with 2-64 answers (standard target: 2.5%), 93.8% of sparse puzzles with more than 10,000 answers without any list of answers (0.0%), and 90.9% of 200-node 3-colourings after training on graphs of at most 50 nodes (36.9%), and different random starts end at different answers. Against GRAM, a stochastic-latent reasoner, a single run is correct far more often (98.8% vs. 57.7%) and twenty runs find more of the answers (53.6% vs. 37.4%), while GRAM is slightly ahead on its own 10-node colouring benchmark; in Scrabble the knob becomes a calibrated strength dial.
  </details>

- **[PACD: Provenance-Aware Consensus Decoding for Mitigating Cross-Modal Hallucinations in Omnimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22456)**  `arXiv:2611.22456`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Chang Sun, Yijun Tian, Zhenwei Tang, Yuchen Deng, Yifan Simon Liu, Chenxu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omnimodal large language models (Omni-LLMs) integrate audio, video, and text for multimodal reasoning, yet remain vulnerable to cross-modal hallucinations, where one modality improperly influences predictions about another. Existing training-free decoding methods mitigate these errors through modality perturbation or relevance-based weighting. However, due to interference from irrelevant modality cues during fusion and difficulty detecting shared errors, these methods may suppress correct unimodal predictions and allow erroneous consensus to persist. To address these limitations, we propose Provenance-Aware Consensus Decoding (PACD), a training-free framework for mitigating cross-modal hallucinations. PACD comprises two complementary paths based on whether the branches reach consensus. For non-consensus cases, Relevance-Guided Candidate Arbitration identifies task-relevant unimodal evidence and selectively arbitrates between predictions using alternative candidates. For consensus cases, Provenance-Based Consensus Recovery leverages the outranking principle to evaluate the consistency and strength of layer-wise modality evidence for alternative answers, selectively resolving erroneous consensus under confidence constraints. Extensive experiments on four Omni-LLMs and five benchmarks across two tasks demonstrate the superiority of PACD. Compared to existing methods, PACD achieves up to 27.6% relative improvement over standard decoding across CMM, AVHBench, and SVHalluc for cross-modal hallucination mitigation, and an average relative improvement of 7.7% over best baseline across OmniVideoBench and FutureOmni for general audio-visual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[MedGuardBench: A Patient-Oriented Benchmark for Personalized Guardrail Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22069)**  `arXiv:2611.22069`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sara Khosravi, Xiaofei Wen, Majid Sarrafzadeh, Muhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety is personal: the same action can be safe for one person but unsafe for another. Yet current guardrails judge content without considering who is affected. Using medication safety, we introduce \textit{patient-conditioned medication guarding}, which predicts a safety verdict and 17 risk categories from a patient profile and prescription. We release \textbf{MedGuardBench}, a drug-disjoint benchmark of 5{,}001 scenarios across 87 medications. We find that standard guardrails rely on surface cues, such as wording and profile presence, rather than patient facts. To address this, we develop \textit{personalized reasoning guardrails} with three components. \textbf{Personalized Reasoning Distillation} links risks to relevant patient fields. \textbf{Context Unrolling (CU)} makes models check each field themselves, reducing false alarms on most backbones; controls show that providing the check does not yield the same benefit. A \textbf{rule-based reward (guided GRPO)} improves unsafe recall and complex-risk detection, including allergies and adverse reactions, on some backbones, but increases false alarms on others. Our models outperform base models and matched no-profile controls in accuracy, while reducing false alarms relative to no-profile controls. Profile removal and swapping confirm that gains come from patient information, not fine-tuning alone.
  </details>

- **[CORE: Concept-Oriented Reinforcement for Bridging the Definition-Application Gap in Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18857)**  `arXiv:2512.18857`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zijun Gao, Zhikun Xu, Xiao Ye, Ben Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often solve challenging math exercises yet fail to apply the concept right when the problem requires genuine understanding. Popular Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) pipelines reinforce final answers but provide little fine-grained conceptual signal, so models improve at pattern reuse rather than conceptual applications. We introduce CORE (Concept-Oriented REinforcement), an RL training framework that turns explicit concepts into a controllable supervision signal. Starting from a high-quality, low-contamination textbook resource that links verifiable exercises to concise concept descriptions, we run a sanity probe showing LLMs can restate definitions but fail concept-linked quizzes, quantifying the conceptual reasoning gap. CORE then (i) synthesizes concept-aligned quizzes, (ii) injects brief concept snippets during rollouts to elicit concept-primed trajectories, and (iii) reinforces conceptual reasoning via trajectory replacement after group failures, a lightweight forward-KL constraint that aligns unguided with concept-primed policies, or standard GRPO directly on concept-aligned quizzes. Across several models, CORE delivers consistent gains over vanilla and SFT baselines on both in-domain concept-exercise suites and diverse out-of-domain math benchmarks. CORE unifies direct training on concept-aligned quizzes and concept-injected rollouts under outcome regularization. It provides fine-grained conceptual supervision that bridges problem-solving competence and genuine conceptual reasoning, while remaining algorithm- and verifier-agnostic.
  </details>

- **[FrameRouter: Frame Budget Routing for Long-Video Understanding on Video-MME-v2 and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23055)**  `arXiv:2611.23055`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Bingjun Luo, Jialin Guo, Siqi Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video question answering benchmarks like Video-MME-v2 typically evaluate systems under a uniform frame budget for every question, despite stark variation in the evidence each query actually demands. We analyze this mismatch on Video-MME-v2 and find that different question subsets prefer different frozen frame-budget policies, while uniformly increasing the frame count does not reliably improve all metrics. These findings motivate fixed-mean frame-budget routing, a controlled inference setting in which each question may receive a different frame budget while the average budget matches a uniform reference. We introduce FrameRouter, a training-free test-time framework that combines an evidence-demand router, a budget-constrained allocation rule, and a frozen bank of frame-sampling policies. By controlling how much visual evidence each question receives while leaving the underlying samplers unchanged, FrameRouter is orthogonal to query-aware frame selection. Across Video-MME-v2 and several long-video understanding benchmarks, FrameRouter improves long-video question answering by using the same average visual budget more selectively across questions. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PACT: Plan-Action Consistency Training by Preserving Correct Plans and Repairing Actions in GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22591)**  `arXiv:2611.22591`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuohan Cai, Zhangxuan Gu, Runze Li, Beitong Zhou, Ming Wen, Hongbo Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI agents can formulate a correct immediate plan in their reasoning yet emit an action that fails to execute it, creating an execution gap. This recurring mismatch undermines reliable task completion, as a single execution error can derail a multi-step workflow. In outcome-supervised reinforcement learning, broadcasting a negative response-level advantage across all generated tokens risks penalizing valid planning together with erroneous execution. To address this credit-assignment conflict, we introduce PACT (Plan-Action Consistency Training), a training framework that combines reference-aligned plan protection and plan-aligned action repair without altering the original scalar action reward. Using annotated reference actions to identify execution gaps, PACT protects the verified plan by masking negative policy-gradient contributions across its token span. Keeping the generated reasoning unchanged as context, it then favors the reference action over the emitted erroneous action through a preference surrogate with stop-gradient pair weights. Compared with the outcome-only baseline, PACT raises AndroidWorld task success from 60.34% to 68.97% (+8.63 pp), while improving rule-based plan-action consistency on AndroidControl High from 83.71% to 89.74% (+6.03 pp). Adapting PACT to desktop agents also improves task success from 40.92% to 44.17% (+3.25 pp) on the full 369-task OSWorld benchmark. Together, these results establish PACT as an effective credit-assignment framework for bridging execution gaps and improving task completion across mobile and desktop GUI environments.
  </details>

- **[LoCaR: Localized Canvas Retrieval With Frozen Vision-Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22131)**  `arXiv:2611.22131`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Rahim Hossain, Dipanwita Saha, Ashirbad Mishra, Xinxin Shu, Svetlana Strunjas, Binbin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whole image embeddings can under represent small objects and local attributes that distinguish relevant images for localized queries. We present LoCaR, a training free framework for localized text to image retrieval with frozen vision language models. LoCaR constructs and utilizes independent proposals (SAM2) focusing on the object so as to get a more localized representation that independently encodes each region using the frozen visual encoder. We further study paired-support adaptation as an optional residual that uses matched text region exemplars without updating the underlying encoders. We also introduce LOCFG, a Visual Genome derived benchmark designed to evaluate localized retrieval for smaller and more challenging referents. Across several vision language backbones, our results show that improving the region representation itself is the main source of gain.
  </details>

- **[CADMAGEDDON: High-Fidelity CAD Reconstruction with Local Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22563)**  `arXiv:2611.22563`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Oleg Pavlov, Nikita Gavrilov, Soslan Kabisov, Gennadii Savrasov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Anton Konushin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CAD practitioners often work with proprietary parts that they are unwilling to share with external services. For individuals and small companies, bringing this work in-house also requires computational resources they can afford. We introduce CADMAGEDDON, an open-source agent that reconstructs editable CAD from meshes and can run locally on single-GPU systems and current-generation laptops. The method decomposes this complex task into a chain of simple visual decisions about what to construct and what to correct. Geometric tools support precise construction, allowing the agent to focus on visual reasoning. Using the mid-sized Qwen3.8-27B model, CADMAGEDDON outperforms prior approaches, including systems based on frontier models, in reconstruction quality on all the main benchmarks. CADMAGEDDON integrates with FreeCAD, bringing its reconstructions into practitioners' existing CAD workflows. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SkillEarth: Teaching General-Purpose Agents to Act on Earth via Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15481)**  `arXiv:2611.15481`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Haojun Tang, Wenda Zhao, Xiang Zhou, Qiwei Lin, Lida Huang, Yan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently developed remote sensing (RS) agents execute real-world tasks with complex workflows through multi-step tool invocation. These agents, however, typically rely on predefined expertise or parameter updates. They thus require updating expert knowledge or retraining models whenever new tools, tasks, or execution patterns emerge, limiting their continual adaptability in dynamic Earth environments. In this work, we seek to equip general-purpose agents with RS tool-call knowledge that can be automatically extracted from demonstrated trajectories without expert intervention or parameter updates. Specifically, we propose SkillEarth, a self-evolving skill extraction method that externalizes RS tool-call knowledge as reusable, retrievable, and evolvable skill files and refines them through observation-grounded evolution. Unlike existing automatic skill extraction methods that rely on outcome-based reflection to refine skills holistically, SkillEarth grounds skill evolution in real tool-chain execution and combines dual-judge feedback with a grounded observation routing graph for precise component-level refinement. The resulting skills are generalizable, reliable, and transferable: they improve overall RS tool-call performance across three frontier LLMs and four open-source LLMs, are refined through real tool execution feedback, and support cross-model reuse without retraining. Experiments on OpenEarth-Bench and Earth-Bench demonstrate that SkillEarth achieves better tool selection, argument correctness, execution-order consistency, and task completion than existing RS agents and skill extraction methods.
  </details>

- **[SkillBuffBench: Measuring What Agent Skills Contribute across Harnesses, Models, Skill Access Modes and Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22017)**  `arXiv:2611.22017`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Gengyue Dong, Ye Wei, Zheng Zhu, Yidong Han, Jiale Miao, Xiaojun Jia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent Skills, file-based packages of procedural knowledge that a coding agent can discover and load on demand, are now shipped by every major agent harness, yet the evidence for how much they help comes almost entirely from settings in which the right Skill is hand-picked for the task and the agent starts from a blank slate. Neither holds in deployment, where an agent chooses from whatever library happens to be installed and frequently carries memory from earlier work. We present SkillBuffBench, a benchmark and paired evaluation protocol that treats Skill access as a three-level factor (no Skill, a curated Skill, and automatic invocation from an installed library), crosses it with memory (off and on), with four agent harnesses and with multiple models (the state-of-the-art proprietary models from Anthropic, Google and OpenAI, and the state-of-the-art open-weight models from Chinese providers), every model being run under every harness, and evaluates the result on research tasks contributed by domain experts together with the Skills they use, currently in materials science and biology, with the suite extended on a rolling basis. Every run is marked by a deterministic verifier; uncertainty is reported with a paired, task-level bootstrap; and cost, agent rounds and wall-clock time are reported alongside the score rather than folded into it. This version of the paper describes the design of the benchmark, its evaluation protocol and its current tasks and Skills. The evaluation is in progress: results will be added once the runs are complete, and the task suite, results and analysis will continue to be updated on a rolling basis.
  </details>

- **[Conserved Verdicts, Drifting Commitments: Identifying Harmful Overthinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21946)**  `arXiv:2611.21946`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jianghong Zhou, Hao Rong, Chen Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A reasoning model that has reached a correct answer can lose it by continuing to think. Deciding whether to stop means comparing two probabilities at the same point of a chain: that the answer the model would give if stopped now is correct (its commitment), and that the answer it will finish with is correct (its verdict). We show that the verdict alone, which is what final-correctness probes estimate, cannot make this comparison. As a forecast of a fixed outcome it has no predictable drift, whether continuing helps or hurts. The commitment-verdict gap equals the expected remaining change in answer quality and the accuracy gained by stopping. A second identity splits any accuracy-versus-budget curve into answer change within chains and change in which chains are still running. We measure both on MATH-500. At all 379 points where DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B holds a correct answer, selected without regard to how its chain ends, continuing loses 3.6 points on average (95% CI [2.2, 5.2]), and the loss concentrates in a few problems. In six reasoning models from three families, on MATH-500 and OlympiadBench and with budgets up to 16,384 tokens, declining accuracy curves come from which chains remain, while the chains still reasoning keep or improve their answers. An oracle stopping rule gains at most +1.8 and +2.5 points at 1.5B and 7B. The framework says what to measure before building a stopping rule.
  </details>

- **[ForecastGym: A Live, Controlled, and Replayable Benchmark for Forecasting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23045)**  `arXiv:2611.23045`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yicheng Tao, Yuante Li, Zipei Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents built on large language models (LLMs) increasingly forecast real-world events by searching the Web and reasoning over what they find. How well such an agent forecasts depends on both the retrieved evidence and the model, making control over that evidence essential for evaluation. Live evaluations can mix retrieval with reasoning when each system searches on its own, while retrospective search can leak later information through date filters or today's ranking of historical pages. Comparing models, search sources, and memory strategies requires both fixed evidence and a record of how it evolved before resolution. We introduce ForecastGym, which records what four commercial web search APIs return for open prediction-market questions every two days and replays this frozen evidence offline once outcomes are known, comparing models, memory, and captured search sources under a fixed evidence-acquisition policy. Its first release covers 150 questions, 614 evidence states, one per question and collection time, and 131k search results. Rerunning the same queries after settlement returns results that all but state the outcome for up to 92% of the questions, despite the tested date filters. On the frozen evidence, all tested LLMs trail the market in forecasting accuracy. Our diagnostic analysis suggests that missed public information contributes more to the gap in high-gap states than misreading does. Switching the search API changes a model's Brier score by up to 0.043, more than the spread across models, while persistent notes score similarly to rereading all evidence at half the input tokens. The evidence a forecaster sees can matter as much as the model that reads it.
  </details>

- **[CrossEnv: Synthesizing Verifiable Cross-Environment Tasks for Long-Horizon Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22777)**  `arXiv:2611.22777`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhengfeng Yu, Tian Lan, Zhijing Wu, Enbo Zhao, Xuannan Liu, Feihu Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Executable synthetic environments provide scalable training tasks and verifiable feedback for long-horizon agents. Existing environment synthesis methods primarily generate tasks within individual environments. However, these tasks do not adequately capture real-world interactions across services, where actions in one service depend on information obtained from another. Synthesizing such tasks is challenging because data generated across environments must remain consistent to support executable and verifiable interactions. To address this challenge, we introduce CrossEnv, a framework that uses an entity-tool graph to identify connections between environments and aligns task-specific data to synthesize executable, verifiable cross-environment tasks. We further propose CrossEnv-Curriculum, which progresses from single-environment tool use to coordination across more environments and business domains, before introducing user interaction. Across four external benchmarks, combining cross-environment training data with this curriculum achieves competitive performance with fewer training examples. Further analyses support the benefits of cross-environment data and curriculum training, and provide evidence of transfer to new environment combinations, external tasks less similar to the training data, and longer workflows.
  </details>

- **[The Between-Round Decision in Autonomous Research: An LLM Controller That Reads the Whole Trajectory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22661)**  `arXiv:2611.22661`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Caoxin Wang, Shengkai Gan, Xiang Hu, Lili Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Executing an idea often changes how promising it looks, so the next step in autonomous research should rest on results, not on proposals. After each round of experiments, the system must decide whether to refine the best solution or start a new direction, which solution to build from, and what goal to set. We call this the between-round decision. Existing systems wrap it in machinery such as rules, scores, search trees, and schedules; where an LLM makes it, what the LLM needs to read is left open. We ask whether an LLM can make this decision from accumulated evidence, without a search structure around it, and how much of that evidence it needs to read. We present BRED (Between-Round Evidence-based Decision), an LLM-based controller that maintains the best verified solution so far (the Champion) and a log of past rounds, and makes one decision per round. On three widely used research-agent benchmark tasks, BRED improves on the task baselines by 4.80% in MAE on ETTh1 (to 0.4172) and by 3.44 and 3.43 points of accuracy on SST-2 (to 95.07%) and CIFAR-100 (to 84.63%), ahead of the published results. When the controller does not read the accumulated evidence, the same loop gains less: with no history it reaches 0.4299 on ETTh1 and 83.71% on CIFAR-100, whereas the full controller's MAE is 2.95% lower and its accuracy 0.92 points higher. On a new task, speech-based depression detection under a leakage-free protocol, it reaches a macro-F1 of 0.6164 on seeds held out from selection, 5.5 points above its baseline and 4.0 above the strongest published system. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fine-Grained Japanese Clinical Named Entity Recognition: A Controlled Comparison of BERT and Open-Weight Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22219)**  `arXiv:2611.22219`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Feng Han, Ayako Yagahara, Kenji Hirata, Atsushi Yoshida, Kohsuke Kudo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Generative large language models (LLMs) offer an alternative to encoder-based clinical named entity recognition (NER), but their performance on fine-grained Japanese extraction tasks and sensitivity to generation settings remain uncertain. Methods: We compared two fine-tuned BERT-CRF baselines with nine open-weight LLMs on iCorpus, which defines 113 entity categories. The evaluation used 433 sentences from 36 test documents; 1,672 sentences were used for training. LLMs were evaluated using zero-shot prompting with and without JSON Schema constraints and three-seed QLoRA fine-tuning. Fine-tuned models were assessed under different output-token limits, including 900 and larger model-specific limits of 2,600 or 4,096 tokens. Performance was measured using sentence-level label-surface matching with relaxed and exact-surface F1. Results: The highest zero-shot relaxed F1 was 0.337, compared with 0.836-0.841 for the BERT baselines. At the larger output limits, all eight fine-tuned LLMs with at least 13B total parameters exceeded both BERT baselines (relaxed F1, 0.882-0.914; exact-surface F1, 0.840-0.880). Across three-seed means, increasing the output limit improved relaxed F1 by 0.052-0.085, alongside higher recall and fewer length-limited outputs. JSON Schema constraints changed relaxed F1 by -0.014 to +0.008 among seven models outside the two reasoning-oriented configurations, while medical-general model pairs showed no consistent advantage for medical specialization. Conclusions: On this historical Japanese case-report benchmark, task-specific fine-tuning combined with adequate generation capacity was associated with stronger LLM extraction performance. Output-token limits materially affected comparisons with BERT, whereas enforcing output structure alone did not overcome low zero-shot accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Long-Context Generation Is a Sampling Problem (When You Turn Up the Heat)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22148)**  `arXiv:2611.22148`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Allen G. Roush, Minh Nhat Nguyen, Ravid Shwartz-Ziv, Judah Goldfeder, Sanjay Basu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-weight language models are widely observed to get worse the longer they generate. Often, attempts to fix this involve more training. At high temperature, The sampler, i.e. the rule that picks the next token out of the model's probabilities, decides whether long and agentic generation survives and/or thrives. We test 29 open-weight models from 1B to 122B parameters on ten established benchmarks plus needle retrieval out to one million tokens, covering SWE-bench Verified coding agents, long chain-of-thought reasoning, long-form writing, long structured output and long-context reading. Samplers that keep their pool of candidate tokens small and steady as temperature rises don't lose accuracy. Samplers that let in low-probability tokens fall apart. At temperature 2, coding agents running top-p, top-k or Top-H resolve 0 of 100 GitHub issues, while the same agents running top-nsigma resolve 46 to 62 and win every issue the two samplers disagree on, on three Qwen3.5 models. Over all benchmarks top-nsigma keeps a median 100% of its temperature-1 score where top-p keeps 26%. Counting how many tokens each sampler lets through explains why. In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. Across 133 combinations of sampler setting and temperature, the typical pool size predicts judged quality (Spearman correlation of -0.77), and when we pick the pool at temperature 1 and only apply temperature afterwards, Top-H, min-p and p-less stop collapsing. At the default temperature of 1 most samplers perform about the same, We release all code and data to the public.
  </details>

- **[Mingbird: A Local-First Agent Harness Enabling Small Open Models to Complete Real Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02001)**  `arXiv:2610.02001`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Wang, Ting Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small open-weight language models now run on a laptop, but they fail at multi-step work in ways a large model does not: they stop with the files missing, repeat one action until the budget ends, and report success without checking. At the one-to-nine-billion-parameter tier the harness supplies almost all of the procedural control that a large model supplies forthis http URLpresent Mingbird, an open-source harness for that tier on a consumer graphics card: 12k lines of Python, a 628-test suite, and eighteen mechanisms for context economy, completion, time budget and the host boundary, plus a crawl guard outside those four groups. Its design point is completion control: the harness runs executable checks on the working directory and refuses a claim of completion when theythis http URLevaluations accompany it. A configuration-surface audit finds four defects that the 625-test suite of v3.1.1 missed, each because a test checked a property adjacent to the one that broke. A ten-task probe suite gives each mechanism a reason to fire and reports where the change reaches the artifact, including one mechanism that cuts the signal it protects. Disabling five of six completion-time checks costs 0.152 of the artifact score on eighteen held-out tasks, three replications per cell (95% CI [+0.064, +0.246], Holm p = 0.014). On a 288-cell matrix the harness reaches 0.886 against 0.631, 0.479 and 0.405 for three open scaffolds, and on {\tau} 2 -bench it ranks first or tied first in all three domains against the armsthis http URL, scorer, source and every per-cell result are public.
  </details>

- **[The Memory Trust Gap: Reliance on Stale Evidence Across Model Scales in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01852)**  `arXiv:2609.01852`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jundong Hu, Shekar Ramachandran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Persistent-memory agents can follow stale information despite current authoritative evidence. We study stale-evidence reliance across Qwen3 0.6/1.7/4/8B checkpoints using two controlled, action-scored suites: a Benefit suite requiring stored information and a Safety suite providing current evidence as static prompt text. We distinguish stale-value reliance from net accuracy loss relative to no memory. Benefit-suite reliance is 0.92-1.00 across checkpoints. In the Safety suite, larger checkpoints resist a plain stale note but incur substantial harm when it appears newer. A full $2\times2\times2\times2$ factorial manipulates label, recency, source authority, and position. Direct cross-size contrasts identify stronger recency effects in the larger tested checkpoints and a position-effect sign reversal within Qwen3. Metadata improves accuracy at all four checkpoints, with larger gains at 4B/8B; pre-resolution yields near-ceiling accuracy across all four. Llama-Instruct comparisons provide cross-family evidence for selected effects, while RGB and MisBench corroborate stale-value reliance. A framing control shows that the failure extends beyond memory-labeled content. These results characterize cue-dependent vulnerability across tested checkpoints without isolating capability or evaluating a complete memory lifecycle.
  </details>

- **[PhysScope: Agentic Multimodal Physics Reasoning with Grounded Visual Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23010)**  `arXiv:2611.23010`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyang Liu, Kecheng Liang, Xinyu Li, Weixing Chen, Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can correctly interpret physics diagrams yet apply constraints outside the states or conditions in which they hold, causing premise errors to propagate through subsequent calculations. We introduce PhysScope, an inference-time visual harness that supports self-correction of these scope mismatches without updating model weights. PhysScope enables the model to link physical scopes directly to its visual observations and explicitly constructs derived views to support reasoning. The harness imposes explicit constraints on state distinctions, conditions, and physical scopes during model reasoning, and checks agent-proposed revisions against sources and scopes. Accepted revisions invalidate registered dependents, thereby maintaining scope-aware working memory. On PhysAlign, PhysScope achieves higher solving scores than direct solving across all six evaluated VLMs. With Gemini-3.8-flash, it outperforms Canvas-CoT by 9.34 percentage points and substantially improves solving accuracy on the out-of-distribution (OOD) PhysUniBench benchmark. Ablation studies support explicit scope management and dependency maintenance as design principles for multimodal physics reasoning agents.
  </details>

- **[Done, But Still Working: Anytime Evaluation of When Coding Agents Should Stop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22901)**  `arXiv:2611.22901`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zerui Yang, Quan Xue, Miaomiao Dong, Chaorui Zhang, Xuanyu Zheng, Xiyuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents often continue working after their own tests pass. Does this additional work improve the patch, and how much could be saved by stopping earlier? We study these questions through anytime evaluation: replaying recorded runs, scoring intermediate patches with hidden tests, and measuring the cost incurred at each step. For policies that only stop a run or select an earlier patch, this allows paired comparisons without generating new trajectories, provided replay preserves the relevant states. Across about 6,000 runs of eight open models in two scaffolds on SWE-bench Verified, we find substantial differences in the value of continued work. The hindsight saving available at passing self-tests ranges from under 1% to 57%, depending on the model and scaffold. A rule that stops after the same patch passes twice saves 23% in a preregistered replication, but the preregistered quality test does not establish non-inferiority. We explain these differences by measuring how often a rule fires, how often the patch changes afterwards, and whether those changes repair or break it. Fine-tuning agents to imitate the rule saves 30% over six seeds, while quality preservation remains uncertain. Our results help identify both the opportunities and the risks of early stopping.
  </details>

- **[Verifying Agents in Rubric-Graded Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22841)**  `arXiv:2611.22841`  `cs.AI`  
  _Markus Dücker, Vaibhav Kumar, Yi Liu, Ronak Chaudhary, Andreas Plesner, Francisco Guzmán, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI agents take on increasingly open-ended and complex tasks, verifying their outputs has proven to be difficult. Rubric-graded agent environments, where a verifier judges multiple natural-language criteria against the deliverables and environment state changes produced by the agent, have emerged as a popular paradigm. We conduct the first systematic study of verifiers for rubric-graded agent environments. First, we create BankerVerifierBench (BVB), a meta-evaluation dataset of 2,464 human-judged rubric criteria across 24 investment-banking tasks. Next, we develop a methodology that derives from any rubric corpus the capabilities a verifier should possess, yielding a nine-capability taxonomy that we distill into three general design principles---reactive verification, environment alignment, and domain guidance---which we implement in Gandalf, an open-source verifier. Finally, we evaluate Gandalf against three existing verifiers on BVB. Gandalf matches or exceeds every baseline's F1 at lower cost: with Gemini 3 Flash it matches the strongest baseline's F1 (0.864 vs. 0.852) at one-fifth of its cost, and with GPT-5.4 models it improves F1 by over 0.12 at lower cost. Gandalf also generalizes to OpenClaw, a structurally different personal-productivity environment, where it again achieves the highest F1 (0.955 vs. 0.934 for the best baseline). We release BVB, Gandalf, and the code and data to reproduce our experiments.
  </details>

- **[Synergizing Evidence Retrieval and Utilization for Self-Improving Multimodal Scientific Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22837)**  `arXiv:2611.22837`  `cs.AI`  
  _Frank Nie, Ethan B. Liu, Yuan Zhu, Jindong Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific document reasoning requires an agent to acquire and integrate evidence distributed across text, figures, and tables. Retrieval can identify relevant sources while missing complementary support, and answer-level feedback alone does not reveal whether an error stems from evidence acquisition or evidence use. We present an agentic Retriever--Perceiver--Reasoner framework for multimodal scientific document reasoning. The Retriever sequentially selects source-aligned evidence under a context budget. Given the selected evidence and question, the Perceiver constructs a question-conditioned latent ontology, an implicit organization of multimodal evidence in a shared continuous space. The Reasoner combines this latent guidance with the original sources to produce grounded answers. We train the three components through diagnostic-guided co-evolution: alternating updates optimize them with a shared answering reward, while Retrieval Potential Feedback (RPF) and Latent Efficacy Feedback (LEF) identify questions that need better retrieval or latent guidance and direct targeted resampling. Across four benchmarks and seven evaluation settings, our framework obtains average scores of 69.9 and 75.0 with 7B and 8B Reasoner backbones, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.0 and 9.1 points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Remembered as Brief: Stored Preferences SilentlyRemove Decision-Critical Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22697)**  `arXiv:2611.22697`  `cs.AI`  
  _Joshua Adrian Cahyono_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. We release the scenarios, harness and logs.
  </details>

- **[Right Diagnosis, Unsafe Route: Auditing Clinical AI Decision Trajectories and Corrective Oversight for Validity and Potential Harm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22612)**  `arXiv:2611.22612`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guanqiao Chen, Wei Jiang, Shu Zhang, Qian Lin, Guanxu Yu, Siran Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A clinical AI system can reach a correct final diagnosis through intermediate decisions that could have harmed the patient, and can repair an error while introducing new risks. Most evaluations score final diagnoses; even stepwise benchmarks, which release a case's evidence over time, score whether systems reach the right diagnosis or gather the right information, not whether the decisions a patient passes through are safe. We make patient safety the target of stepwise evaluation. A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.
  </details>

- **[EvoSkill-S1: Evolving Reusable Skills with Genetic-Inspired Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22610)**  `arXiv:2611.22610`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yujun Wang, Jiqiang Li, Yinuo Lu, Jinhe Bi, Aniri, Wenxuan Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reusable skills allow language-model agents to accumulate and reuse problem-solving experience. However, most existing methods improve a skill bank only indirectly through rollout-driven accumulation and heuristic reflection, often resulting in shallow and weakly controlled updates. We present EvoSkill-S1, an RL-driven framework that instead treats reusable skills themselves as the direct objects of evolution. Given hard-negative failures, EvoSkill-S1 selects parent skills and transforms them into candidate child skills through five genetic-inspired operators: revision, cross-revision, mutation, dual-reflection, and crossover. Downstream task rewards train the skill evolver to generate more effective child skills, while evaluator-guided write-back selectively retains beneficial updates. Complementary Skill Bank Fusion consolidates operator-specific banks to exploit their distinct recovery patterns, while a Procedural Skill Interface supports transfer to unseen problems. Across all reported math and code benchmarks, EvoSkill-S1 improves the corresponding initialized skill banks under both Qwen and GPT-family solvers, with relative gains of up to 12.39%. Compared with the strongest external baseline without direct skill evolution, the largest relative improvement reaches 20.39%.
  </details>

- **[Simplicity Goes Far: Auditing Prompt Optimizers with Demonstration-Conditioned Prompt Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22605)**  `arXiv:2611.22605`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bohe Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic prompt optimization is growing more elaborate. Recent methods stack policy-gradient updates, trace reflection, and evolutionary search. These methods share a generate-and-select step; without controlling for it, reported gains may conflate the contributions of optimizer mechanisms and larger sampling budgets. We isolate this step as Demonstration-Conditioned Prompt Search (DCPS), repurposing the Automatic Prompt Engineer (APE) recipe as a mechanism-attribution control: it freezes the generator, conditions it on randomly sampled demonstrations, scores candidates on validation data, and keeps the best---no learned update, reflection, or evolution. Across two optimizer families, DCPS replaces Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) in StablePrompt, matching classification accuracy within 0.5 pp at roughly one-third the measured cost; in DSPy-based compound AI systems, under the reported budgets, it matches GEPA's macro accuracy on GPT-4.1-mini while using 55% fewer rollouts at 32% lower measured API cost on average. The audit reveals a task-dependent pattern: proposal and selection recover much of heavier optimizers' performance on structured and instruction-following tasks, while additional mechanisms retain advantages in some open-ended and reasoning-heavy settings. We recommend benchmarking prompt optimizers against generate-and-select controls and reporting rollout counts, token usage, and dollar costs alongside accuracy. Code and artifacts:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SciWorkBench: A Benchmark for Real Scientific Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22490)**  `arXiv:2611.22490`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Taolin Han, Jinghang Wang, Jinxin Wang, Yun Wu, Wai Yuet Chiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are increasingly being used as agents capable of planning and executing projects, as well as refining their actions through feedback. This shift raises a question: can current agents complete projects like real scientists? To examine this question, we introduce SciWorkBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents' abilities on real science tasks across biology, chemistry, materials science, and physics. SciWorkBench is built from peer-reviewed literature or expert-contributed research scenarios. We also propose a unified scientific analysis framework validated by domain experts, which can categorize most tasks in SciWorkBench. Each task covers one or more stages of this framework. We evaluate up-to-date models within the Claude Code and Codex agent frameworks under a unified execution and grading protocol. The best-performing agent achieves a mean score of 57.38, indicating that current agents still struggle with realistic scientific workflows. Our failure analysis identifies three recurring weaknesses at the workflow level: difficulty translating scientific knowledge into task-specific procedures, weak integration of evidence across different modalities, and limited capacity for scientific self-correction. These findings highlight the importance of workflow-based evaluation for measuring progress toward reliable AI systems for scientific research.
  </details>

- **[RehearsalBench: Can AI Predict What Happens When You Change a Company?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22480)**  `arXiv:2611.22480`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mika Siddiqui, Tharaka Perera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Organizations increasingly ask AI systems what will happen if they change how they operate: which work to automate, which team to resize, what a voice agent will do to waiting times. We introduce RehearsalBench, a benchmark that measures whether AI models can diagnose an operation, choose an intervention, and forecast its consequences. Each model is placed in a simulated bank contact center fitted to real call-level records, sees only what a manager would see, and submits a plan and forecasts that are then played out across 200 seeded futures and three plausible versions of the organization. Decisions are scored in dollars at the stakeholders' stated valuations; forecasts are scored on the effect of each change relative to doing nothing. Ten models made 280 attempts across 14 organizations. The best model, GPT-6 Astra, improved on doing nothing by 6.8% of the no-change cost and made 77% less forecast error than a forecaster who always predicts no effect. On average, every model forecast the direction of every specified effect correctly, but models erred on the size of the same effects in the same direction, overstating a voice agent's effect on repeat contacts about five-fold. No model ever recommended holding off when doing nothing was the right choice. To test external validity, twelve models forecast 33 real organizational changes with known outcomes. Under verdict rules fixed in advance, the benchmark's forecast ranking agreed with the real-world ranking (Spearman $\rho = 0.92$, 95% lower bound 0.50). We report the result alongside an inconclusive first test, the analysis choices made after outcomes were seen, and evidence that part of the agreement reflects how well models size their uncertainty.
  </details>

- **[Symbolic Abstraction Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22404)**  `arXiv:2611.22404`  `cs.AI`  
  _Daniel Bryce, Robert P. Goldman, David J. Musliner, Jeremy Gottlieb, John Skovbekk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous systems choose actions whose outcomes are uncertain and are subject to time constraints. Probabilistic timed automata (PTAs) model exactly this, and verifying one answers a question about the system's decision-making: the maximum probability of reaching a goal, or the minimum probability of reaching a hazard, that a controller can guarantee. Verification normally works over symbolic states, each pairing a discrete location with a region of clock values, and its difficulty is that the best behavior generally varies within such a region. The PRISM verifier's most capable PTA method responds by splitting regions until each is uniform, rebuilding the transitions that reach them and propagating the split backward through the state graph of a stochastic game, a cascade of graph reconstruction that partitions regions the property never depends on. We present SYMAR, which instead makes the value function symbolic: each symbolic state carries a function from sub-regions of its clock region to probability bounds, so refinement is the appearance of new sub-regions in that function, and previously generated states are never split nor their incoming transitions reconstructed. Both sources of PTA nondeterminism, the timing and the choice of actions, are captured implicitly by the preimage computation that produces those sub-regions. Heuristic forward search then bounds how much refinement is done at all, expanding an And-Or graph from the initial state along the most promising behaviors so that effort is confined to the property-relevant fragment, guided by bounds and preferred actions from a single unrefined run of the existing method; a partial-refinement backup keeps the otherwise combinatorial sub-region count lean, and every stored interval provably contains the true probability. On the PRISM PTA benchmarks, whose subproblems recur in autonomous systems (distributed agreement under contention, leader election, and self-configuration without central coordination), SYMAR is up to 108x faster, solves an instance on which the existing method exhausts the time limit, and is markedly less sensitive to the deadline constant, resolving one instance in a 37-node search graph, unchanged as the deadline triples, where the existing method needs 86,380 abstract states and 73 refinement rounds.
  </details>

- **[HealthCLBench:Evaluating Context Learning in Health Conversations through Controlled Case Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22362)**  `arXiv:2611.22362`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ting Yang, Zijing Hong, Chengjie Wang, Peng Wei, Chunxiao Guo, Yun Yue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex health conversations require answers grounded in individual information and currently valid evidence. We evaluate health context learning: acquiring, integrating, and applying relevant context during inference with fixed model parameters to answer health questions and respond appropriately to changes in facts, evidence, or task goals. To our knowledge, Chinese-language medical benchmarks have yet to systematically combine explicit contextual difficulties, controlled case variations, and member-specific medical criteria for open-ended answers. We introduce HealthCLBench, comprising 328 controlled clusters, 1,103 cases, and 4,931 rubric criteria across medical consultation and everyday health management. Members share backgrounds while evidence, patient states, or task goals change; each has its own medical scoring requirements. A 70-person medical team annotates and reviews the simulated cases and their rubrics. Human review of 700 full-context responses from the original evaluation yields 90.5% pooled agreement with the original automated judgments at the rubric-item level. We report full-context and query-only results for seven general-purpose models and Ant Afu. Across the eight systems, full- query differences range from 11.83 to 31.76 case points and from 10.87 to 38.97 geometric cluster points. GPT-6-Astra leads both full-context metrics at 75.03 and 60.42, followed by Claude-Opus-5-5 and Ant Afu. Cross-condition differences describe score changes under the reported evaluation settings. Response comparisons reveal failures in checking historical information, expressing uncertainty when warranted, and ensuring recommendations respect required conditions. The resource supports model comparison and targeted follow-up evaluation.
  </details>

- **[EviDARL: Evidence-Centric Dual-Agent Reasoning via Alternating Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Table Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22310)**  `arXiv:2611.22310`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dunhan Li, Leilei Gan, Lirong Gao, Wenyan Fan, Yongwei Wang, Sheng Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite advances in LLM-based table reasoning, most studies focus on single-table settings. Multi-table QA requires multi-hop reasoning over complex table relationships and evidence dispersed across long contexts, often without verifiable intermediate traces. To address these challenges, we introduce EviDA, an evidence-centric dual-agent framework comprising an Evidence Construction Agent and a Reasoning and Answering Agent. The Evidence Construction Agent generates and executes table-manipulation programs over complete tables to produce question-specific evidence and verifiable intermediate traces, simplifying subsequent reasoning. The Reasoning and Answering Agent then performs multi-hop reasoning over this evidence to derive final answers. We further introduce EviDARL, which combines agent-specific fine-grained rewards with parameter-efficient alternating reinforcement learning to improve collaboration between the agents. By optimizing each agent in turn while holding the other fixed, EviDARL enables bidirectional adaptation between evidence construction and answer reasoning. Experiments on MMQA and TQA-Bench demonstrate that EviDARL substantially outperforms competitive open-model and multi-table reasoning baselines under constrained model and training budgets.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Edit IFC Building Models: Verifiable Checkers as the Training Signal for Dependency-Aware Editing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22307)**  `arXiv:2611.22307`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaxin Zhang, Yao Luo, Yuze Dan, Yunqin Li, Bowen Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
