  Editing a building information model in the Industry Foundation Classes (IFC) schema requires propagating changes to dependent elements while preserving unrelated entities. Existing language-model agents for IFC editing use prompted models without learning from edit outcomes. Target-file comparisons do not specify which alternative edits are allowed or which unrelated entities must remain unchanged. We present IfcGym, a CodeAct environment in which a language model edits IFC through executable code and four checkers verify validity relative to the input model, intent, dependency and preservation against a task specification. Their conjunction is strict success. IfcEdit distils verified teacher episodes into a Qwen3.5-9B student through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), then applies Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with strict success as the reward. On IfcEditBench, 1,990 editing tasks on 13 held-out real projects, the final student reaches 86.78% strict success, from 79.15% after SFT. The paired confidence interval for the difference from its teacher includes zero; the same recipe on a 27B model reaches 91.76% and exceeds the teacher. On the public benchmarks BIM-Edit and BIBIMBAP the final student scores 55.19 and 54.50. On IfcEditBench, the students outperform all prompted models except the teacher under matched tool access; both evaluated frameworks also score lower. Rewarding all four checkers yields 4.83 points more strict success than rewarding intent and validity alone.
  </details>

- **[MCTS Is Counting the Wrong Thing: Learning Exploration Counts for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22301)**  `arXiv:2611.22301`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuan Zhang, Zhijian Zhou, Zicheng Hu, Yan Feng, Hongyan Hao, Yuan Qi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) has powered landmark successes in reinforcement learning, yet its gains in LLM reasoning can remain modest despite substantial search effort. We identify an overlooked bottleneck: what MCTS counts. Standard UCT assigns independent visit counts to textual branches, allowing different formulations of the same reasoning strategy to repeatedly receive exploration bonuses. Our theoretical analysis shows that this count fragmentation can impair exploration efficiency, as quantified by increased cumulative regret. To address this problem, we introduce Learned Pseudo-Count MCTS (LPC-MCTS), which changes only the exploration statistic in UCT. A lightweight Coin-Flipping Network head on the policy's hidden representations estimates representation coverage, enabling related branches to share exploration evidence. Across 5 mathematical-reasoning and 7 search benchmarks, LPC-MCTS consistently outperforms strong MCTS baselines. Holding each LPC-MCTS-trained policy fixed, replacing standard MCTS counts at inference improves average mean@16 accuracy across 5 mathematical benchmarks by 3.79 percentage points on Qwen2.5-7B and 2.71 points on Qwen3-4B. Applying LPC-MCTS during both training and inference yields gains of 4.96 and 4.37 points over standard MCTS, respectively. Together, our theory and experiments show that changing what MCTS counts can substantially improve its exploration efficiency and reasoning performance.
  </details>

- **[Harnesses That Improve Themselves: A Unifying Framework for Self-Improving Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22299)**  `arXiv:2611.22299`  `cs.AI`  
  _Deep Shah, Nehal Kathrotia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a foundation model succeeds as an autonomous agent depends as much on its runtime software as on its weights. This runtime, the agent harness, manages context and memory, steers multi-turn control, executes tool actions in sandboxes, and verifies work before stopping. Because models differ in how they use tools and how they fail, a static harness tuned for one model or task often stalls, wastes tokens, or regresses on another. This bottleneck has driven a shift from prompt tuning to self-improving agent harnesses: systems that inspect their own execution traces, separate runtime bugs from model limits, and rewrite their own code. We present a unifying architectural and design framework for self-improving agent harnesses. The framework pairs four editable runtime components (context and state, agent control, action interfaces, and verification and recovery) with a four-stage loop that diagnoses failures, proposes code patches, validates candidates, and deploys updates. Using this framework, we show how optimizers trace errors across long trajectories and discover reusable runtime mechanisms such as pre-stop verifiers, loop breakers, and persistent memory. We further show why full-harness rewrites overfit or break across modules, and how modular updates with validation gates generalize across models while bounding token cost. To keep self-modification safe, we establish practical defenses against recurring failure modes including prompt bloat, benchmark gaming, and evaluator tampering. Finally, experiments on SWE-bench Lite validate this framework, showing that gated modular evolution outperforms prompt reflection and full-harness rewrites across models and repositories at a fraction of the token cost.
  </details>

- **[SeasonRainBench: Global Seasonal Precipitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22281)**  `arXiv:2611.22281`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qiuyan Sun, Mengxuan Chen, Haoyuan Liang, Guowen Li, Zhen Huang, Jianhao Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate seasonal precipitation forecasts help societies prepare for drought, flooding, and crop failure months in advance. While data-driven methods have made rapid progress in weather and subseasonal forecasting, operational seasonal precipitation prediction still relies on numerical ensemble systems. These systems are computationally expensive and exhibit biases and drift that increase with lead time, motivating more efficient data-driven alternatives. Yet this transition faces three linked challenges: comparing data-driven forecasts with operational ones requires (1) a global benchmark that does not exist, scoring them requires (2) metrics that capture operational priorities beyond anomaly correlation (ACC) and RMSE, and training them must (3) rely on a single, short observational record. To address the first two challenges, we introduce \BenchName, the first such benchmark, covering monthly precipitation forecasts 1--5 months ahead. It pairs global $1^\circ$ satellite precipitation with oceanic and atmospheric predictors, includes seven operational centers, and provides 310,944 simulated months from 52 climate models for training. Beyond standard scores, we introduce two operationally motivated metrics for anomaly strength and monthly rain-belt position. To address the third challenge, we develop \MODEL, which learns from simulations what the record lacks and transfers it to Earth through three modules: SimForced aligns the climate background, SimMemory transfers a history encoder pretrained on simulations, and SimAnalog supplements Earth labels with simulated analog outcomes. Extensive experiments show that \MODEL{} improves ACC, CRPS, anomaly strength, and rain-belt position by 12.0\%, 3.6\%, 2.9\%, and 0.3\% over the strongest operational or data-driven model, opening a path from simulated worlds to Earth system prediction.
  </details>

- **[DACE: Diversity-Driven Adversarial Co-Evolution for Robust LLM Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22278)**  `arXiv:2611.22278`  `cs.AI`  
  _Peng Yu, Xiaoyu Wen, Zhida He, Ziyuan Zhou, Han Qi, Shao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensuring robust safety alignment of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly difficult as adversarial attacks evolve and outpace alignment pipelines built on pre-collected data. Existing co-evolutionary frameworks let the defender chase a moving attacker, yet their training dynamics expose two compounding pathologies: \emph{attack strategy collapse}, where the attacker overfits to a narrow set of high-reward rewrites, and \emph{defense adversarial forgetting}, where the defender loses competence against earlier attacks as the attack distribution drifts. Existing remedies are partial: attacker-side diversity rewards score textual novelty rather than strategy novelty, and defender-side replay relies on discrete judge scores that ignore estimation uncertainty, response stochasticity, and defender non-stationarity. We introduce \textbf{\method}, a diversity-driven adversarial co-evolution framework that targets both pathologies jointly. On the attacker side, \method couples an explicit two-dimensional strategy space (risk category $\times$ attack style) with a \emph{normalized marginal coverage gain} reward, providing a bounded, non-vanishing exploration signal at the strategy layer. On the defender side, \method maintains a unified \emph{Bayesian adversarial replay pool} whose Beta--Bernoulli threat posteriors are refreshed by time decay and sampled via Thompson sampling, anchoring defender training to an evolving threat landscape. Across standard safety, automated-attacker, and general-capability benchmarks, \method improves robustness to out-of-distribution attacks while preserving general capabilities, and yields broader attacker strategy coverage.
  </details>

- **[Context Has a Price: Adding and Removing Context for LLM Coding Agents under Prefix Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22238)**  `arXiv:2611.22238`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zerui Yang, Miaomiao Dong, Quan Xue, Chaorui Zhang, Xuanyu Zheng, Xiyuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents repeatedly send their conversation history to a language model. Adding useful code may reduce search, while removing old tool outputs may shorten later prompts. But do these changes reduce the cost of solving a task? We study this as a measurement problem in more than 20,000 runs of open models on SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench and Loc-Bench. We account separately for uncached input, cached input and output tokens. Three findings emerge. First, in our main evidence-package experiments, retrieved code reduces read and search commands by 18--34%, but increases cost by 33--42% when cached input costs one tenth of uncached input, without a measurable improvement in resolution. Second, removing old observations can either save money or increase cost: the same approach saves 19--36% without a cache discount but increases cost by 38--116% with a tenth-price cache. Third, shorter prompts do not guarantee cheaper runs because agents may respond by taking more steps. We also find that concurrent experiments can share cached prefixes and distort cost comparisons. These results show why context-management methods should be evaluated using both token prices and the agent's subsequent behavior.
  </details>

- **[RAG in a Trenchcoat: When Minimal Memory Is Enough for Agentic Systems, and When It Isn't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22179)**  `arXiv:2611.22179`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyu Liu, Zongze Li, Zach Xu, Zhanhui Zhou, Tahseen Rabbani, Dawn Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic memory research has accumulated extraction, supersession, reflection, and graph synthesis on top of retrieval-augmented generation. However, we found that almost none of those primitives earn their cost in our tested memory benchmarks: we show this with BRIDGE, a deliberately minimal RAG memory with chained observation notes, hybrid BM25 with cosine retrieval, and chronological rendering, which reaches parity within bootstrap CI with the prior SOTA on LongMemEval-S and BEAM, and beats the best memory baseline on EverMemBench by 5.7-6.3 points at matched reader. It runs at ~5-7K reader-prompt tokens per query, using a single LLM call for each ingestion or query. We argue that this parity is itself the result: standard benchmarks score what memory retrieves, not what memory enables when the agent acts. To test the latter, we introduce five adversarial probes (Rule Application +-, Pattern Induction Style / Procedural, Dynamic Multiturn) judged at generation time by a cross vendor verification chain, where each query can only be solved by applying a remembered rule or pattern it never mentions. Across 5 probes x 20 scenarios, no architecture wins universally: atomic fact extractors (LangMem) win rule application, while BRIDGE's block-summary memory wins pattern induction. This result holds across four domains, three graders, and three reader families. Each design fails where the other wins, for reasons a recall benchmark cannot see: atomic-fact extractors discard the form a demonstrated pattern takes, while block-summary memory retrieves the rule but the reader does not act on it, a gap that presenting the rule more prominently largely closes. What separates memory designs is not what they retrieve but what they enable an agent to do, and that is what agentic memory should be evaluated on.
  </details>

- **[Reconfiguring Procedural Knowledge for Compositional Robot Skill Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22174)**  `arXiv:2611.22174`  `cs.AI`  
  _Daehee Lee, Dongsu Lee, Sanghyun Ahn, Minjong Yoo, Honguk Woo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose behavior models increasingly internalize broad procedural knowledge during training, making their adaptation to new goals and skill interfaces less a problem of learning entirely new behaviors than of reconfiguring existing competence. We study this capability as reconfigurability, the ability to realize new behaviors by cost-efficiently restructuring existing components and relations under sparse governing information, where task specifications provide high-level goals and procedure specifications expose a limited set of reusable factors, leaving the concrete bindings and compositions required for execution underdetermined. We propose RecPo, a framework that represents skills task-agnostically through multiple procedure views and materializes executable skill sequences by composing stored transitions to realize task-specific changes. RecPo maintains a procedure knowledge base of skill-induced transitions, with each view exposing a reusable factor and preserving local skill connectivity. At deployment, it interprets task specifications through the same views and resolves the resulting compositional queries over the stored relations to bind skills for execution. On long-horizon tasks in an extended Franka Kitchen benchmark, RecPo achieves 91.9% ordered completion with 1.0 ms of task inference per episode across varying procedure support and task-record sparsity. Real-robot studies further demonstrate adaptation through local memory edits without task-specific retraining.
  </details>

- **[From Sorry to QED: Refining LLM Math Proofs via Angelic Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22103)**  `arXiv:2611.22103`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yanju Chen, Hanzhi Liu, Ruizhe Qian, Jiaming Shan, Xu Yang, Yufei Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can now generate Lean proof scripts that capture the high-level structure of many mathematical arguments. However, these scripts frequently fail on complex theorems: a single missing side condition or misapplied lemma can cause the prover to abort early and discard an otherwise viable proof outline. This paper presents MathEye, a framework that converts imperfect LLM-generated proof scripts into fully verified Lean proofs by combining symbolic reasoning with minimal human interaction. At the core of MathEye is angelic proof checking, an optimistic execution model that replaces failing proof fragments with placeholders while preserving the overall structure of the argument. This optimistic check produces an angelic proof that isolates the exact proof obligations that the LLM fails to discharge. Because these unresolved obligations are often logically interdependent, asking a mathematician to address every placeholder directly is inefficient. To minimize human effort, MathEye performs residual analysis over a proof dependence graph that captures symbol-level data flow in the angelic proof. By formulating obligation selection as a MaxSAT optimization problem, MathEye selects a minimal-cost set of essential obligations whose resolution suffices to complete the entire proof. MathEye presents these essential obligations to the mathematician in ordinary mathematical language and uses the mathematician's answers to automatically reconstruct the complete Lean proof. Experiments on 30 real-world benchmarks across analysis and algebra show that MathEye solves 93.3% of the problems while reducing the number of required user interactions by 55%.
  </details>

- **[EvoDisco: Strategy Discovery for Evolving Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22102)**  `arXiv:2611.22102`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mingyang Liu, Qingcan Kang, Shixiong Kai, Kaichao Liang, Xiaojin Fu, Tao Zhong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents accumulate experience in memory, and how that memory evolves during interaction is as important as what it stores. Recent methods update memory online, yet both the operators that transform memory and the rules that select them remain fixed by design. We propose EvoDisco, which formulates agent memory evolution as evolutionary strategy discovery: the agent must discover which operator to apply, when to apply it, and how to instantiate it, rather than rewriting memory under a static operator schedule. A learnable policy conditions on the current memory state and recent task history, then selects and instantiates one of four semantic operators---Abstract, Refine, Bridge, and Reframe---which respectively compress related memories, decompose overloaded entries, connect weakly linked experiences, and reorganize retrieved reasoning chains. The policy is updated with a bi-level fitness. An inner signal scores each transformation using a retrieval-quality improvement regularized by memory complexity. An outer signal scores strategic efficacy over a window of past decisions, combining historical operator-utility regret, utility-prediction error, and selection diversity. Under a one-pass online plus frozen-transfer protocol on ALFWorld, WebShop, and LifelongAgentBench (OS and DB), EvoDisco improves over strong memory baselines including MemP and MemRL. The largest gains are on ALFWorld ($+8.8$ online / $+12.9$ frozen-transfer points over MemP; $+16.4$ / $+17.9$ over MemRL). Ablations attribute these gains to both fitness levels and the operator library.
  </details>

- **[ATLAS-Finance: Benchmarking AI Agents in Realistic Banking Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22085)**  `arXiv:2611.22085`  `cs.AI`  
  _Elaine Lau, Antoine Pangas, Alexandre Finot, Collin Schweiker, Amir Bouzidi, Hui Wen Goh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Professional work involves under-specified assignments, where relevant information is spread across workplace applications and may conflict or change over time. To evaluate how well frontier agents can perform economically valuable work in such settings, we introduce ATLAS-Finance, a benchmark of 100 end-to-end investment-banking tasks across 13 simulated firms. As in real banking workflows, agents must search email, chat, calendars, notes, and files to identify requirements, gather information, reconcile conflicts/updates, and then build complex financial models. Tasks were developed by experienced bankers based on their real deal work and take them 15-30 hours to complete. Agent deliverables are evaluated with expert-authored rubrics (median: 124.5 criteria) covering financial rationale and results, spreadsheet construction, completeness, and formatting. Despite completing substantial portions of the requested work, current agents rarely produce correct deliverables. Across today's frontier models, the best ones (Claude Opus and Fable) pass fewer than 11% of tasks.
  </details>

- **[What Does an Agent Need? Bundle-Aware Agent Resource Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22030)**  `arXiv:2611.22030`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunxiao Shi, Tingwei Chen, Dannong Xu, Ahad N. Zehmakan, Jialie Shen, Zhendong Chu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent resource's value depends on its backbone and companion resources and is known only after execution. Token-based generators remain tied to their training catalog, while independent resource scoring misses complementarities and reused bundles target other tasks. We formulate Task-Conditioned Agent Resource Composition (AgentComposition): jointly selecting a backbone model and a tool-skill bundle to maximize end-to-end execution utility. Our Retrieval-Grounded Bundle-Aware Agent Generation retrieves candidate resources and past configurations of similar tasks. A fine-tuned generator reads resource descriptions and recombines candidates through constrained beam search, allowing new resources without retraining. A bundle-aware critic ranks complete configurations using a large language model (LLM) teacher and historically successful configurations as execution proxies, and adapts to execution feedback when available. We evaluate reference recovery, offline execution with judged and native rewards, and online recommendation with real execution, building AgentSelect-Exec with 5,060 executed configurations. Our method recovers the complete reference configuration within ten candidates for 67.73% of AgentSelect queries, nearly twice the strongest baseline, and leads all baselines on SkillsBench. The proxy-trained critic raises the top-ranked agent's execution score from 7.58 to 8.37 out of 10; execution feedback increases it to 8.78, above the strongest baselines. Four target-domain queries suffice to adapt the generator to SkillsBench. Agents recommended online outperform the strongest baselines under both an LLM judge and native verifiers. These results support learning and transferring joint resource composition from task experience.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Can Rewrite Their Own Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21885)**  `arXiv:2611.21885`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Changyi Yang, Andy Mao, Xi Zhu, Chaoran Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are trained once, frozen and deployed: improving one again takes another training run, and a prompt, a scaffold or extra computation at inference is gone once the task ends. A frontier model has been shown to edit the weights of a much smaller one. We ask whether a model can rewrite its own weights to make itself better, and find that it can, by its own reasoning, with no gradients and no training; we call this self-rewriting. Given its weights, graded answers and the ability to run itself, a 27B model diagnoses its failure and edits the weights behind it. It surpasses itself: its own edit makes it 6.6 points more accurate on 490 problems it never saw, in a test fixed in advance, and another session's edit also makes it significantly better. It keeps getting better: it submits an edit only when its own tests support it, each edit replacing the last, and over its first eight such sessions its accuracy only rises. Unlike test-time scaling, the gain persists in its weights and adds no cost per answer; unlike training, it needs no pre- or post-training infrastructure, only an open model and hours on one GPU. It repairs a damaged copy of itself: it locates the corrupted matrix and rewrites it with values of its own, never reading the undamaged weights; found on text math problems alone, the repair lifts the copy on six image benchmarks it never saw from 7% to 70% accuracy, 83% of the undamaged model's. And the ability emerges with scale: across four sizes of one family, the 2B model makes no edit and the 4B and 9B models break themselves; only the 27B model works on itself as a human would, comparing its hidden states on answers that finish and on answers that run out of tokens, weakening the direction that separates them. Like a human, a model need not separate training from inference; improving a language model may come to take a conversation rather than a training run.
  </details>

- **[How Much of SAR Vehicle Recognition Is the Vehicle? Background Shortcuts, Angle Gaps and Partial Libraries on MSTAR and ATRNet-STAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21827)**  `arXiv:2611.21827`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mousa Shaya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep networks score near 99% on the MSTAR vehicle benchmark, and the newer ATRNet-STAR benchmark was built to be harder. We ask how much of these scores comes from the vehicle itself. With a modern network (ConvNeXt-Small, 3 seeds) we first reproduce the known MSTAR result: with the central 96x96 pixels zeroed out, accuracy is still 73.3% (chance 10%). The same shortcut is present in ATRNet-STAR, which was collected across several scenes: with the vehicle removed, accuracy is 63.3% on the 40-class SOC split (chance 2.5%). Under the official scene-change split, the background stops helping and starts hurting. Accuracy is 22.8% on full chips and 30.8% when only the vehicle is shown. A SAR foundation model, SARATR-X, leans on the background more than ConvNeXt does (+4 to +6 points with the vehicle removed, in every seed) and does not solve the scene change. We then hold the amount of training data fixed and vary the depression angles used for training. Accuracy is set mainly by the gap to the nearest training angle: 57% at 15 degrees, 39% at 30 degrees and 26% at 45 degrees. Adding more training angles does not help. Finally, when most classes have training images at the test angle and a few do not, the missing classes do worse than when no class has them: 9.0% instead of 22.4% at a 45-degree gap. Most of their images are assigned to known classes. We recommend reporting background-only and target-only controls, and the angle gap, next to any accuracy figure. Code is public.
  </details>

- **[NEBench R1: a Reasoning Benchmark for Evaluating Nuclear Domain Competence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21682)**  `arXiv:2611.21682`  `cs.AI`  
  _Liyan Wang, Longze Li, Mohamed Hassan, Isshu Lee, Dajie Sun, Ruimin Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored for knowledge-intensive and safety-critical applications in nuclear engineering. However, existing nuclear-domain benchmarks provide limited content coverage or focus primarily on answer accuracy without systematically evaluating the quality of the underlying reasoning. This study introduces NEBench R1, a reasoning-oriented benchmark comprising 7,546 unique multiple-choice questions extracted from U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) reactor operator licensing examinations covering 18 nuclear facilities. Each question is classified according to the NRC Knowledge and Abilities catalogs and accompanied by an official explanation serving as a reference for reasoning evaluation. Six LLMs were evaluated, with overall accuracy ranging from 34.0% to 73.9% across three distinct performance tiers. To assess reasoning quality, an LLM-based judge evaluated correctly answered responses using a multidimensional rubric. Although responses generally demonstrated strong problem comprehension, they scored lower in factual and procedural accuracy and distractor analysis, indicating that correct answers were not necessarily supported by technically sound justifications. Response clarity was also weakly indicative of technical quality. Furthermore, longer reasoning traces were associated with lower accuracy in both frontier reasoning models, suggesting that reasoning length may reflect question difficulty rather than improved problem-solving performance. Reasoning-token consumption may therefore provide an additional signal for identifying potentially unreliable responses. NEBench R1 establishes a benchmark for evaluating both answer accuracy and reasoning quality, providing a foundation for assessing the domain competence and reliability of LLMs in nuclear engineering.
  </details>

- **[Sage S2.1 Flash Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21072)**  `arXiv:2611.21072`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kun Cai, Haodong Chen, Guangran Cheng, Qingxiu Cheng, Xu Dai, Guanting Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Sage S2.1 Flash, a 320-billion-parameter general-purpose mixture-of-experts model designed to reason over scientific evidence and act in interactive environments. Sage Sci-Perception uses dedicated encoders for molecular graphs and biological sequences and represents spectra as ordered patches of measurements. Modality-specific projectors map these representations into the input space of a shared backbone, which processes them alongside text and images. To develop the model's agent capabilities, we construct verifiable environments for tool use, search, and coding, as well as specialized workflows involving machine learning experiments and scientific software. Task completion is checked against execution results, changes in environment state, or submitted artifacts. We evaluate Sage S2.1 Flash on 33 benchmarks across seven domains. In the primary comparison against five baselines, it ranks first on OfficeQA Pro (72.2), MLE-bench Lite (90.3 HumanRank), Harvey LAB, and SpreadsheetBench 2, and second on OSWorld-Verified (85.2), WebArena-Verified (67.6), and DeepResearch Bench II (54.2). These results show particular strengths in using documentary evidence, producing verifiable workbooks, and carrying out machine learning tasks, alongside competitive performance in desktop and browser workflows that require the model to act on visual feedback.
  </details>

- **[Regularizing Treatment Leakage for LLM-based Uplift Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20794)**  `arXiv:2611.20794`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bojing Feng, Jinqiu Deng, Zihan Hong, Yuan Xue, Wenqi Xiang, Shuhan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) provide semantic features for uplift estimation, but treatment-revealing text can induce prediction shortcuts that do not transfer to deployment. We propose \emph{LLM-based Uplift Leakage Regularization} (\textsc{LULR}) to address this risk. With the LLM encoder frozen, \textsc{LULR} penalizes dependence between the causal head's trainable shared representation and treatment while retaining separate outcome heads. The penalty uses a variational approximation to the contrastive log-ratio upper bound (CLUB) of mutual information, targeting treatment dependence rather than generic parameter shrinkage. Controlled proxy experiments provide exploratory evidence of improved ranking scores under injected treatment information relative to no regularization and the tested weight-decay setting. Structured-only benchmarks show modest, dataset-dependent effects. On an industrial pricing randomized controlled trial, mean offline $\text{lift@}0.3$ is $0.258$ for \textsc{LULR}, compared with $0.106$ for the same-backbone baseline. A separate one-week online A/B test of the complete deployed pricing policy records $5.45\%$ higher sales volume and $1.91\%$ higher gross merchandise value than the control arm. These short-term policy-level gains do not isolate the regularizer's effect.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming for Prediction and Classification of Target Variables in Structured Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19292)**  `arXiv:2611.19292`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space programming fixes every parameter of a feed-forward network by hand, with no gradient descent anywhere in the construction. Every demonstration so far starts from a target function handed to the model as a rule system, making it a compilation problem. A supervised dataset withholds that specification, so the procedure must do the statistical inference itself and then express the result in the parameterisation it was given. We ask whether a large language model can do both. It is shown a small dataset in full, with the architecture of a feed-forward network, and writes out every weight and bias as a literal number, with no optimizer, no gradient, no code execution, and no chance to evaluate it first. Each construction is sealed and pre-registers its expected score. The graded suite runs from two toy problems, through nine synthetic problems disguised by a hidden rotation, to the classical benchmarks and one real engineering regression. The frozen network matches the closed-form optimum wherever that optimum is linear, reaches the irreducible error of the generating process on three rungs where it is not, and beats every fitted baseline on two of those, including the same architecture trained by gradient descent. It fails on two rungs, both times because the structure to be found is oblique to the coordinates it analysed. Three further constructors separate two failure modes a single score conflates: one returns no parameter vector at all on the hardest rungs, another delivers everywhere while writing networks worse than predicting the mean. One of those failures proves avoidable: another constructor solves the oblique-hinge rung by a margin that survives multiple-comparison correction. What predicts whether the hidden layer earns its keep is the training error of a linear separator, which the composite complexity index misses.
  </details>

- **[RIPPLE: Recovery Internalization through Process-Level Policy Learning and Effect-Aware Credit for Long-Horizon Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31276)**  `arXiv:2610.31276`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingxuan Xu, Zihang Xu, Yuechen Luo, Ziqi Miao, Wenqiang Zhu, Weihao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon coding agents can observe a failure and generate a plausible critique yet continue along the same incorrect trajectory. We call this the Critique-to-Action Handoff Gap. We introduce RIPPLE, a recovery-oriented training framework that internalizes process-level guidance into a single self-recovering policy. A prefix-only teacher provides evidence-grounded recovery supervision without access to future actions or terminal outcomes. After lightweight distillation, reinforcement learning assigns stage-specific credit to reflection triggering, diagnosis, and corrective action. Local validity signals gate trajectory advantages so that intermediate recovery decisions do not indiscriminately inherit terminal outcomes. Across four repository-level software-engineering benchmarks, RIPPLE improves the base policy by an arithmetic mean of 4.8 score points, with no auxiliary critic at deployment. Controlled ablations isolate the benefits of local, stage-aligned credit, and matched-prefix interventions show that critique semantics affect subsequent correction. Recovery gains concentrate where the prefix contains sufficient diagnostic evidence and the repository state remains reversible within the remaining budget.
  </details>

- **[ABACUS: Program-Guided Multistep Reasoning over Frozen Judgment Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25963)**  `arXiv:2610.25963`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhao Wang, Zelin Zang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Judgment models answer semantic questions by scoring a finite set of candidate outcomes. Can reusable programs turn these local decisions into powerful multistep reasoning? We introduce \method, a framework that jointly constructs judgment schemas and composition programs for frozen judgment models. A generative teacher designs the procedure offline; the judgment model resolves local semantic questions online, and the saved program composes its decisions into an answer. We study three allocations of design effort between humans and the teacher, focusing on construction through a shared interface. We instantiate the framework with Kev. On three selected BIG-Bench Hard tasks, nine independently constructed programs solve all 450 executions of 150 development inputs. A separate deployment study freezes three programs after development-set reference-agreement refinement and evaluates them on 600 held-out questions. These programs achieve near-perfect 99.67\% macro accuracy, versus 64.00\% for direct Kev and 68.80\% for final-answer-only Qwen3.5-4B. They attain comparable observed accuracy to Qwen's unrestricted response policy (99.07\%) with an \textbf{8.43-fold online speedup}: 0.255 versus 2.150 seconds per question. These results show how reusable programs can substantially expand the reasoning capability of a compact judgment model while keeping its parameters fixed.
  </details>

- **[Realtime-Bench: Can Frontier Models Solve Real-time Interactive Problems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22155)**  `arXiv:2610.22155`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyu Tang, Xu Yang, Zhehao Zhang, Zheng Wang, Qi Cheng, Haozhe Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, yet existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. We introduce Realtime-Bench, a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success. Under Wall Clock, where environments advance according to actual inference latency, the best success rate reaches only 44% and the best Pass^3 is only 36%, demonstrating that real-time interaction remains challenging. To separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability, we compare Wall Clock with Paused Clock, which removes inference delays as an idealized reference, and introduce Token Clock, which advances simulated time according to generated token counts, testing temporal awareness independently of physical serving speed. We find that temporal costs limit success in evolving environments and that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution. Based on these results, we discuss future directions for improving real-time multimodal models, including adaptive reasoning with temporal awareness and continuous perception and action.
  </details>

- **[Code4Scene: Benchmarking Coding Agents for Constructing and Editing 3D Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36777)**  `arXiv:2609.36777`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaokang Ye, Siddhant Hitesh Mantri, Zimeng Chen, Edward Zhang, Zhaoxu Zheng, Yuanheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier coding agents can now write and execute code that authors 3D environments, but whether they reliably understand 3D structure and precisely control scene state remains unclear. The generated 3D scene is a persistent, executable artifact: a convincing render can hide incorrect spatial relations, intersecting objects, or unintended modifications. We introduce Code4Scene, a benchmark of 170 Unreal Engine cases built from human-assembled scenes that evaluates coding agents on two complementary settings under a shared execution interface. Construction tests scene-level spatial reasoning from open-ended language specifications, where many realizations are valid; editing tests precise control of scene state, where the agent must recover the target scene from reference images while preserving everything else. Rather than scoring code or rendered views, Code4Scene evaluates the generated engine-native scene for task fulfillment, artifact integrity, and static physical validity, with edits additionally compared against withheld ground truth. Across 14 coding-agent systems on the 83-case public set, construction and editing performance are strongly correlated but not interchangeable (Spearman $\rho = 0.67$): Claude Fable 5.1 leads construction, Gemini 3.8 Flash leads editing. Spatial Composition is the weakest construction category for every agent, while editing remains imprecise: the best Repair F1 is only 0.562, and 34.2% of edits that fully recover the target still introduce unintended changes elsewhere in the scene. These results expose a gap between plausible 3D generation and reliable spatial reasoning and state control. The project webpage is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PhysAlign: A Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Role Alignment in Multimodal Physics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33319)**  `arXiv:2609.33319`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kecheng Liang, Haoyang Liu, Zexin Chen, Zirong Liu, Weixing Chen, Qiufeng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A key challenge in physics diagram understanding is correctly associating visual information with the physical entities, relations, and conditions it describes. Even when a value, symbol, or other local element is accurately recognized, assigning it to the wrong entity or scope can distort the underlying physical premise and lead to incorrect reasoning. To systematically study this challenge, we introduce \textbf{PhysAlign}, a benchmark designed to assess whether multimodal models correctly associate information recognized from physics diagrams with its intended physical role. By disentangling visual recognition from physical-role assignment through localized probes and controlled variants, PhysAlign isolates correspondence errors from recognition failures. It contains 3,341 human-validated probes spanning 986 physics problems, enabling systematic evaluation of visual recognition and physical-role correspondence at scale. We further introduce five complementary evaluation metrics, including CAcc, GAcc, and JAcc, which provide a comprehensive assessment of models' ability to recognize diagram content, establish correct physical correspondences, and solve the underlying physics problem. Across our evaluated multimodal models, PhysAlign reveals a consistent gap between local visual recognition and physical-role grounding. Even when the queried content is correctly recognized, the conditional correspondence error rate remains 13.8\% for GPT-6-Astra and rises to about 50.6\% for InternVL3.5-8B. These findings indicate that strong perception alone does not ensure reliable physical interpretation, exposing a distinct grounding bottleneck that is largely hidden by answer-level accuracy and highlighting the need for future models to better align recognized visual evidence with its physical meaning.
  </details>

- **[Process Advantage Signal Shaping: Advantage Construction for Process-Supervised Reinforcement Learning in LLM Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29296)**  `arXiv:2606.29296`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Wang, Hongtao Tian, Tao Yang, Yunsheng Shi, Ting Yao, Wenbo Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense process supervision provides local evaluations, but leaves open how these scores should become policy advantages. We introduce PASS (Process Advantage Signal Shaping), an advantage-construction method for group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). PASS separately normalizes and fuses reward channels, constructs batch-shared credit units from variation in the fused score trace, and normalizes return-to-go by the remaining unit count. These operations connect feedback scale, credit resolution, and accumulation horizon in a single computation. We evaluate learned process reward model (PRM) feedback for mathematical reasoning and teacher-derived token feedback for multi-hop question answering (QA). Across seven mathematics benchmarks, PASS improves average pass@1 by 4.62 percentage points over outcome-only GRPO with approximately $2\%$ shorter responses. Matched comparisons with Step-GRPO and component ablations further support the choice of shared credit units and residual normalization. On multi-hop QA, PASS improves over process-signal controls across two normalizers; token-level ablations reveal an accuracy--length trade-off: token-level accumulation slightly favors pass@1, while PASS achieves higher pass@8/16 with shorter responses. The results establish the conversion from local process scores to policy advantages as a consequential design choice across distinct supervision sources.
  </details>

- **[Seeing with You: Perception-Reasoning Coevolution for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28618)**  `arXiv:2603.28618`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqi Miao, Haonan Jia, Lijun Li, Chen Qian, Yuan Xiong, Wenting Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially enhanced the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing RLVR approaches typically rely on outcome-driven optimization that updates both perception and reasoning using a shared reward based solely on the final answer. This shared reward blurs credit assignment, frequently improving reasoning patterns while failing to reliably enhance the accuracy of upstream visual evidence extraction. To address this perception bottleneck, we introduce PRCO (Perception-Reasoning Coevolution), a dual-role RLVR framework with a shared policy. PRCO consists of two cooperative roles: an Observer that generates an evidence caption tailored to the question and a Solver that predicts the final answer based on this caption. Crucially, PRCO employs role-specific reward signals: the Solver is optimized using verifiable outcome rewards on the final answer, while the Observer receives a utility reward derived from the Solver's downstream success. Extensive experiments across eight challenging multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that PRCO yields consistent improvements across model scales by over 7 points on average accuracy compared to the base model, outperforming prior open-source RL-tuned baselines.
  </details>

- **[Do Multi-Agents Dream of Electric Screens? Achieving 100% on AndroidWorld Through Task Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07787)**  `arXiv:2602.07787`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pierre-Louis Favreau, Jean-Pierre Lo, Clement Guiguet, Charles Simon-Meunier, Nicolas Dehandschoewercker, Allen G. Roush, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Minitap's mobile-use, a multi-agent system for Android UI control, and evaluate it on the 116 tasks of AndroidWorld, on Android emulators in Minitap's cloud device infrastructure, real Pixel 7 phones from our device farm and the stock emulator. On fresh, held-out task instances (three pre-registered seeds never used during development), the final system reaches a pass@3 of 100.0% (95% CI 96.8-100.0); pass^3 is 94.0% (95% CI 89.7-97.4) and the mean success rate 98.0% (95% CI 96.0-99.7). Minitap decomposes each task across five LLM agents (planning, subgoal tracking, state capture, decision, execution) and deterministic nodes, with mechanisms that read typed text back from the device, verify the goal on device evidence and detect repeated failures. A study of the initial system measures what each part contributes: removing only the decomposition changes success by -26.1 [-35.6, -16.7] points, the mechanisms together add +11.7 [+2.2, +21.1] points over a single agent, and the decision agent is the one role that needs a frontier model. We then improve reliability with general changes, a few of them triggered by the goal's wording. On MobileMiniWoB++, a second benchmark never used during development, the final system improves on the initial one by 7.1 points (McNemar p = 0.035; 95% CI -0.5 to +14.7). Minitap is open source:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AutoPBO: LLM-powered Optimization for Local Search PBO Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04007)**  `arXiv:2509.04007`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yi Chu, Jinyuan Li, Yiwen Sun, Mengchuan Zou, Shaowei Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pseudo-Boolean Optimization (PBO) provides a powerful framework for modeling combinatorial problems through pseudo-Boolean (PB) constraints. Local search solvers have shown excellent performance in PBO solving, and their efficiency is highly dependent on their internal heuristics to guide the search. Still, their design often requires significant expert effort and manual tuning in practice. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated potential in automating algorithm design, their application to optimizing PBO solvers remains unexplored. In this work, we introduce AutoPBO, a novel LLM-powered framework to automatically enhance PBO local search solvers. We conduct experiments on a broad range of four public benchmarks, including one real-world benchmark, a benchmark from PB competition, an integer linear programming optimization benchmark, and a crafted combinatorial benchmark, to evaluate the performance improvement achieved by AutoPBO and compare it with six state-of-the-art competitors, including two local search PBO solvers NuPBO and OraSLS, two complete PB solvers PBO-IHS and RoundingSat, and two mixed integer programming (MIP) solvers Gurobi and SCIP. AutoPBO demonstrates significant improvements over previous local search approaches, while maintaining competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art competitors. The results suggest that AutoPBO offers a promising approach to automating local search solver design.
  </details>

- **[NS-Gym: A Comprehensive and Open-Source Simulation Framework for Non-Stationary Markov Decision Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.09646)**  `arXiv:2501.09646`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nathaniel S. Keplinger, Baiting Luo, Yunuo Zhang, Kyle Hollins Wray, Aron Laszka, Abhishek Dubey, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many real-world applications require decision-making where the environmental dynamics evolve over time. These non-stationary environments pose significant challenges to traditional decision-making models, which typically assume stationary dynamics. Non-stationary Markov decision processes (NS-MDPs) offer a framework to model and solve decision problems under such changing conditions. However, there are no standardized simulation frameworks for NS-MDPs, as opposed to widely popular frameworks for stationary problems. We present NS-Gym, the first simulation toolkit designed explicitly for NS-MDPs, integrated within the popular Gymnasium framework. In NS-Gym, we segregate the evolution of the environmental parameters that characterize non-stationarity from the agent's decision-making module, allowing for modular and flexible adaptations to dynamic environments. We review prior work in this domain and present a toolkit encapsulating key problem characteristics and types in NS-MDPs. This toolkit is the first effort to develop a set of standardized interfaces and benchmark problems to enable consistent and reproducible evaluation of algorithms under non-stationary conditions. We also benchmark several algorithmic approaches from prior work on NS-MDPs using NS-Gym. We envision that NS-Gym will enable researchers to study decision-making under non-stationarity by providing standardized interfaces, simulation frameworks, and benchmark problems.
